# Elias van den Broeck: Study Notes

**Site:** Elias van den Broeck (born 1649/50 in Antwerp, or c. 1652 in Amsterdam -- buried 6 February 1708, Amsterdam), Flemish/Dutch still-life painter; de Heem's pupil who brought the banquet still life back to Amsterdam in 1685, painted the bouquet in three dead-coloured passes with orpiment yellows, kept a flower court beside his house on the Molenpad, pasted real butterfly wings onto his forest floors, and was -- if Weyerman is to be believed -- driven out of Antwerp for it.

**Doorway:** Hop from the Nicolaes van Verendael study: from the Antwerp collaborator who loosened Seghers's garland into groups around empty cartouches and reworked a de Heem bouquet twenty years after it was painted, to de Heem's own pupil, who learned the banquet table in Utrecht, became a Guild master in Antwerp, and carried the manner back to Amsterdam in 1685.

One-hundred-and-eighty-third doorway check in a row. The legs agree on every verdict, and the agreement is recorded rather than smoothed over. Leg 1 records it as a NON-HOLD on the substance and a NON-HOLD on the letter -- the fifty-seventh non-hold; the substance-hold count stays at 133. Leg 3 records it the same way: NON-HOLD on the substance, NON-HOLD on the letter. The parent follows both legs on every claim: the substance fails on D5 alone, the letter fails on D1, D3 and D5. The check is recorded as a NON-HOLD on the substance and a NON-HOLD on the letter -- the fifty-seventh non-hold; the substance-hold count stays at 133 -- and the streak that began at #420 now stands at one hundred and thirty-three holds and fifty-seven non-holds. (The tally arithmetic is flagged, not fixed: 133 + 57 = 190 against 183 checks run -- see C825.)

D1 "Elias van den Broeck (c. 1649/1652 - buried 6 February 1708)" -- HOLDS in substance, FAILS in the letter (both legs agree). The birth-year dispute is real: 1649/50 in Antwerp (SMK paper title; Sotheby's "Antwerp 1649/50"; NL Wikipedia body "1649 of 1650"; Houbraken "born in Antwerp") against c. 1652, probably in Amsterdam (EN Wikipedia). The burial on 6 February 1708 is the one firm point, solid across EN Wikipedia, NL Wikipedia and the SMK paper's 1708 death year. But no source writes "c. 1649/1652": the sources write "1649/50" or "c. 1652", and the composite reads as a range 1649-1652 that no source supports, while dropping the birthplace half of the dispute (leg 1 C792, C799; leg 3 C809).

D2 "de Heem's documented pupil (Utrecht 1669, Antwerp 1673)" -- HOLDS in substance and in letter (both legs agree). Pupil of de Heem in Utrecht in 1669; accompanied de Heem to Antwerp and became a Guild master there in 1673. Both dates are documented (EN Wikipedia, citing RKD; de Heem's apprentice list names Broeck). Note: Sphinx and askART give 1672 for the move to Antwerp against the Broeck article's 1673, and de Heem left Utrecht in 1671 when French troops approached, so the accompaniment year is 1672 or 1673; the 1673 mastership is firm. And 1673 is not a second pupillage date -- the study must not say he became de Heem's pupil in Antwerp in 1673 (leg 1 C806; leg 3 C810).

D3 "the pronk still life carried back to Amsterdam in 1685" -- HOLDS in substance, FAILS in the letter (both legs agree). He returned to Amsterdam in 1685 (EN Wikipedia; Sphinx) and painted banquet still lifes in de Heem's manner (Sotheby's orange still life; Christie's oyster still life, c. 1669-1670, from the de Heem workshop years). The geographic move and the genre are both real. But "carried back" implies he brought the pronk still life to Amsterdam, and Willem Kalf was already the Amsterdam pronk master, painting pronk there from the 1650s, three decades before the return; no source read calls Broeck a "pronk" painter in so many words -- EN Wikipedia says "banquet still lifes". The accurate phrasing is that he brought de Heem's banquet manner back to Amsterdam (leg 1 C793, F24; leg 3 C811).

D4 "a technical mechanism (SMK study: dead-colouring and orpiment)" -- HOLDS in substance and in letter (both legs agree, verified from the poster read end to end by both legs). The Monaghan and Vila poster (ICOM-CC Melbourne 2014) reports evidence of dead-colouring by optical microscopy and infrared imaging, describes the three-step method, and lists realgar/orpiment in the yellow-flower palette, with the yellow fox rose confirmed in Broeck's works. "SMK study: dead-colouring and orpiment" states exactly what the poster reports, no more. Caveat: the poster's extracted text garbles the flower name as "yellow fox roses" -- the study quotes the palette table ("Yellow flowers: Realgar/orpiment"), not the garbled sentence (leg 1 F14, F15; leg 3 C817).

D5 "After Verendael closes the Antwerp arc, Broeck opens Amsterdam with Kick, Kouwenbergh and Walscapelle in tow" -- FAILS in substance and in letter (both legs agree). The Amsterdam opening is real (Broeck returns 1685), but the trio cannot be "in tow": Cornelis Kick died in Amsterdam on 18 June 1681, four years before the return, and was Amsterdam-born and Amsterdam-based -- Broeck returns to Kick's city, not with Kick; Jacob van Walscapelle (1644-1727) was Kick's Amsterdam pupil and Broeck's fellow pupil, and by Houbraken's account had quit painting in 1667 and taken up another profession; Philip van Kouwenbergh (1671-1729) was Broeck's own pupil, taught in Amsterdam after the return. Not one of the three travelled from Antwerp to Amsterdam with Broeck in 1685. "In tow" asserts a physical following that the dates forbid, and "opens Amsterdam" is looser than the evidence -- Amsterdam was already open, with Kalf, Kick and Walscapelle there. The true Amsterdam line is Kick to Broeck to Kouwenbergh, with Walscapelle as Kick's other pupil (leg 1 C794; leg 3 C808).

D6 "VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero '## Study #' headers; 8 whole-log mentions of the full name in 5 lines (was 4 in 3 at #560 pick time; the #560 entry added 4); no local file; muse slug study/elias-van-den-broeck.md 404" -- HOLDS in substance and in letter (both legs agree). Zero headers: a strict grep for lines beginning with "## Study #" and containing Broeck returns 0 over all 152 headers in the log (the loose-grep count of 5 is the C762 false-positive pattern, quoted "## Study #" text inside VERIFIED NOT STUDIED paragraphs). The count reconciles exactly as a snapshot at check time: before the #560 entry, occurrences were #557 (1) + #558 (1) + #559 (2) = 4 in 3 lines; the #560 entry added 4 in 2 lines = 8 in 5. The #561 entry itself then added 3 occurrences in 2 lines, so the live log now reads 11 occurrences in 7 lines -- the drift is the entry's own writing after the check, not a miscount. No local file; the slug returned 404 on the authenticated GitHub API (verified by the parent this run) and on raw.githubusercontent.com (re-checked by the parent, by leg 1 and by leg 3 this run) (leg 1 C802; leg 3 D6).

D7 "FLAG: birth 1649 vs c. 1652; Houbraken 'died 1711' against the 1708 burial" -- HOLDS in substance and in letter (both legs agree). Both disputes verified from sources read end to end: the birth poles (D1); Houbraken, vol. 3, p. 379, "died in the year 1711", read verbatim by leg 1, against the burial record of 6 February 1708. Note: the precise first pole is "1649/50 (Antwerp)"; "1649" alone compresses it by a year, but the flag names the two poles correctly and the burial opposition is exact (leg 1 F1, F8; leg 3 C812).

## The life

Elias van den Broeck was born in 1649/50 in Antwerp, or c. 1652 in Amsterdam -- the sources split, and the study does not pick a side. EN Wikipedia gives "c. 1652 in probably in Amsterdam"; NL Wikipedia's body gives "Antwerpen, 1649 of 1650", while its own infobox says "Geboren Amsterdam, 1652"; the SMK technical paper titles him "(1649/50-1708)"; Sotheby's gives "Antwerp 1649/50 - 1708 Amsterdam"; Christie's gives both "ANVERS 1649 - AMSTERDAM 1708" and "1650-1708". Houbraken says he was "born in Antwerp". No source writes "c. 1649/1652" (C792). The one firm point is the end: buried in Amsterdam on 6 February 1708 (leg 1 F1; leg 3 D1).

He was the son of Jan van den Broeck (Paludanus) (1618-1678) and Susanna van Diependaal (1634-1655), married the year before his birth; his mother died when he was seven, his father placed him in an orphanage with a substantial monetary settlement, and he was first apprenticed with a goldsmith (EN Wikipedia; Sphinx also gives the goldsmith apprenticeship in 1665). No independent corroboration beyond Segal and Alen (cited) was reached this leg (leg 1 F2).

In 1665 he became a pupil of Cornelis Kick in Amsterdam -- 27 August 1665, for four years, until 1669 (Boijmans 1907 catalogue, via leg 3 N1; Sphinx: "in the same year he became a pupil of the still-life painter, Cornelis Kick, with whom he remained until 1669"). Kick (bapt. 12 March 1634 - died 18 June 1681, Amsterdam) had Broeck and Jacob van Walscapelle as pupils, and painted flowers from nature in his father-in-law's garden outside the city walls, with Walscapelle beside him. Kick himself was trained from c. 1650 under his father Simon Kick, and worked in the manner of de Heem (leg 1 F3; leg 3 N1).

In 1669 he became a pupil of Jan Davidsz. de Heem in Utrecht. Houbraken: "it was to be seen from his handling that he had also studied with Jan Davidsz. de Heem." De Heem's apprentice list in the EN Wikipedia de Heem article names Broeck among ten apprentices (leg 1 F4; leg 3 N2).

He is registered in a document stating his intention to go to Italy in 1672, but it is likely he never made the trip. No Italian period exists in any source read; his "Italy" is Antwerp (leg 1 F5).

In 1673 he accompanied de Heem to Antwerp, where he became a master in the Guild of St. Luke there (EN Wikipedia; Sphinx says "member of the guild there"). The accompaniment year is 1672 or 1673 (C810). On 10 October 1674 he signed a deed committing himself to work a year for the art dealer Bartholomeus Floquet. He married Marie Leenaerts in Antwerp on 5 October 1677; children Jan (bapt. 13 August 1678), Francisca Maria (bapt. 22 Dec 1679), Elias (bapt. 5 July 1685) (leg 1 F6; leg 3 N10).

In 1685 he returned to Amsterdam, where he lived outside the Utrechtse Poort on the Amstel beyond the Bergenvaarderskamer. Houbraken: "He lived outside the Utrecht gate of Amsterdam on the Molenpad, where he had a flower court next to his house for his use." The Molenpad lay outside the Utrechtse Poort; the two addresses agree. From 1689 onwards children were baptised in the Catholic church at the Kuiperspad. He worked in Amsterdam until his death (leg 1 F7; leg 3 C812).

Houbraken says he "died in the year 1711" (vol. 3, p. 379, read verbatim by leg 1). Against this stands the burial record of 6 February 1708. Houbraken is three years out (leg 1 F8). Houbraken also makes him, taken literally, a pupil of Ernst Stuven -- "ELIAS van den BROECK, born in Antwerp, was one of his first and best students", the "his" being Stuven, whose biography immediately precedes. That conflicts with the documented Kick (1665) and de Heem (1669) pupillages and with chronology: Stuven was born c. 1657 and arrived in Amsterdam in 1675 aged 18. Broeck cannot have been Stuven's pupil in any meaningful sense. Houbraken's "born in Antwerp" is usable; the pupillage is not, and must not be repeated as fact (leg 1 F9, C795; leg 3 C822).

His pupil was Philip van Kouwenbergh (1671-1729), who became a respected flower painter. Kouwenbergh was taught in Amsterdam after the 1685 return, not brought from Antwerp; the NGA hedges the pupillage as "may have been", against RKD's flat statement (leg 1 F10; leg 3 N4, C814).

He is mainly known for his flower pieces, and also painted fruit pieces, forest floor pieces, mushroom pieces and banquet still lifes. His early works show the influence of his first master Cornelis Kick; his banquet still lifes were influenced by his second master Jan Davidsz. de Heem. Only one dated work is known: a 1676 picture sold by Valls Limited, London, in 1998 (Christie's oyster-still-life essay). The oyster still life itself is dated c. 1669-1670, "alors qu'il travaillait encore dans l'atelier de Jan Davidsz. de Heem". Chronology of the oeuvre therefore rests on one dated picture (1676) plus the 1673 and 1685 geographic anchors (leg 1 F11, F12, C800).

What the sources give for technique is unusually concrete, because of the SMK study. Monaghan (2012-13 Samuel H. Kress Fellow, SMK) and Vila (CATS-SMK) examined five still-life paintings by Broeck for the exhibition "Flowers and World Views" (SMK, 22 March - 20 October 2013), presented as a poster at ICOM-CC Melbourne 2014. The five: Blomsterbillede KMSst60, 64.5 x 57 cm; Blomsterbillede KMSst61, 64.5 x 57 cm; Blomster i en skal KMS1878, 62.8 x 51 cm; En skal med Blomster KMSst241, 62.6 x 53.6 cm; En skal med Blomster KMSst217, 63 x 53 cm. The poster was read end to end by legs 1 and 3 (leg 1 F13). Visual examination with optical microscopy and infrared imaging revealed evidence of dead-colouring, "a technique commonly used in De Heem's circle": first each figure is blocked out in its own basic colour, then shadows are added in a darker colour or black, finally volume is given by painting the lighter areas and highlights in a mixture of white and basic colour. The ground is two layers of brown or grey paint. The palette: ground lead and iron oxide; white lead white; red flowers vermillion; blue indigo and a copper-containing pigment; green a mixture of blue (indigo and/or azurite) and yellow; yellow flowers realgar/orpiment (arsenic sulphide), sometimes mixed with Naples yellow, lead-tin yellow, goethite or yellow lake. Wallert and Dik, who studied de Heem, believe the use of orpiment, specifically when painting yellow fox roses, can be considered a signature flower for paintings from the workshop of De Heem, and analysis confirms that this flower is also found in works by van den Broeck (leg 1 F14, F15; leg 3 D4).

The same paper records the workshop's reuse system: the repetition of nearly identical flowers and insects in Broeck's work "definitely suggests that he was using an approach very similar to" de Lairesse's manual -- "Paint all kinds of flowers on cardboard or thick paper... Make five of six of each... Then cut them all out, and keep the flowers, of each colour separately, in small boxes... Then paint a green festoon, or bouquet made up with foliage on a panel or cardboard. On this you can place these flowers, arranging and re-arranging them according to your concept." Blue morning glory and roses were featured in all five of SMK's works. And Segal's compositional rule for de Heem's circle holds here too: bent, twisted or broken tree branches, and the fixed placement of flower species -- peonies and roses in the bottom or middle, opium poppies and sunflowers at the top (leg 1 F16, F17).

The SMK works are also rare physical survivals: all five have maintained their original format, including tacking edges 1-1.5 cm wide with original tack holes 4-6 cm apart, and two retain their original mounting on slim wooden strainers with wooden tacks, "forming some of the few physical examples of this format in existence today". The vertical and horizontal canvas weave densities of two works are similar enough that they were painted on the same roll of canvas, and two other works (KMS1878 and KMSst217) also had similar weave maps (leg 1 F18).

His butterflies were, in common with Otto Marseus van Schrieck, Jeremias Falch and Philip van Kouwenbergh, made by attaching the actual wings of a butterfly on a white painted ground, so the beauty of the wings could be captured with greater realism and greater permanence. "Ironically, of course, in almost all such cases, the wings have long disappeared, and are frequently painted in by a later hand" (Christie's lot essay, citing F. Meyer, 'Philip van Kouwenbergh', Oud Holland, LII, 1980, p. 320). Weyerman's telling, via Sphinx, turns the same practice into an expulsion: Broeck "had been forced to leave Antwerp because of the indignation he had caused by pasting real butterflies on to his canvasses rather than painting them". The two tellings must not be merged: Christie's treats the practice as standard contemporary technique; Weyerman turns it into an expulsion. No source read documents an expulsion (leg 1 F19, C801; leg 2 correction 11).

Despite the influences, Broeck created a distinctive personal style. "This personal touch is visible in his flower pieces in which he evoked an evening atmosphere with strong lighting effects" (EN Wikipedia, summarising Segal and Alen 2020, pp. 447-450). "Like de Heem, he accentuated the sharpness of the flowers by adding a very fine outline in white or grey. The influence of Willem van Aelst is apparent in the diagonal composition of his flower pieces" (leg 1 F20).

Misattribution is structural, not accidental. Sphinx: "Due to the fact that his style is an amalgam of those of several better known artists, his paintings have often been misattributed." The SMK paper: "De Heem's influence can be observed in the work of countless still-life painters working in the 17th-century, sometimes leading to miss-attributions." The Fitzwilliam forest-floor piece (PD.39-1975) is "Attributed to" Broeck, was once attributed to Ruysch, and the museum note adds "this could be by the Irish painter Gustavus Hamilton (1739-1775)" (leg 1 F21, C807).

Collections named in the sources read: Fitzwilliam Museum, Cambridge; Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam; Ashmolean Museum, Oxford; Courtauld Institute, London (Sphinx); Statens Museum for Kunst, Copenhagen, which holds five (SMK paper). The Ashmolean holds "Bloemen in een vaas op een stenen tafelblad", c. 1690. No Mauritshuis holding was verified this leg (leg 1 F22).

The spelling varies in the sources -- van den Broeck, v. D. Broeck, VD Broeck in signatures -- and the study uses one form consistently: Elias van den Broeck, the Wikipedia lead form, which is also the muse slug. The Commons filenames' "(1657-1708)" is impossible: 1657 would make him eight years old when he became Kick's pupil in 1665. Do not use 1657 (leg 2 correction 6).

## M1. Dead-colouring in three passes

What it IS: the ground is two layers of brown or grey paint; each figure is blocked in its own flat basic colour (dead-colouring); shadows are laid in a darker colour or black; volume is finished with white mixed into the basic colour for light areas and highlights. Shows up: in all five SMK works, confirmed by microscopy, infrared imaging and cross-sections (KMSst60, KMSst61, KMS1878, KMSst241, KMSst217); the paper's cross-section diagram of the pink rose sample from KMSst217 shows the stack -- varnish, paint layers, highlights, shadows, dead-colouring, two ground layers, canvas (leg 1 F14).

**Takeaway:** the picture is built in three passes, and the first pass -- the flat block-in -- is the one the instruments can still see.

## M2. Orpiment yellow, the workshop signature

What it IS: yellow flowers painted with realgar/orpiment (arsenic sulphide), sometimes mixed with Naples yellow, lead-tin yellow, goethite or yellow lake. Shows up: the yellow fox rose, which Wallert and Dik treat as a signature flower of the de Heem workshop, is confirmed present in Broeck's works; yellow flowers in all five SMK pictures use the arsenic-sulphide system. Watchable behaviour: yellows that stay luminous and almost mineral against the dark ground -- and, as the 2022 Science Advances work on Mignon's yellow rose shows for the same pigment family, an orpiment yellow that degrades over centuries, so the light-shadow modelling a modern viewer sees is not quite the modelling Broeck laid down (that study's case is Mignon, not Broeck) (leg 1 F15).

**Takeaway:** the brightest yellow in the picture is a poison, and it is the workshop's fingerprint.

## M3. Fixed species zoning

What it IS: Segal's compositional rule for de Heem's circle -- peonies and roses low or middle, opium poppies and sunflowers at the top, with bent, twisted or broken branches added. Shows up: all five SMK works follow it; blue morning glory and roses recur in every one. The bouquet is a vertical hierarchy, not a random bunch: heavy blooms anchor the bottom, airy poppies and sunflowers crown the top (leg 1 F17).

**Takeaway:** the bouquet has a seating plan -- heavy flowers below, light flowers above, and the plan never changes.

## M4. The white/grey hairline

What it IS: a very fine outline in white or grey around each flower, borrowed from de Heem, that lifts the petal off the dark ground. Shows up: across the flower pieces; it is the detail that makes a rose read as cut-out sharp rather than melting into shadow. Mechanically it is the last, thinnest pass -- the opposite of blending (leg 1 F20).

**Takeaway:** the flower is drawn, not blended, at its edge -- one thin light line, and the petal stands clear of the night.

## M5. Evening atmosphere, one hard light

What it IS: Broeck's personal turn inside the inherited system -- the bouquet staged as if at dusk, a single strong light raking across the flowers against a dark ground. Shows up: the flower pieces generally; it is what Segal and Alen name as his distinctive touch. Watchable behaviour: the picture has a time of day; petals on the lit side flare, the rest sinks (leg 1 F20; leg 2 W01, W03).

**Takeaway:** the bouquet is lit at evening, not at noon -- one hard light, and everything else is dusk.

## M6. The van Aelst diagonal

What it IS: the bouquet composed on a diagonal thrust rather than a centred pyramid, an influence of Willem van Aelst. Shows up: the flower pieces' stems and sprays run corner to corner, so the eye enters low on one side and exits high on the other, instead of circling a symmetric vase (leg 1 F20; leg 2 W04, W08).

**Takeaway:** the bouquet leans -- the composition is a diagonal, and the eye travels it like a road.

## M7. Cut-out flowers, rearranged

What it IS: the de Lairesse workshop method -- paint individual flowers on cardboard or thick paper, cut them out, keep them sorted by colour in small boxes, paint a green festoon or bouquet on the panel, then place and re-place the cut-outs to taste. Shows up: nearly identical flowers and insects repeat across Broeck's works; the same blue morning glory and the same roses appear in all five SMK pictures. The bouquet is an arrangement of reusable parts, which is why the same rose can bloom in two pictures at once -- and why species that never flower together can share one vase (leg 1 F16; leg 2 Evolution).

**Takeaway:** the bouquet is assembled from parts, not grown -- the same rose, cut out and used again.

## M8. Real butterfly wings on white ground

What it IS: instead of painting a butterfly, glue the insect's actual wings onto a white-painted ground, for realism and permanence. Shows up: the Christie's forest-floor pair and the Fitzwilliam thistle/boletus piece, where butterflies sit among snails, lizard and bee; the Sotheby's forest-floor still life with snake, snail, grasshopper, worm, lizard, bird's nest and toadstools belongs to the same practice. Watchable behaviour: the wings are the one thing in the picture that was once alive twice over -- and in almost all surviving cases they have vanished and been painted in later by another hand, so the butterfly is a repaint sitting where a real wing was (leg 1 F19; leg 2 W09, W14).

**Takeaway:** the butterfly is not painted at all -- it is pasted, and what you see now is usually the repaint of a vanished wing.

## M9. The forest floor as a horizontal world

What it IS: the still life laid flat on the ground -- thistle, boletus, snail, lizard, butterflies, bee, bird's nest, toadstools, snake, grasshopper, worm -- with a landscape opening beyond, instead of a table with a vase. Shows up: Christie's pair ("A forest floor still life with butterflies and snails, a landscape beyond"); Sotheby's forest-floor still life with snake, snail, grasshopper, worm, lizard, bird's nest and toadstools; the Fitzwilliam thistle/boletus piece; W09 (snake about to strike a grasshopper), W18, W19, B11 (two snakes fighting). Watchable behaviour: the eye travels at ground level among creatures, not down onto a table; the landscape beyond gives the floor a horizon (leg 1 F11, F23; leg 2 W09, W18, W19, B11).

**Takeaway:** the table is gone -- the picture is a patch of ground at night, and something on it is about to kill something else.

## M10. The banquet table: peeled citrus as light-catcher

What it IS: the de Heem banquet piece -- a partly-peeled orange or lemon on pewter, raspberries, cherries, grapes, bread, pipe, water jug on a partly-draped table; oysters, butterflies, fruit and a gilded hanap; herring, crab, bread and grapes. Shows up: Sotheby's orange still life (39.8 x 32 cm); Christie's oyster still life, c. 1669-1670, from the de Heem workshop years; B04 (the true pronk piece, landscape format, the gold cup as the light source); B05 (a peeled lemon with its peel spiralling down to the ledge). The peeled citrus is the mechanism: the spiral of peel catches the light and gives the table its brightest note, the way the white hairline does for flowers (leg 1 F23, M10; leg 2 B04, B05, B13).

**Takeaway:** the peel is the lamp of the table -- a spiral of citrus that holds the light for the whole picture.

## M11. Amalgam as misattribution engine

What it IS: Broeck's style is a compound of Kick, de Heem and van Aelst, so his pictures are repeatedly filed under better-known names. Shows up: the Fitzwilliam forest-floor piece, attributed to Ruysch, then to Broeck, with Gustavus Hamilton proposed; the SMK paper notes de Heem's influence "sometimes leading to miss-attributions" across the circle; Kick's own paintings are often mistaken for Walscapelle's, and Walscapelle's for Kick's. Watchable behaviour: the same picture changes author with the catalogue -- the system (shared grammar, shared motifs, shared pigments) is stronger than the signature (leg 1 F21; leg 3 N1, N3).

**Takeaway:** the style is a mixture, and mixtures get misfiled -- the catalogue, not the picture, decides the author.

## M12. The unlined canvas, tacking edges intact

What it IS: Broeck's SMK pictures survive in their original 17th-century format -- original tacking edges 1-1.5 cm wide, tack holes 4-6 cm apart, two still mounted on slim wooden strainers with wooden tacks. Shows up: all five SMK works; the two Rijksmuseum Broeck works resemble the same format. Two works share a canvas roll (matching weave maps). Watchable behaviour: the picture still carries its making -- the edge that was folded round the strainer is part of the object, not a later trim (leg 1 F18).

**Takeaway:** the painting keeps its working edge -- the tacking margin is still there, and two pictures were cut from the same roll.

## M13. The flower court as a living model bank

What it IS: a private garden kept beside the house so the painter can work from living specimens rather than from books alone. Shows up: Houbraken's Molenpad flower court; the Kick precedent (painting flowers from nature in his father-in-law's garden outside the Sint-Antoniespoort with his pupil Walscapelle). The garden is the counterweight to M7: cut-out studies for the bouquet's architecture, living plants for the detail (leg 1 F7, M13; leg 3 N1, C813).

**Takeaway:** the garden is the studio's other half -- the flowers are painted from life, then cut out and reused.

## M14. The stone plinth as stage

What it IS: the bouquet or forest-floor group set on a stone ledge, plinth or table-top, a hard horizontal that separates the arranged world from the dark ground behind it. Shows up: "Still life of Austrian briars and other flowers on a stone plinth" (EN Wikipedia illustration); the Ashmolean "Bloemen in een vaas op een stenen tafelblad", c. 1690; W07 (one rose stem on a stone ledge against near-black); W12 (bouquet in a stone niche); W13 (thistle in a stone niche); the Sotheby's Kick note on the cognate motif (a bouquet "in a glass vase over a stone ledge"). The stone edge is the picture's floor line: everything above it is arrangement, everything behind it is night (leg 1 F23, M14; leg 2 W07, W12, W13, W16).

**Takeaway:** the stone is the stage -- a hard horizontal edge, and everything arranged stands on it against the dark.

## Works

Twenty works inspected as images (W1-W20), all downloaded from Wikimedia Commons and viewed as images this leg, plus fifteen beyond the twenty (B01-B15), nine seen as images and six text-only. Dimensions are marked VERIFIED only when read on a museum or collection page read end to end; REPORTED when they come from a scholarly article, a technical poster, a dealer page or an auction listing; otherwise the study says "not verified". Dates are marked VERIFIED (seen on an image or read on a page read end to end) or REPORTED (a search snippet, a Wikidata row, a dealer or repro listing). Nothing is invented. Images in ~/workspace/fly-watch/562-broeck/ (29 JPEGs, MANIFEST.txt, 0 FAILED). The Commons category page fetch failed with HTTP 403; files were found by Commons file search instead, and there is no Commons category "Elias van den Broeck" under that exact name (leg 2 correction 9).

## W1 -- Flowers (Blomsterbillede), Statens Museum for Kunst, Copenhagen, KMSst60, undated

Date: undated; SMK range 1672-1708 (file data). Medium: oil on canvas. Dimensions: 64.5 x 57 cm REPORTED (Monaghan and Vila ICOM-CC 2014 poster; the poster was read end to end by legs 1 and 3, but it is a conference poster, not a museum page, so the dimensions stay reported under the #533 definition). Collection: Statens Museum for Kunst, Copenhagen. Saw: near-black ground. Three white roses across the top, one pink rose and one large red poppy centre, orange poppies bottom. A bird's nest with eggs bottom right, a grasshopper at right, a small snail centre. Bouquet has NO visible vase -- flowers emerge from darkness. Heavy craquelure. The "evening atmosphere" mode at its darkest (M1, M3, M5).

## W2 -- Flowers (Blomsterbillede), Statens Museum for Kunst, Copenhagen, KMSst61, undated -- NOT A FLOWER PIECE

Date: undated; SMK range 1672-1708 (file data). Medium: oil on canvas. Dimensions: 64.5 x 57 cm REPORTED (same poster). Collection: Statens Museum for Kunst, Copenhagen. Saw: NOT a flower piece despite the file name (leg 2 correction 1). A stone plinth holds a pink rose, orange poppies and white blossom; above it an oak branch with acorns and a large grasshopper; a stag beetle bottom left, mushrooms bottom left, a lizard on the plinth edge, a tortoiseshell butterfly, physalis and stones on the ground. Brown-olive ground, much lighter than W01. The "flowers" in it are a minority element; any study text or prompt that calls KMSst61 a vase or bowl flower piece is wrong (M9, M14).

## W3 -- A Bowl of Flowers (En skal med blomster), Statens Museum for Kunst, Copenhagen, KMSst241, undated

Date: undated; SMK range 1672-1708 (file data). Medium: oil on canvas. Dimensions: 62.6 x 53.6 cm REPORTED (same poster). Collection: Statens Museum for Kunst, Copenhagen. Saw: bowl on a ledge, but the top two-thirds are huge dark-green leaves; two white roses and orange nasturtiums low in the frame, a red poppy at right. Grasshoppers perch in the leaves at top. The darkest and leafiest of the five SMK works -- flowers almost swallowed by foliage (M1, M3, M7).

## W4 -- Flowers in a Bowl (Blomster i en skal), Statens Museum for Kunst, Copenhagen, KMS1878, undated

Date: undated; SMK range 1672-1708 (file data). Medium: oil on canvas. Dimensions: 62.8 x 51 cm REPORTED (same poster). Collection: Statens Museum for Kunst, Copenhagen. Saw: bowl on a stone ledge. Red poppy top left, red-white striped poppy top right, big white peony centre, a drooping pink rose hanging over the ledge edge at bottom right, poppy buds, a small blue morning glory. More open and legible than W01/W03; the bouquet is a diagonal cascade (M3, M4, M6).

## W5 -- A Bowl of Flowers (En skal med blomster), Statens Museum for Kunst, Copenhagen, KMSst217, undated

Date: undated; SMK range 1672-1708 (file data). Medium: oil on canvas. Dimensions: 63 x 53 cm REPORTED (same poster). Collection: Statens Museum for Kunst, Copenhagen. Saw: diagonal bouquet rising to a sunflower top right and a pale iris top left; white rose centre, orange poppy, pink peony bottom left, a coral-pink rose fallen onto the ledge at bottom right. Signature legible bottom left ("Elias v... Broeck"). Dark ground, visible craquelure (M1, M3, M6).

## W6 -- Vivat Rex, Paleis Het Loo, Apeldoorn, c. 1689 REPORTED -- NOT A FLOWER PIECE

Date: c. 1689 REPORTED (Paleis Het Loo / MyOpenMuseum; wahooart 1689). Medium: oil on canvas. Dimensions: not verified. Collection: Paleis Het Loo, Apeldoorn. Saw: NOT a flower piece (leg 2 correction 5). A nature tableau in a warm brown interior: bird's nest with blue eggs in a tree top right; a stag beetle and a rhinoceros beetle in flight centre; grasshoppers; a large orange fruit on a stone plinth carved "VIVAT REX"; a lizard at right; red mushrooms bottom left; physalis bottom right. Signature on the left plinth. The lightest ground of any work seen -- brown, not black (M9, M14).

## W7 -- Still Life with Roses, Rijksmuseum, SK-A-1463, 1670-1708 (life-span range, not a date)

Date: 1670-1708 on the Rijksmuseum page -- a life-span range, not a date (leg 2 correction 12). Medium: oil on canvas. Dimensions: not verified (the Rijksmuseum page read end to end lists no dimensions). Collection: Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam, SK-A-1463. Saw: the sparsest work seen: one diagonal rose stem on a stone ledge against near-black -- one huge pale-pink rose, one orange-red rose, a drooping bud below, a banded snail on the ledge, a red admiral butterfly at right. Most of the canvas is empty black. Heavy craquelure. The Rijksmuseum description mentions "a butterfly and two snails" -- one butterfly and one snail are clearly visible (a second small snail is half-hidden at the ledge edge, left) (M5, M6, M14).

## W8 -- Still Life with Passionflowers, Rijksmuseum, SK-A-788, 1670-1708 (life-span range, not a date)

Date: 1670-1708 on the Rijksmuseum page -- a life-span range, not a date (leg 2 correction 12). Medium: oil on canvas. Dimensions: not verified (the Rijksmuseum page read end to end lists no dimensions). Collection: Rijksmuseum, Amsterdam, SK-A-788. Saw: one passionflower vine on a diagonal against black: a huge white passionflower centre-left with a minutely rendered striped corona and orange anthers; buds, tendrils and lobed leaves; a halved orange passionfruit bottom right; a banded snail beside it; a small green lizard at bottom centre. Red-script signature bottom left. Palette almost monochrome pale green-white against black, with the orange fruit as the single warm accent (M4, M6, M14).

## W9 -- Still Life with a Snake, Ashmolean Museum, Oxford, WA1940.2.63, ca. 1695 REPORTED

Date: ca. 1695 REPORTED (JHNA fig. 6 caption). Medium: oil on canvas. Dimensions: 62 x 53 cm REPORTED (JHNA caption; a journal, not a museum page, so not VERIFIED under #533). Collection: Ashmolean Museum, Oxford. Saw: nocturnal forest piece. A coiled snake centre-left, jaws wide open at a grasshopper; a tall thistle with pale moths/butterflies at its purple flower top; a mouse at right; toadstools and a snail bottom; a white moth bottom right. The pale moth wings are the "pale spots" JHNA says are all that remains of the glued-on real wings -- but a pale painted moth and a former glued wing cannot be told apart from a reproduction, so do not state as fact that any specific pale spot here IS a former real wing (leg 2 correction 11). Low-resolution file (725 px wide) but the composition is clear. Do not repeat "Antwerp, c. 1695" for the Snake: JHNA says Broeck "was working in Antwerp on a series of forest pieces", but he left Antwerp in 1685 -- either the c. 1695 date or the Antwerp location is wrong (leg 2 correction 5) (M8, M9).

## W10 -- A Vase of Flowers, Ashmolean Museum, WA1940.2.16, undated

Date: undated. Medium: oil on canvas. Dimensions: not verified. Collection: Ashmolean Museum, Oxford. Saw: dense bouquet on a stone ledge, warm brown ground: orange poppies top and one fallen on the ledge at bottom, a red-orange tulip, white rose centre, pink rose bottom left, yellow marigolds. One of the most warmly lit, least black-grounded flower pieces. See leg 2 correction 4: B08 is a near-duplicate of this composition -- a second version or copy, not an independent invention; count the motif once (M3, M5, M7).

## W11 -- A Vase of Flowers, Ashmolean Museum, WA1940.2.86, undated

Date: undated. Medium: oil on canvas. Dimensions: not verified. Collection: Ashmolean Museum, Oxford. Saw: glass vase on a ledge, black ground: two red-white striped (Rembrandt) tulips at top, white and pink roses, a pink rose fallen over the ledge edge bottom left, small blue morning glories. The most de Heem-like of the flower pieces -- tulips + roses + glass vase + fallen bloom (M3, M4, M7).

## W12 -- Vase of Flowers, Fitzwilliam Museum, Cambridge, PD.19-1966, undated VERIFIED DIMENSIONS

Date: undated. Medium: oil on canvas. Dimensions: 56 x 43.5 cm VERIFIED (Fitzwilliam object page read end to end, 74 lines). Collection: Fitzwilliam Museum, Cambridge; bought from Levine and Mosley 1942; given 1966 by Fairhaven. Saw: bouquet in a stone niche (arched top visible): red poppy top right, pink rose centre, white dahlia-like flower, yellow marigold, orange calendula bottom; a small white butterfly bottom left. Olive-brown ground, lighter than the SMK pieces (M5, M14).

## W13 -- Stone Niche with Thistle, Lizard and Insects, Fitzwilliam Museum, PD.45-1966, undated

Date: undated. Medium: oil on canvas (presumed; not stated on a page read this leg). Dimensions: not verified. Collection: Fitzwilliam Museum, Cambridge. Saw: no flowers at all: a thistle plant fills the left of a stone niche, a large grasshopper on it, stag beetles in flight at right, a big green lizard centre-bottom with jaws open, two snails on the ledge. Grey-green, muted, the most "specimen-cabinet" of the niche pieces. Smallest file (586 px wide) (M9, M14).

## W14 -- Still Life with a Thistle, Boletus, Snail, Lizard, Butterflies and a Bee in a Landscape, Fitzwilliam Museum, PD.39-1975, undated -- ATTRIBUTION CONTESTED

Date: undated. Medium: oil on canvas (presumed). Dimensions: not verified. Collection: Fitzwilliam Museum, Cambridge. Saw: a thistle and boletus in a landscape that opens to a blue dusk sky and hills at left -- the only work seen with a real landscape background. Huge pale-white butterflies dominate (one lower left, one top right), a wasp centre, a green lizard bottom right, a banded snail bottom left, red boletus, tiny blue forget-me-nots. The pale butterflies look like the glued-wing motif. ATTRIBUTION: the Fitzwilliam/Commons filename says "(attributed to)". It was once given to Ruysch, and the museum note proposes Gustavus Hamilton as an alternative. Present it as attributed, never as certainly his (leg 1 C807; leg 2 correction 3) (M8, M9).

## W15 -- Still life of flowers with ants in a glass vase, undated

Date: undated. Medium: oil on canvas (presumed). Dimensions: not verified. Collection: private / not stated in file. Saw: the brightest and most colourful bouquet seen: tall glass vase on a ledge, red poppy top, orange marigolds, pink rose centre, white rose, blue morning glories, orange nasturtiums spilling over the ledge at bottom. Signature "E. v. d. Broeck" bottom right on the ledge. NO ants visible at this resolution (leg 2 correction 7) -- do not assert ants as a motif in this work. (An ant IS visible in W18, bottom left -- cite that one instead.) (M3, M5, M7).

## W16 -- Still life of Austrian briars and other flowers on a stone plinth, with a flying stag beetle and other insects, undated

Date: undated. Medium: oil on canvas (presumed). Dimensions: not verified. Collection: unknown; the EN Wikipedia lead image. Saw: stone plinth, warm brown ground: two enormous grasshoppers dominate the top half (one in flight, wings spread, legs dangling); orange briar roses centre-right; a stag beetle in flight at right; a banded snail bottom left; hazelnuts/beechnuts hanging over the ledge. Signature bottom left on the plinth edge. Insects are as large as the flowers -- the scale joke is the picture (M9, M14).

## W17 -- Flowers in a Vase (Bloemen in een vaas), Museum Boijmans Van Beuningen, Rotterdam, inv. 2966 (OK), undated

Date: undated. Medium: oil on canvas (presumed). Dimensions: not verified. Collection: Museum Boijmans Van Beuningen, Rotterdam. Saw: PHOTOGRAPH OF THE FRAMED PAINTING on a white wall, not a clean reproduction (leg 2 correction 8). Small bouquet in a dark vase: orange marigold top, white rose, yellow rose, red poppy; a small green lizard on the ledge at bottom. Frame plaque reads "Elias van den Broeck 1650-1708". Colour, crop and proportions taken from this file are approximate only; do not use it for palette claims (M3, M14).

## W18 -- Forest floor still life with blackberries, reptiles and butterflies, undated

Date: undated. Medium: oil on canvas (presumed). Dimensions: not verified. Collection: private / auction. Saw: night forest floor: blackberry bramble with huge veined leaves, a red admiral and a white butterfly top right, a blue butterfly bottom left, a large grasshopper centre, orange toadstools at right, ripe blackberries bottom, a banded snail and a green lizard bottom right, one ant visible bottom left. Top half is near-black night sky with a hint of dusk. The richest sottobosco seen (M8, M9).

## W19 -- Rocky Floor with Flowers, Mushrooms, Butterflies, a Snail and a Thistle, undated

Date: undated. Medium: oil on canvas (presumed). Dimensions: not verified. Collection: unknown. Saw: the lightest forest piece: a landscape opening top-left (blue sky, trees), a big olive-brown rock behind, orange toadstools centre, pink mallows, thistles, two large butterflies at top (tortoiseshell + red admiral), a yellow moth bottom right, a banded snail bottom left. Mushrooms and leaves are painted at near life size against the rock (M9).

## W20 -- Stilleben mit zerlegtem Fisch und Krabben (Still life with cut-up fish and crabs), Wallraf-Richartz-Museum, Cologne, WRM 1918, 1686 REPORTED -- BLACK-AND-WHITE ONLY

Date: 1686 REPORTED (dealer/repro listings, not from a museum page). Medium: oil on canvas (presumed). Dimensions: not verified. Collection: Wallraf-Richartz-Museum, Cologne. Saw: BLACK-AND-WHITE archival photograph only (leg 2 correction 2). A dish with a cut fish and a crab, grapes hanging above, leaves at right. Colour, palette and handling cannot be judged from this file. See leg 2 correction 10: this may be the same painting as B10 (Still Life with Herring, Crab, Bread and Grapes, Wallraf-Richartz, c. 1686) under an English title -- do not count them as two works and do not give them two different dates (M10).

## Beyond the 20

B01 -- Rosiers, fleurs, insectes et serpents (Roses, flowers, insects and snakes), Musee des Beaux-Arts de Narbonne, c. 1700 REPORTED (dealer/repro listings). Very dark, murky museum photograph. Pink roses centre, a fallen rose at bottom, a bird's nest with eggs top right, a pale butterfly at left, a grasshopper bottom right, a snake barely visible bottom right. The latest-dated work in the set; the darkness may be the photograph, not the painting -- do not judge palette from this file.

B02 -- Flowers, Lizards and Insects, Nationalmuseum, Stockholm, NM 18360, undated. Dark urn/vase centre with a big pink peony-rose, red and yellow poppies; a lizard on the steps at left, another in the leaves at right; grasshoppers; big textured leaves bottom right. The vase itself is barely visible -- flowers and creatures crowd it out.

B03 -- Still Life with Flowers and Butterflies, Museums Sheffield, VIS.LI.1121, undated. Arched niche, dark urn, orange poppies top, pink rose centre, red admiral butterflies; signature bottom right on the ledge. A compact, dark, de Heem-like bouquet.

B04 -- Still Life with Oysters, Butterflies, various Fruits and a gilded Hanap, undated. The true pronk piece, landscape format: a tall gilded lidded hanap (cup) centre-top, two open oysters foreground, a peach, an orange, a halved pomegranate with red seeds at right, a chestnut in its husk, white moths, a blue morning glory. Dark ground; the gold cup is the light source of the picture (M10).

B05 -- Still life with oysters, fruits, flowers, wineglass and porcelain bowl, undated, signed "v. D. Broeck" top left. Pronk-flower hybrid: a porcelain bowl centre, a peeled lemon with its peel spiralling down to the ledge, an oyster shell bottom left, blackberries and raspberries, a huge orange dahlia, a big yellow-red butterfly centre (one wing yellow, one red-black), a wine glass with red wine top right. The most de Heem-indebted composition seen (M10).

B06 -- A forest floor still life with a bird's nest, toadstools, snails, blackberries, Tortoiseshell, Aurora, and Common Blue butterflies (Christie's 1999, lot 141), undated. Low-resolution auction photograph (400 px). Bird's nest in a bramble centre, tortoiseshell + white butterflies top, blue butterfly bottom right, toadstools left, snail. Same nest-in-bramble motif as W01 and B01. Detail is not legible -- use only for motif, not handling.

B07 -- Still life with flowers (Commons "Elias van den Broeck 001"), undated. Dark urn bouquet: big white-pink rose and red poppy top, orange marigolds bottom right, a tortoiseshell butterfly centre-right, a lizard and a snail at the base, a trailing stem with rosebuds. Very dark; the urn is a black silhouette.

B08 -- A vase of flowers (generic Commons file), undated. Near-duplicate composition of W10 (leg 2 correction 4): same orange poppies top, red-orange tulip, white rose centre, pink rose bottom left, fallen orange poppy on the ledge. A second version or copy of the same motif -- count once.

B09 -- Still life with flowers (generic Commons file; EXIF: Panasonic DMC-FS30, 2014-05-19), undated. Amateur museum-wall photograph, flash-lit and washed green: a tall thistle/poppy plant, a swallowtail butterfly at left, roses bottom right. Colours unreliable; use only for motif (thistle plant as subject).

B10 -- Still Life with Herring, Crab, Bread and Grapes, Wallraf-Richartz-Museum, Cologne, c. 1686 REPORTED (repro listings). TEXT-ONLY (no Commons image found). Listed by wahooart/Meisterdrucke as the earliest dated work (1686). See leg 2 correction 10: may be the same painting as W20 under an English title -- do not count as a second work.

B11 -- A Forest Floor Landscape with a Thistle, Fungi, Moths, a Lizard and Snakes (Sphinx Fine Art; ex Beaussant Lefevre 2007 lot 83, as Marseus van Schrieck), undated. TEXT-ONLY (dealer page has an image not downloaded; description read end to end, 106 lines). Two snakes fighting, one about to swallow the other, intertwined round a thistle; fungi at the base; lizard; moths/butterflies at the purple thistle flower; spotlighted plant against receding dark forest. 37.4 x 29.9 cm REPORTED (dealer page read end to end; not a museum page, so not VERIFIED). Authenticated by Fred Meijer (RKD). Smallest dimensions in the set -- a cabinet piece (M9).

B12 -- A Sottobosco with a Snake Threatening a Lizard (auction catalogue via DocsLib snippet), undated. TEXT-ONLY. Title only, from a catalogue listing; no image located. The snake-vs-lizard pairing matches the W09/B11 motif.

B13 -- Still life with a partly-peeled orange on a pewter plate with raspberries, cherries, grapes, a loaf of bread, a pipe and a water jug on a partly-draped table (Sotheby's, April 2022), undated. TEXT-ONLY. Banquet/pronk piece in the de Heem line (peeled orange, pewter, pipe, water jug). 39.8 x 32 cm unframed REPORTED (auction listing). Provenance: Paul Delaroff, St Petersburg (wax seal) (M10).

B14 -- Bird's nest at the foot of a tree, Museum Boijmans Van Beuningen, Rotterdam, 1700 REPORTED (repro listings). TEXT-ONLY (no Commons image found). Listed as his top-ranked work on wahooart/ArtsDot. The nest-at-the-foot-of-a-tree motif recurs in W01, B01 and B06 -- this may be the best-known instance of it. Do not confuse with W01 (SMK, nest bottom right of a flower piece).

B15 -- Still Life with Roses, Insects and Snails (listed in repro-site "notable works"), undated. TEXT-ONLY (no image located). Title only. Do NOT merge with W07 (Still Life with Roses, Rijksmuseum) -- no evidence they are the same work.

Works touched: 20 main (W1-W20) + Beyond the 20 (B01-B15) = 35 works; 29 seen as images, 6 text-only (B10-B15).

## Evolution

What is uncertain, stated plainly. There is no securely dated early work in the set. The dated corpus that can be verified from sources read is four works: W20/B10 c. 1686, W06 c. 1689, W09 ca. 1695, B01/B14 c. 1700 -- all Amsterdam-period (post-1685) or undatable, and all four dates are dealer or repro-listing dates, reported not verified. The five SMK works (W01-W05), the core of the oeuvre, are dated only to the range 1672-1708 by the museum. The two Rijksmuseum works are dated 1670-1708, which is a life-span range, not a date. No work in the set can be placed in Utrecht (1669-1672) or Antwerp (1673-1685) with confidence. The only securely dated work known is the 1676 picture sold by Valls Limited, London, in 1998 (leg 1 C800) -- and no image of it was inspected this leg. Any early-vs-late claim built on these works is therefore a grouping by TYPE, not by chronology, and the study says so (leg 2 Evolution).

Grouping by type (what the images actually show):

(a) Dark-ground flower pieces -- W01, W03, W04, W05, W10, W11, W12, W15, B02, B03, B07, B08. The dominant mode: a bouquet (vase, bowl or glass) on a stone ledge against a near-black ground, lit as if at evening (Wikipedia's "evening atmosphere with strong lighting effects" is exactly what W01/W03 look like). Palette: white and pink roses, red/orange poppies, orange marigolds, blue morning glories, dark green leaves. Repeated motifs across works: the same pink rose, the same striped poppy, the same grasshopper -- consistent with the SMK poster's finding that he reused cut-out flower/insect studies (de Lairesse's method, via de Heem). The five SMK works cluster tightly in size (62-65 x 51-57 cm) and in format; KMSst60/KMSst61 are the same size (64.5 x 57) and the poster notes similar canvas weave, suggesting the same roll of canvas.

(b) Stone-plinth / stone-niche nature pieces -- W02, W07, W08, W13, W16, W12 (niche). Flowers give way to a single plant or a single stem: a passionflower vine (W08), an Austrian briar (W16), a thistle (W13), one rose stem (W07). Grounds lighten to warm brown/olive; the composition is a diagonal (van Aelst's influence, per Wikipedia) with a snail, a lizard or a butterfly as the second actor. These are the quietest, most minimal works -- W07 is mostly empty black.

(c) Forest-floor / sottobosco pieces -- W09, W18, W19, B06, B11, B12. The distinctive mode, and the one the literature singles out: a patch of forest floor at night, thistle or bramble as the vertical, and a small violent narrative -- snake about to strike a grasshopper (W09), two snakes fighting (B11), snake vs lizard (B12). Butterflies, moths, snails, toadstools, blackberries. JHNA places the Snake "in Antwerp on a series of forest pieces" -- but see leg 2 correction 5: that conflicts with the 1685 departure if the c. 1695 date holds. Sphinx traces the subgenre to Otto Marseus van Schrieck.

(d) Pronk / banquet pieces -- B04, B05, B13, W20/B10. The de Heem inheritance, and the rarest mode in the set: gilded hanap, oysters, peeled lemon/citrus, porcelain bowl, wine glass, grapes. Only B04 and B05 were seen as images; both are as dark-grounded as the flower pieces, with the gold/silver as the light source.

What changes, if anything, across the datable works: the c. 1686 fish-and-crab piece (W20, black-and-white only) and the c. 1689 Vivat Rex (W06) are both non-floral nature/banquet tableaux with warm brown grounds; the ca. 1695 Snake (W09) and the c. 1700 Narbonne roses (B01) are both dark-grounded. That is too thin a thread for an evolution claim. The honest statement is: no stylistic break is visible between the (undated) flower pieces and the (dated) late works -- the same motifs, the same dark ground, the same small format run through the whole set. The one real shift the sources support is SUBJECT, not style: the forest-floor pieces (the mode that, per Weyerman, got him expelled from Antwerp) sit alongside the flower pieces, and the pronk pieces are the smallest group. The study presents the oeuvre as four coexisting modes, not as a dated progression.

Scale: everything measured is small -- the SMK five are 62-65 cm tall, the Snake 62 x 53 cm, the Fitzwilliam vase 56 x 43.5 cm, the Sphinx forest piece 37.4 x 29.9 cm. No large-scale work was found. This is a cabinet painter: pictures made to be leaned over and inspected -- the insects and snails only resolve at close range.

Handling (from the images): fine, almost miniaturist detail on insects and snail shells; petals modelled with a pale outline (Wikipedia's "very fine outline in white or grey", inherited from de Heem -- visible on the white roses in W04/W11); leaves painted with visible veining and a waxy highlight; craquelure is heavy on the Rijksmuseum and SMK pictures (W07/W08 especially). Repeated flowers/insects across works confirm the cut-out-study workshop method.

Vanitas? No skull, watch, candle or bubble appears in any of the 29 images seen. The vanitas reading rests on the transience of the motifs themselves (fallen roses, a dropped poppy on the ledge, a snake about to kill, butterflies as resurrection symbols per Sphinx) -- not on vanitas props. Do not call any of these a vanitas still life.

## Dimensions verified vs not verified (tally)

VERIFIED on a page read end to end (1 work): W12 Fitzwilliam vase, 56 x 43.5 cm. REPORTED, NOT VERIFIED (6 of the 20): W01-W05, the SMK five (64.5 x 57, 64.5 x 57, 62.6 x 53.6, 62.8 x 51, 63 x 53 cm, from the Monaghan and Vila poster -- reported, not verified, because the poster is a conference paper, not a museum page, under the #533 definition; see C833); W09, 62 x 53 cm (JHNA caption). Beyond the 20, reported: B11, 37.4 x 29.9 cm (Sphinx dealer page); B13, 39.8 x 32 cm (Sotheby's listing). Tally for W1-W20: 1 of 20 verified (W12); 6 of 20 reported (W01-W05, W09); 13 of 20 with no dimensions at all. Do not state dimensions for any other work in the study file.

## Aesthetic logic

The picture is a workshop in miniature, and everything in it serves one decision: the bouquet is not grown, it is assembled. Flowers are painted one by one on card, cut out, sorted by colour in small boxes, and arranged on a green festoon until the composition holds -- then the whole arrangement is painted in three passes, blocked, shadowed, highlighted, with a thin white line drawn last around every petal so nothing melts into the dark. The flowers are lit as if at evening, one hard light against a ground that is already night, and set on a stone ledge that is the only floor the picture admits. The same system, turned horizontal, is the forest floor: thistle and toadstool, snake and grasshopper, a butterfly that is not painted at all but pasted on, wing by wing, because paint could not match the real thing. And when the system is turned toward the table, it is the banquet piece: the same dark ground, the same hard light, the peeled citrus doing the work of the white hairline. The garden beside the house feeds the system with living specimens, and the misattributions follow from it: a grammar this shared, with motifs this reusable and pigments this consistent, cannot be told apart from its neighbours by looking. That is why the pictures are small -- they are made to be leaned over, at the distance where a snail's shell and a moth's wing resolve, and where the cut-out rose and the real wing sit side by side without giving themselves away.

## Positions

1. Birth 1649/50 in Antwerp vs c. 1652 in Amsterdam; the burial on 6 February 1708 is the one firm point. The study states both poles, never one as fact (leg 1 C792, C799; leg 3 C809).
2. "c. 1649/1652" is struck. No source writes it; the sources write "1649/50" or "c. 1652" (leg 1 C792).
3. "Carried the pronk still life back to Amsterdam" is struck as written. Kalf was already the Amsterdam pronk master; Broeck brought de Heem's banquet manner back in 1685 (leg 1 C793; leg 3 C811).
4. "With Kick, Kouwenbergh and Walscapelle in tow" is struck. Kick died in 1681; Walscapelle was a fellow pupil who had quit painting by 1667; only Kouwenbergh followed Broeck, as his pupil in Amsterdam (leg 1 C794; leg 3 C808).
5. Houbraken's "died in the year 1711" is wrong; the burial is 6 February 1708 (leg 1 F8; leg 3 C812).
6. Stuven was not his teacher. Houbraken's "one of his first and best students" is a muddle -- chronologically impossible, and RKD does not list Broeck among Stuven's pupils. Do not repeat it (leg 1 C795; leg 3 C822).
7. The 1676 picture is the only securely dated work; W20/B10 c. 1686, W06 c. 1689, W09 ca. 1695 and B01/B14 c. 1700 are dealer or repro-listing dates, reported not verified (leg 1 C800; leg 2 Evolution).
8. Weyerman's expulsion is gossip, not fact. Christie's technique -- real wings pasted on a white ground, standard contemporary practice -- is the documented account (leg 1 C801; leg 2 correction 11).
9. W02 (KMSst61) is a stone-plinth forest piece, not a flower piece, despite the file name and the poster's listing (leg 2 correction 1).
10. W20 is a black-and-white archival photograph only; no colour or palette claim may rest on it (leg 2 correction 2).
11. W14 is attributed, not secure: once Ruysch, with Gustavus Hamilton proposed (leg 1 C807; leg 2 correction 3).
12. No vanitas props appear in any of the 29 images seen; do not call any of these a vanitas still life (leg 2 Evolution).
13. The "yellow fox rose" wording is quoted from the SMK poster's palette table ("Yellow flowers: Realgar/orpiment"), not from the garbled sentence in the extracted text (leg 3 C817).
14. Antwerp year 1672 vs 1673: the 1673 Guild mastership is firm; the accompaniment year is 1672 or 1673; 1673 is not a pupillage date (leg 1 C806; leg 3 C810).
15. There is no Commons category "Elias van den Broeck" under that exact name; files were found by Commons file search (leg 2 correction 9).
16. The Commons "(1657-1708)" birth year is impossible: 1657 would make him eight when he became Kick's pupil in 1665 (leg 2 correction 6).

## Quotes

- "Visual examination with optical microscopy and infrared imaging of the five works revealed evidence of deadcolouring, a technique commonly used in De Heem's circle." -- Monaghan and Vila, ICOM-CC Melbourne 2014 poster.
- "First, each figure in the picture is blocked out in its own basic colour; this process is often called deadcolouring. Then shadows are added to the figures painted in a darker colour or black. Finally, the figures are given more volume by painting the lighter areas and highlights in a mixture of white and basic colour." -- Monaghan and Vila, ICOM-CC Melbourne 2014 poster.
- "Yellow flowers: Realgar/orpiment (arsenic sulphide)." -- Monaghan and Vila, ICOM-CC Melbourne 2014 poster, palette table.
- "Wallert and Dik, who have studied the work of De Heem believe the use of orpiment, specifically when painting yellow fox roses, can be considered a signature flower for paintings from the workshop of De Heem. Analysis confirms that this flower is also found in works by Van den Broeck." -- Monaghan and Vila, ICOM-CC Melbourne 2014 poster. (The flower name is quoted as the poster has it; see C817 and Position 13.)
- "Paint all kinds of flowers on cardboard or thick paper... Make five of six of each... Then cut them all out, and keep the flowers, of each colour separately, in small boxes... Then paint a green festoon, or bouquet made up with foliage on a panel or cardboard. On this you can place these flowers, arranging and re-arranging them according to your concept." -- Gerard de Lairesse, quoted in Monaghan and Vila.
- "De Heem and his followers have specific compositional ties: the addition of bent, twisted or broken tree branches and the fixed placement of flower species; peonies and roses in the bottom or middle, and opium poppies and sunflowers at the top of their compositions." -- Monaghan and Vila, citing Segal.
- "Despite these various influences, van den Broeck created a distinctive personal style. This personal touch is visible in his flower pieces in which he evoked an evening atmosphere with strong lighting effects." -- EN Wikipedia, Elias van den Broeck, summarising Segal and Alen 2020, pp. 447-450.
- "Like de Heem, he accentuated the sharpness of the flowers by adding a very fine outline in white or grey. The influence of Willem van Aelst is apparent in the diagonal composition of his flower pieces." -- EN Wikipedia, Elias van den Broeck.
- "His technique for painting butterflies was, in common with others of his contemporaries, including Otto Marseus van Schriek, Jeremias Falch and Philip van Kouwenbergh, to attach the actual wings of a butterfly on a white painted ground." -- Christie's, forest-floor lot essay.
- "Ironically, of course, in almost all such cases, the wings have long disappeared, and are frequently painted in by a later hand." -- Christie's, forest-floor lot essay.
- "van den Broeck had been forced to leave Antwerp because of the indignation he had caused by pasting real butterflies on to his canvasses rather than painting them." -- Weyerman, via Sphinx Fine Art. (Gossip, not documented fact; see Position 8.)
- "He lived outside the Utrecht gate of Amsterdam on the Molenpad, where he had a flower court next to his house for his use and died in the year 1711." -- Houbraken, De Groote Schouburgh, vol. 3, p. 379 (RKD Studies translation). (The death year is wrong; the burial is 6 February 1708.)
- "it was to be seen from his handling that he had also studied with Jan Davidsz. de Heem." -- Houbraken, De Groote Schouburgh, vol. 3.
- "Due to the fact that his style is an amalgam of those of several better known artists, his paintings have often been misattributed." -- Sphinx Fine Art.
- "The Van den Broeck works in SMK's collection are rare examples of works from the 17th-century since all five have maintained their original format, including tacking edges." -- Monaghan and Vila, ICOM-CC Melbourne 2014 poster.

## Network

1. The first teacher, the Amsterdam root (N1). Cornelis Kick (bapt. 12 March 1634 - buried 21 June 1681) -- Broeck's pupil-master in Amsterdam from 27 August 1665 for four years, until 1669. Kick was trained from c. 1650 under his father Simon Kick, and painted flowers from nature in his father-in-law's garden outside the city walls, with his pupil Walscapelle. A limited range of flower species; heavily veined foliage, especially the undersides of leaves. Paintings rare, often mistaken for Walscapelle's (Sotheby's). Kick's Amsterdam shop is where Broeck's flower manner was made -- before de Heem's Utrecht studio ever touched it. FLAG: birth 1631 vs 1634 vs 1635; use bapt. 12 March 1634 as the anchored date. The #561 Kick entry's "also trained by / in the manner of Jan Davidsz. de Heem" is unverified this run (leg 3 C823); keep the hedge ("in the manner of") or verify before building on a pupillage.

2. The second teacher, the pronk system (N2). Jan Davidsz. de Heem (17 April 1606 - before 26 April 1684) -- Broeck became his pupil in Utrecht in 1669 and followed him to Antwerp (1672 per Sphinx/askART, 1673 per the Broeck article; Guild master of St. Luke 1673). De Heem's apprentice list names Broeck among ten apprentices. Mechanism (SMK, Monaghan and Vila): dead-colouring; orpiment for yellow flowers, "a signature flower for paintings from the workshop of De Heem", confirmed in Broeck's works; bent/twisted/broken branches and fixed species placement. Broeck's banquet still lifes are de Heem-influenced; his fine white/grey outline is like de Heem's; his diagonal composition takes from Willem van Aelst.

3. The fellow pupil, not a follower (N3). Jacob van Walscapelle (1644 - 16 August 1727) -- Kick's pupil 1664-1667, overlapping Broeck in Kick's Amsterdam shop 1665-1667. When Kick moved to Loenen aan de Vecht in 1667, Walscapelle left his service, moved back to Amsterdam and "took up another profession" (EN Wikipedia Walscapelle, per Houbraken; he was still living when Houbraken was writing and was probably Houbraken's source). He painted fruit/flower still lifes in de Heem's manner; many works confused with Kick's; the V and A: "his early flower paintings can barely be discerned from his teacher's". Dated works 1667-1685 (plus a recently-surfaced example dated 1699, per the Web Gallery of Art). He influenced the painter Michel Nicolas Micheux. Walscapelle is Broeck's contemporary and fellow-pupil -- he is NOT Broeck's pupil, NOT Broeck's follower, and by Houbraken's account he had quit painting before Broeck ever returned to Amsterdam (leg 3 C821).

4. The only documented pupil, the forward hop (N4). Philip van Kouwenbergh (1671-1729) -- "According to the RKD he was a pupil of Elias van den Broeck and is known for flower still lifes" (EN Wikipedia Kouwenbergh). Amsterdam his whole life. The NGA page hedges: Broeck "may have been Van Kouwenbergh's teacher", and places him among "late followers of Heem, specifically Elias van den Broeck (c. 1650-1708) ... and Rachel Ruysch (1664-1750), with whom his still lifes are sometimes confused". None of his few known paintings is dated, so no chronology can be established; Flowers in a Vase c. 1700 (NGA) probably dates from early in his career on stylistic similarity to Broeck. Kouwenbergh is the genuine forward continuation of the Broeck line into the 18th century -- and the reason he is a Spare, not the top pick: the record is too thin (few works, none dated) to carry a study under the depth standard.

5. The grandfather of the line (N5). Simon Kick (1603-1652) -- Cornelis Kick's father and teacher (Cornelis trained under him from c. 1650). Son of Delft varnish worker Willem Kick; married Christina (Stijntje) Duyster, sister of Willem Cornelisz Duyster; painted portraits and genre works with soldiers and horsemen; one of only ten 17th-century Dutch painters to practice the guardroom-scene formula. A 2021 monograph by Jochai Rosen is the first complete study, with a critical oeuvre catalogue. The chain steps back two generations: Simon (guardroom, Delft/Amsterdam) to Cornelis (flower, Amsterdam garden) to Broeck (flower + pronk, Amsterdam). Dates and mechanism rest on search snippets this leg (leg 3 S7); the study leg for Simon Kick must read his EN Wikipedia article end to end. NEW candidate for #563 (Alternate).

6. The Amsterdam pronk precedents (N7). Willem van Aelst (1627-1683) and Willem Kalf (1619 - 31 July 1693) -- van Aelst's diagonal composition influenced Broeck (EN Wikipedia Broeck). Kalf was painting pronk still lifes in Amsterdam from the 1650s -- three decades before Broeck's 1685 return. Both already carried (van Aelst #550 Reserve; Kalf #557 Spare). Their existence is what breaks D3's letter: Broeck brought his own de Heem-derived banquet manner to Amsterdam in 1685; he did not introduce the pronk form to the city.

7. The fellow apprentice, the German branch (N8). Abraham Mignon (bapt. 21 June 1640 - 27 March 1679) -- fellow de Heem apprentice; Marrel's boy who took over de Heem's Utrecht workshop; Stuven's teacher. Already carried (#556 Spare). Not re-proposed as new.

8. Fellow de Heem apprentices (N9). Alexander Coosemans (bapt. 18 March 1627 - 28 October 1689) and Jacob Marrel (1613/1614 - 1669) -- both named in de Heem's apprentice list alongside Broeck. Coosemans: pupil from 1641, Guild master 1645 at 18, Italy 1649-1651. Marrel: the Utrecht-Frankfurt bridge, Flegel's pupil, Mignon's and Merian's teacher. Already carried (Coosemans #561 Reserve; Marrel #556 Spare). Not re-proposed as new.

9. The parallel, not a follower (N6). Rachel Ruysch (1664-1750) -- the NGA places Ruysch beside Broeck as a "late follower of Heem" and notes Kouwenbergh's still lifes are sometimes confused with hers. She is the Amsterdam flower line's great contemporary -- a parallel branch, not a Broeck pupil or follower. Already carried (#536 Spare / #549 extra spare). Not re-proposed as new.

10. The dealer, HELD (N10). Bartholomeus Floquet -- Antwerp art dealer. The 10 October 1674 deed bound Broeck to work a year for him. A patron/dealer, not a maker; no dates established in any source read this run. HELD -- do not propose (leg 3 C820).

11. The one-line influencee, HELD (N11). Michel Nicolas Micheux -- Walscapelle "influenced the painter Michel Nicolas Micheux", a single RKD-derived line in the EN Wikipedia Walscapelle article, and the only record found anywhere this run. No dates, no works, no standalone article. HELD -- do not propose until a leg finds dates (leg 3 C819).

12. The Houbraken trap, NOT a link (N12). Ernst Stuven (c. 1657-1712) -- the #557 extra-spare entry calls Stuven "van den Broeck's teacher", tracing to the EN Wikipedia Stuven article's reading of Houbraken ("Houbraken mentions in his biography of Elias van den Broeck that he had been Stuven's first and best pupil (c. 1665)"). That reading is chronologically impossible: Stuven was born c. 1657, Broeck c. 1652, and Broeck was documented as Kick's pupil from 27 August 1665 -- an 8-year-old Stuven cannot have taught a 13-year-old Broeck in 1665. RKD lists Stuven's pupils as Herman van der Mijn, J.W. Windtraken and Willem Grasdorp -- not Broeck -- and Stuven's own teachers as Hainz, Voorhout, van Aelst and Mignon. Do NOT use Stuven as a Broeck network link (leg 3 C822).

## Future candidates

Top pick for #563 is Cornelis Kick (bapt. 12 March 1634 - 18 June 1681), promoted from Alternate in #561: Broeck's first teacher (1665-1669) and Walscapelle's teacher (1664-1667), the Amsterdam flower painter who taught the pupil who carried the pronk still life back to Amsterdam in 1685. After Broeck opens Amsterdam, the chain steps back to the man who made Broeck's Amsterdam manner -- as #557 stepped back to de Heem, and as the sons followed the father. Kick offers two mechanisms no other candidate offers: flowers painted from nature in his father-in-law's garden (per Houbraken, with his pupil Walscapelle), with a limited species range and heavily veined foliage; and the attribution tangle with Walscapelle, whose works are confused with his teacher's. Kouwenbergh, the forward hop, is too thin (few works, none dated) to top-pick now; Walscapelle quit painting in 1667 per Houbraken and is the weaker alternate. FLAG: birth 1631 vs 1634 vs 1635 (leg 3 C745); use bapt. 12 March 1634 as the anchored date. See also C823 (the "also trained by de Heem" hedge in the #561 Kick entry is unverified this run) and C813 (the Kick article's garden chronology does not cohere).

The study of a follower so close he disappears into his master. The study of the garland as connoisseurship -- the man who catalogued the collection that collected Seghers. Jean-Baptiste Monnoyer (12 January 1636 - 20 February 1699) -- began in Antwerp as a student of Davidsz. de Heem, then Paris, Gobelins and Beauvais, Versailles, and Montagu House in London; the French turn of the de Heem line. Attached to the Gobelins and Beauvais tapestry workshops (cartoons of fruit and flowers); decorative painting at Marly, Meudon, Vincennes, Saint-Cloud, Versailles and the Grand Trianon; received at the Academie Royale in 1665; in 1690 left France for England to decorate Montagu House, Bloomsbury (over fifty panels of fruit and flowers); died in London 1699. FLAGS: birth 19 July 1634 (Dictionary of National Biography) vs 12 January 1636 (Wikipedia); the de Heem pupillage rests on Sotheby's alone and is squeezed by "in Paris by 1650" (leg 3 C746). 2 whole-log mentions in 2 lines (was 1 in 1 at #561 pick time; the #561 entry added 1). (The 16 names carried in #556 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Reserve: Osias Beert the Elder (c. 1580 - 1623) -- the Antwerp pioneer of both the flower bouquet and the breakfast table, Beert the teacher of Ykens and the model for Hulsdonck. Alternates: Jacob van Hulsdonck (1582 - 1647) -- the Middelburg-orbit Antwerp painter whose restrained tulip pieces are the quiet middle of the flower line; Georg Flegel (c. 1566 - 23 March 1638) -- the first German still-life specialist, whose Frankfurt table-pieces are the German answer to the Middelburg bouquet. Spares: Jacob Marrel (1614 - 1681; see FLAG) -- the Utrecht-Frankfurt bridge, Flegel's pupil, de Heem's pupil, Mignon's and Merian's teacher; Abraham Mignon (bapt. 21 June 1640 - 27 March 1679) -- Marrel's boy who took over de Heem's Utrecht workshop and purified his master's opulence; Maria Sibylla Merian (2 April 1647 - 13 January 1717) -- the stepdaughter who took the flower piece out of the studio and into the field, and then to Surinam; Maria van Oosterwijck (1630 - 1693) -- the Delft preacher's daughter who crossed the country to study under de Heem and became the North's most successful woman flower painter before Ruysch; Bartholomeus Assteyn (1607 - 1669/1677; see FLAG) -- the Dordrecht still-life man whose technique is van der Ast's and whose compositions are Johannes Bosschaert's; Jan Brueghel the Younger (bapt. 13 September 1601 - 1678) -- the son who kept his father's workshop running in his father's manner after 1625; Jan Philip van Thielen (1618 - 1667) -- Seghers's most popular pupil, the garland painter whose three daughters all became flower painters; Frans Ykens (bapt. 17 April 1601 - 1693) -- Beert's nephew and pupil, whose glass-vase bouquets follow Seghers and van Thielen; Hendrick van Balen the Elder (1575 - 1632; see FLAG) -- the figure painter who painted the Virgin inside Brueghel's first garland and co-invented the genre; Sebastian Stoskopff (13 July 1597 - 10 February 1657) -- the Alsatian glass-painter, Flegel's heir in composition, rediscovered after 1930; Otto Marseus van Schrieck (1619 - 1678; see FLAG) -- the forest-floor painter who took the still life down to the ground, among the snakes and thistles; Maria Theresa van Thielen (1640 - 1706) -- the eldest of van Thielen's three flower-painting daughters, the garland carried into the next generation by a woman; Geertje Pieters, also Geertje Wyntges (1636 - 1712) -- the maid van Oosterwijck trained into a painter, the studio's lowest rung become an artist. (The 18 names carried in #555 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Mains: Bartholomeus Spranger (21 March 1546 - 27 June 1611) -- the Prague court turn, the senior Mannerist whose mythologies were the court's house style when Savery arrived, and the painter whose designs Goltzius engraved out of Prague into Europe; Ambrosius Bosschaert the Elder (18 January 1573 - 1621) -- the flower pioneer beside Savery, the first painter of the floral still life as an independent genre, Savery's Utrecht friend, whose dynasty Savery influenced and whose brother-in-law van der Ast carried the line on. Reserve: Hans von Aachen (1552 - 4 March 1615) -- the court's diplomat-painter, appointed Rudolf's official painter 1592, the emperor's art adviser, knighted 1605, and one of the few Rudolfine artists Matthias retained after 1612, the court career Savery's own (1613, unpaid, gone) is measured against. Alternates: Roelant Roghman (14 March 1627 - 3 January 1692) -- the namesake pupil, Savery's great-nephew, named for him, his pupil in the late 1630s, who turned the Savery landscape into 200+ etched views of castles and estates and taught Jan Griffier and Pieter Wouwerman; Gijsbert Gillisz. de Hondecoeter (1604 - 29 August 1653) -- the middle Hondecoeter, Gillis's son, Melchior's father, the barnyard-fowl painter who carried Savery's animal landscape one generation toward the bird piece. Spares: George Edwards (1694 - 1773) -- the dodo's afterlife, the ornithologist who owned Savery's 1626 dodo, gave it to the British Museum, and became "the father of British Ornithology", the study would be a painting becoming a scientific specimen; Conrad Gessner (26 March 1516 - 13 December 1565) -- the book behind the cabinet, Historia animalium (1551-1558) is the encyclopaedia Hoefnagel and Savery both worked from, the first modern zoology; Anselmus Boetius de Boodt (1550 - 1632) -- the court's other naturalist, Rudolf's physician and gemologist, author of the lapidary Gemmarum et Lapidum Historia (1609), the scholar beside the painters in the Kunstkammer; Geertruydt Roghman (1625 - before 1657; see FLAG) -- the engraver sister, Savery's great-niece, who etched her brother's landscapes for Visscher and then made the original Household Tasks suite, five prints of women working indoors; Ambrosius Bosschaert the Younger (1609 - 1645) -- the son who kept the dynasty, eldest of the three flower-painter sons, trained by van der Ast after his father's death, the closest follower of the Bosschaert manner; Otto van Veen (1556 - 1629) -- Rubens's teacher, briefly a Rudolfine, van Mander says he "worked for a short time at the courts of Rudolf II in Prague", with the caveat that no works or documents support it; Wenzel Jamnitzer (1507/08 - 19 December 1585) -- the goldsmith's nature, court goldsmith to four emperors including Rudolf II, publisher of Perspectiva Corporum Regularium (1568), regular solids as a system of nature beside Savery's irregular one; Jan van Kessel the Elder (1626 - 1679) -- the insect painter, Brueghel's grandson, Hoefnagel's heir in miniature, studies of insects, flowers and paradise landscapes with almost scientific accuracy. All thirteen verified NOT STUDIED: zero study titles, zero study-log headers, no local study files; all 14 muse slugs re-checked 404 by the authenticated GitHub API this run (by leg 3, and by the parent). (Main 1 was the top pick, Balthasar van der Ast, now the subject of this study.)

Extra verified spares: Magdalena Roghman (1637 - c. 1669) -- the third Roghman sibling, also an engraver; Johannes Bosschaert (c. 1612/13 - 1628 or later; see FLAG) -- the middle Bosschaert son, dead young; Abraham Bosschaert (b. 1612/13 - 1643) -- the youngest Bosschaert son, longest-lived; Paul Vredeman de Vries (1567 - 1617) -- painted the ceilings and reception rooms of Rudolf's castle 1596-99, the rooms Savery worked under; Hans Vredeman de Vries (1527 - c. 1607; see FLAG) -- Paul's father, perspective theorist, in Prague 1596.
