(The 13 names carried in #554 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Mains: Paul Bril (1554 Antwerp - 1626 Rome) -- the Antwerp landscapist who carried the Flemish forest to Rome, befriended Jan Brueghel the Elder there 1592-94, collaborated with him and Rottenhammer, and his work carries Frankenthal/Coninxloo references; Jan Brueghel the Elder (1568 - 13 January 1625) -- Coninxloo's co-developer of the dense forest landscape, who experimented with wooded glades before Coninxloo's first dated wooded landscape of 1598, friend and collaborator of Paul Bril and Peter Paul Rubens, inventor of paradise landscapes and flower garlands. Reserve: David Vinckboons (baptised 13 August 1576 Mechelen - c. 1632 Amsterdam) -- the Amsterdam line, family fled Antwerp, settled Amsterdam 1591, his early wooded landscapes "most indebted to the Frankenthal painter Gillis van Coninxloo", with Bol and Savery he developed genre painting in the north, teacher of Gillis d'Hondecoeter and Claes Jansz. Visscher. Alternates: Anton Mirou (1578 - 1627?; see FLAG) -- leading Frankenthaler, worked alongside Coninxloo from c. 1586, his bosky landscapes "influenced to a great degree by his fellow Frankenthal painter Gillis van Coninxloo", his 1615 Schwalbach drawings engraved by Merian in 26 plates (1620); Pieter Schoubroeck, or Schaubroeck (c. 1570 - 10 May 1607) -- Frankenthal school member, called Coninxloo's pupil, Italy 1595, Nuremberg 1597-1600, Frankenthal 1601 until his death there. Spares: Frederik van Valckenborch (1566 Antwerp - 1623 Nuremberg) -- imaginary Mannerist landscapes, "his main influence, next to his father, was probably Gillis van Coninxloo"; Alexander Keirincx (1600 - 1652) -- Antwerp-trained, early works "greatly indebted to ... Gillis van Coninxloo", emigrated north 1626-28, worked for Charles I in London 1640/41, settled Amsterdam 1643; Gillis d'Hondecoeter (c. 1575/80 - 1638) -- pupil of Roelant Savery and of David Vinckboons, the bridge from Coninxloo's forest into the Dutch animal landscape, father of Melchior; Nicolaes de Bruyn (1571 Antwerp - 1656 Rotterdam) -- the engraver who carried Coninxloo's designs into the Dutch print market, large engraved landscapes after Coninxloo and Vinckboons from c. 1600, c. 400 prints over sixty years; Jan van Londerseel (c. 1570/72 - c. 1625) -- de Bruyn's brother-in-law, only follower and pupil, publisher and engraver of the Coninxloo-derived large landscapes; Gijsbrecht Leytens (1586 - after 1643 and before 1656) -- the "Master of the Winter Landscapes", "influenced by Jan Brueghel the Elder and Gillis van Coninxloo", wooded landscapes with small figures bracketed by repoussoir trees; Gillis van Coninxloo III (c. 1581 - 1619/20) -- the son and pupil, known for still lifes rather than his father's forests, the family line in one generation; Jacob Savery (c. 1565 - 1603) -- Roelant's older brother and teacher, Hans Bol's best pupil (van Mander), the bench that made the Prague dodo-painter. Gillis Mostaert the Elder (27 or 28 November 1528 - 28 December 1598) -- the workshop host who took Coninxloo in after his mother's death in 1562, winter landscapes and fire scenes, his pupils included Coninxloo; Philipp Uffenbach (15 January 1566 - 6 April 1636; dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3) -- main 2: the Frankfurt teacher who taught Elsheimer before Rome -- sundials, geometry, mechanics and alchemy in the German workshop tradition. Frankfurt painter, etcher and printmaker; trained by Hans Grimmer; published the Zeitweiser sundial booklet in 1598. The doorway is the German workshop: Uffenbach is the bench Elsheimer sat on before Italy, the sundial-maker who taught a painter to measure light before he ever painted it. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; muse slug study/philipp-uffenbach.md 404, re-checked by curl this run.

Johann (Hans) Rottenhammer (1564 - 14 August 1625; dates search-verified only) -- main 3: the Venetian cabinet-painting master who employed Elsheimer as an assistant in 1598/99 and introduced him to Bril -- the first German artist to specialise in cabinet paintings on copper, the format Elsheimer made his own. Rottenhammer collaborated with Bril, Jan Brueghel the Elder and Elsheimer, and two of his drawings belonged to Elsheimer as gifts. The doorway is the format itself: Rottenhammer invented the German cabinet copper in Venice, and Elsheimer took it to Rome and made it the nocturne. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; muse slug study/johann-rottenhammer.md 404, re-checked by curl this run (study/hans-rottenhammer.md also 404). David Teniers the Elder (1582 - 29 July 1649; dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3) -- main 4: the Antwerp painter who studied under Elsheimer in Rome and lodged with him -- the one documented case of Elsheimer teaching a painter in person, and the four Vienna landscapes painted under Elsheimer's influence. He studied under Rubens in Antwerp, and subsequently under Elsheimer in Rome. The doorway is the only pupil: Teniers is the one painter Elsheimer actually taught, face to face, in the Rome lodging -- the control case against the print-mediated Dutch line. Lodewijk Toeput, called il Pozzoserrato (c. 1540/1550 - between 1603 and 1605; dates search-verified only) -- alternate 1: the Venetian landscape elder whose Treviso workshop trained de Momper and shaped the Flemish landscape tradition Rottenhammer came out of; Rottenhammer was documented in Treviso in 1589, possibly staying with Toeput. Toeput influenced de Momper, Verhaecht and Gillis and Frederik van Valckenborch, and collaborated with de Momper on a Seasons pair in 1583. The doorway is the Treviso workshop: the elder who trained the generation Rottenhammer belonged to, a step behind the Venetian cabinet line. Joos de Momper the Younger (1564 - 1635; dates search-verified only) -- alternate 2: Toeput's apprentice and the mountain-landscape transition -- his works were inspired by Jan Brueghel the Elder and Paul Bril, the two Flemish landscape poles of Elsheimer's Rome. Apprenticed in Toeput's Treviso workshop; travelled in Italy. The doorway is the mountain: de Momper took the Flemish landscape up into the high ground Brueghel and Bril never reached, and his workshop trained the next generation. Willem van Nieulandt II, properly Guilliam van Nieuwelandt (1582/84 - 1635; dates search-verified only) -- spare 1: Paul Bril's workshop in Rome 1601-03; engraved after Matthijs and Paul Bril and after Vrancx. Pupil of Roelant Savery from 1599; lived with his uncle and Abraham Janssens in the Via Paulina. Aelbert Cuyp (20 October 1620 - 15 November 1691; dates search-verified only) -- main 1: the friend who painted the daylight into Aert's moonlight. Shared canvases: in the Louvre Landscape at Sunset Cuyp "evidently painted the foreground and cows" (Britannica); in a National Gallery picture he signs his name on the pail of a milkmaid whose figure and red skirt he painted; in the Staedel picture the fishermen, dog and sportsman "are Cuyp's". The golden light against Aert's silver one -- the daylight pair to the nocturne, and the study the series needs next. Jan van Goyen (13 January 1596 - 27 April 1656; dates search-verified only) -- main 2: the tonal root Aert's moonlight inverts. Van Goyen, Salomon van Ruysdael and Pieter de Molijn founded the tonal landscape of the 1630s -- thin paint, low horizon, a near-monochrome palette -- and Aert's coloured-light nocturne is the same thin paint turned to night. The bankruptcy parallel is exact: van Goyen died 18,000 guilders in debt after speculating in tulips and property; Aert went bankrupt in 1662 and died in poverty. The two failures frame the same market. The study would be the teacher whose influence his pupil's work does not show -- the honest negative case. The doorway is the teacher whose pupil shows no trace of him. (The 14 names carried in #547 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Govert Flinck (25 January 1615 - 2 February 1660), Ferdinand Bol (24 June 1616 - 24 August 1680), Willem Drost (baptised 19 April 1633 - buried 25 February 1659) (mains); Jacob Moelaert (1649-1727) (reserve); Margaretha van Godewijk (30 or 31 August 1627 - 2 November 1677), Johannes Vollevens (1649, Geertruidenberg - 1728, The Hague) (alternates); spares: Matthijs Balen (1684-1766), Hendrik Noteman (1654-1737), Augustin Terwesten (1649-1711), Werner Sumowski (8 March 1931 - 3 September 2015), Ernst van de Wetering (9 March 1938 - 11 August 2021), Jacob Adriaensz Backer (1608/9 - 27 August 1651), Barthoud van Slingelandt (1654-1711), Godfrey Kneller (8 August 1646 - 19 October 1723). Govert Flinck (25 January 1615 - 2 February 1660; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 135 lines) -- main 1, strongest NEW name: the Rembrandt pupil who left the manner for Rubens, and whose last commission Rembrandt himself inherited. Apprenticed to a silk mercer, then Lambert Jacobsz in Leeuwarden with Jacob Backer; Sandrart, visiting Holland in 1637, found him acknowledged as one of Rembrandt's best pupils, living in the dealer Hendrick van Uylenburgh's house; he followed Rembrandt's style in all works 1636-1648, then turned to the swelling forms and grand action of Rubens, winning the patronage of Friedrich Wilhelm I, the Great Elector of Brandenburg, and John Maurice of Nassau; Amsterdam burgher 1652, married the heiress Sophie van der Houven 1656, intimate with the poet Joost van den Vondel; in November 1659 the burgomaster contracted him for 12 canvases of David, Samson, Manius Curius Dentatus, Horatius Cocles and the Batavians, and he died leaving only sketches -- Rembrandt was asked to fill one and produced The Conspiracy of Claudius Civilis, which the authorities rejected. His Landscape with Obelisk was historically mistaken for Rembrandt and was stolen in the 1990 Gardner heist. The doorway is the Rembrandt pupil who left the manner for Rubens -- and whose last commission Rembrandt inherited. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions ("Govert Flinck" 0; "Govaert Flinck" 0); no local file; not carried in #522-#546 as a candidate. FLAG: spelling Govert vs Govaert. Ferdinand Bol (24 June 1616 - 24 August 1680; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 93 lines) -- main 2: the Dordrecht Rembrandt pupil who became Amsterdam's most commissioned painter, and whose pictures were mistaken for Rembrandt's for a century. Born in Dordrecht, son of the surgeon Balthasar Bol (painted by Rembrandt twenty years later); apprentice first of Jacob Gerritsz Cuyp in Dordrecht and/or Abraham Bloemaert in Utrecht, then of Rembrandt after 1630, living in his house on the Sint Antoniesbreestraat, neighbours with Flinck; own studio 1641; Amsterdam burgher 1652; married Elisabeth Dell 1653, whose father held Admiralty and wine-merchants' posts that later commissioned him; head of the guild 1655, chimney pieces for Jacob van Campen's new town hall and four more for the Admiralty; by the mid-1650s receiving more official commissions than any other artist in Amsterdam, his palette lightened and his figures grown elegant; four paintings for the Trip brothers' mansions; his pupil was Godfrey Kneller; he retired from painting after remarrying Anna van Erckel in 1669 and died on the Herengracht. His Portrait of Elisabeth Bas and many others were falsely attributed to Rembrandt in the 19th century. The doorway is the Dordrecht boy who out-commissioned every painter in Amsterdam -- and was then mistaken for his master. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#546 as a candidate (mentioned once in the #546 Future candidates section inside the Jacob Gerritsz Cuyp carried entry -- "Teacher of Ferdinand Bol..." -- a mention, not a proposal). FLAG: the 1822 James list gives his death as 1681 (error); apprenticeship dated only as "after 1630". Willem Drost (baptised 19 April 1633 - buried 25 February 1659; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 163 lines) -- main 3: the mysterious pupil who died at 25 in Venice, and whose pictures still carry Rembrandt's name. Rembrandt's student in the late 1640s or early 1650s per Houbraken, developing a close working relationship; his 1654 Bathsheba with King David's Letter answers Rembrandt's Bathsheba of the same year, same subject, different treatment, both now in the Louvre; Amsterdam until 1655, then Rome, friend of Karel Lot (Johann Carl Loth) and Joan vander Meer, collaborating with Loth on a lost Four Evangelists series in Venice, where he died; his Portrait of a Young Woman with her Hands Folded on a Book was attributed to Rembrandt for more than 300 years, and the Polish Rider -- found 1897, Frick Collection -- has been argued as his since Josua Bruyn in 1984, though the Frick has not changed the attribution, and a group of "Rembrandt" drawings reattributed to Drost is more widely accepted. The doorway is the pupil who died at 25 in Venice and left Rembrandt holding his name. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#546 as a candidate. FLAG: birthplace and date "presumably Amsterdam... when and where is unknown" vs baptised 19 April 1633, Amsterdam; the Polish Rider attribution remains unresolved. Jacob Moelaert (1649-1727; dates from the Leiden Collection de Gelder biography snippet) -- the reserve: the friend who left de Gelder his Rembrandts. Amateur painter and printseller, pupil of Nicolaes Maes in Dordrecht; well acquainted with de Gelder, and at his death in 1727 left de Gelder three albums of prints and drawings, including one with work by Rembrandt; de Gelder died suddenly less than a month later, on 27 August of that same year. He is the reserve because his painted oeuvre is thin -- amateur and dealer first -- though his mechanism (the print album as a bequest, the collection as a friendship) is the most intimate in the network. The doorway is the friend whose albums outlived them both by a month. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#546 as a candidate (mentioned once in the #546 Future candidates section inside the Maes carried entry -- a mention, not a proposal). FLAG: dates rest on the Leiden Collection snippet only. Margaretha van Godewijk (30 or 31 August 1627, Dordrecht - 2 November 1677, Dordrecht; dates search-verified only) -- alternate 1: the Dordrecht polymath with no surviving works. Poet and painter; her father, a Latin-school teacher, taught her Greek, Latin, Italian, French and English; learned to paint from Nicolaes Maes per Houbraken (the RKD says pupil of Cornelis Bisschop), skilled at landscapes, villas, houses, flowers and ships in oils, watercolours and embroidery, good at astronomy and at diamond-engraving on glass roemers; Houbraken quoted the Dordrecht historian Mathias Balen (1611-1691) on her among his famous women. She is alternate 1 because no works survive -- the study would be built from the life and the loss -- but the doorway (a woman who painted, wrote, and engraved glass in a town that kept Rembrandt's manner) is the sharpest in the alternates. The doorway is the Dordrecht woman who painted, wrote poetry, and engraved glass -- and left nothing to look at. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions (both spellings Godewijk/Godewijck 0); no local file; not carried in #522-#546 as a candidate (mentioned once in the #546 Future candidates section inside the Maes carried entry -- a mention, not a proposal). FLAG: birth 30 vs 31 August 1627; teacher Maes (Houbraken) vs Bisschop (RKD); no works survive. Johannes Vollevens (1649, Geertruidenberg - 1728, The Hague; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 25 lines) -- alternate 2: the portraitist whose birth year Houbraken got wrong by five years. Born in Geertruidenberg, sketched and drew in charcoal so enthusiastically his parents sent him to learn drawing; first lessons from Caspar Netscher per Houbraken, then Nicolaes Maes, then Jan de Baen in The Hague (RKD: Maes's pupil in Dordrecht, moved to The Hague 1664, studied with de Baen); joined the Confrerie Pictura 1674; his son Johannes Vollevens II also became a respected portrait painter; his portrait of Gaspar Fagel survives. He is alternate 2 because his mechanism (the provincial portraitist who kept moving to the next master) is a career shape rather than a system -- but the Houbraken error (born 1654, "the same year that took Fabritius by a stroke of fate", in fact five years earlier) is the chain's own correction made visible. The doorway is the painter Houbraken misdated by the Delft explosion. The doorway is Arnold's own pupil, the Dordrecht boy who went to The Hague and came home. De Gelder's painted portrait of him has been preserved; together they visited the Hague painter Augustin Terwesten while he was painting a round room in burgomaster Barthoud van Slingelandt's Dordrecht house. The doorway is the woodcarver his painter friend could not stop painting. Werner Sumowski (8 March 1931, Ortelsburg - 3 September 2015, Stuttgart; dates from the HNA obituary snippet) -- spare 4: the scholar who knew the Rembrandt school better than anyone alive. Professor at Stuttgart 1973-1993; from 1979 his ten-volume opus on the drawings of Rembrandt's pupils, and six volumes on the paintings (1983, with later additions) covering 99 named artists and more than 2,500 works; Gary Schwartz's verdict: he knew "more about the Rembrandt school as anyone ever alive". He is a spare, not a main, because he is the cataloguer of the line, not a maker in it -- but the line's attribution problem (de Gelder, Drost, Bol all carried Rembrandt's name) is his life's work. The doorway is the man who sorted ninety-nine pupils from their master. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#546 as a candidate. Ernst van de Wetering (9 March 1938 - 11 August 2021; dates from Dictionary of Art Historians and EN Wikipedia snippets) -- spare 5: the Rembrandt police. Joined the Rembrandt Research Project in 1968, chairman from 1993, directed its later stages; doctorate cum laude 1986 on the workshop practice of the early Rembrandt; professor at Amsterdam 1987-1999, Slade Professor at Oxford 2002-03; Rembrandt: the Painter at Work (1997) and Rembrandt: The Painter Thinking (2016); the six-volume A Corpus of Rembrandt Paintings (1982-2014) was the project's principal result; his evaluations were so conclusive on value that he was dubbed "the Rembrandt police", and Gary Schwartz called his work "deleterious to scholarship" because it ended debate on authenticity. The doorway is the chairman who decided which Rembrandts were Rembrandts. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#546 as a candidate. Jacob Adriaensz Backer (1608/9, Harlingen - 27 August 1651, Amsterdam; dates from Leiden Collection and EN Wikipedia snippets) -- spare 6: Flinck's bedfellow, and the pupil who may never have been a pupil. Mennonite baker's son; with Flinck a pupil of Lambert Jacobsz in Leeuwarden 1627-1633 -- Houbraken calls Backer Flinck's "bedfellow and companion in Art" -- then Amsterdam, probably working for Hendrick van Uylenburgh; about 140 paintings in twenty years, portraits, religious and mythological subjects, his Company of Cornelis de Graeff schutterstuk hung for years on the same wall as the Night Watch; taught Abraham van den Tempel; died at 42, three months after joining the Remonstrant congregation. The doorway is Flinck's companion, whose Rembrandt apprenticeship may be a three-hundred-year misreading. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions ("Jacob Adriaensz Backer" 0; "Jacob Backer" 0); no local file; not carried in #522-#546 as a candidate. Barthoud van Slingelandt (1654-1711; dates from the Leiden Collection de Gelder biography snippet) -- spare 7: the burgomaster who gave Terwesten a round room. Burgomaster of Dordrecht; in his house Terwesten was painting "een kamer in het rond" when de Gelder and Noteman visited. He is a spare because he is a patron, not a maker -- but he is the only named Dordrecht patron in de Gelder's circle, and the round room is the commission the visit turned on. The doorway is the burgomaster whose walls went round. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#546 as a candidate. FLAG: dates rest on the Leiden Collection snippet only. Godfrey Kneller (8 August 1646, Lubeck - 19 October 1723, London; dates from EN Wikipedia snippet) -- spare 8: Bol's pupil who became England's court painter. Born Gottfried Kniller, son of the Lubeck portrait painter Zacharias Kniller; studied mathematics at Leiden, then became a pupil of Ferdinand Bol and Rembrandt in Amsterdam by his father's choice; earliest dated work, Johann Philipp von Schonborn, 1666; with his brother John Zacharias Kneller to Rome and Venice in the early 1670s, in the studio of Carlo Maratti; to England 1676, patronage of the Duke of Monmouth; the face of Restoration and Augustan England for the next four decades. The doorway is the Lubeck boy Bol taught, who painted every face in England. G. van Gelder (1903-80) at Utrecht; his Rembrandt: His Life, His Paintings (1968, with Horst Gerson's text) caused a stir by cutting Bredius's 610 to 380; fifty years in the authenticity debates, calling van de Wetering's work "deleterious to scholarship". Held back because he is a big figure who needs a full study slot of its own -- the same note the Lastman carried entry gives him. (The 10 names carried in #546 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Nicolaes Maes (January 1634 - December 1693) (main); Frans van Mieris the Elder (16 April 1635 - 12 March 1681), Caspar Netscher (c. 1639 - 15 January 1684) (alternates); spares: Arnold Boonen (16 December 1669 - 2 October 1729), Antonina Houbraken (30 May 1686 - December 1736), David van Hoogstraten (14 March 1658 - 21 November 1724), Giorgio Vasari (30 July 1511 - 27 June 1574), Cornelis de Bie (10 February 1627 - c. 1712-1715), Filippo Baldinucci (3 June 1625 - 10 January 1696), Willem van Drielenburch (1632 - c. 1687). Nicolaes Maes (January 1634 - December 1693, buried 24 December 1693; dates search-verified only) - main 3: the Dordrecht Rembrandt pupil who broke away and invented the eavesdropper. In Rembrandt's Amsterdam studio c. 1648-1653, back in Dordrecht by December 1653 for twenty years, then Amsterdam from 1673, where he painted some 900 portraits and died rich. His Dordrecht genre pictures (1655-1665) are the hinge of the chain: the eavesdropper at the foot of the stairs, finger to her lips, breaking the fourth wall -- a domestic interior as a listening machine, the exact subject Vermeer would inherit. His registered Dordrecht pupils included Justus de Gelder, Margaretha van Godewijk, Jacob Moelaert and Johannes Vollevens. He is main 3 rather than top pick because he left Dordrecht for Amsterdam portraiture, where de Gelder stayed and kept Rembrandt's manner alive in the town itself. The doorway is the Dordrecht pupil who left Rembrandt, invented the eavesdropper, and then painted nine hundred portraits. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions ("Nicolaes Maes" 0); no local file; muse repo study/nicolaes-maes.md 404; not carried in #522-#545 as a candidate (mentioned once inside a carried entry, as the man Jacob's recommendation "is said to have sent... to Rembrandt" -- a mention, not a proposal). FLAG: death 24 December 1693 (burial, Wikipedia) vs 24 November 1693 (Prabook, Alchetron). Frans van Mieris the Elder (16 April 1635 - 12 March 1681; dates search-verified only) - alternate 1: the Leiden fine painter at his smallest and most polished. Goldsmith's son; glass-painter's pupil, then Gerard Dou's "Prince of my students"; panels seldom larger than 12-15 inches; shiny brightness and metallic polish, satin skirts, oyster luncheons and doctor's visits; founder of a three-generation Leiden dynasty (Willem, Frans the Younger). Houbraken is the source for his birth date, and the 1911 Britannica already doubts it. He is alternate 1 because his mechanism (the miniature polish) overlaps van der Werff's, and his life is short on incident -- but he is the fijnschilder standard against which Werff and Schalcken are measured. The doorway is the prince of Dou's students, painting satin on panels the size of a hand. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions ("Frans van Mieris" 0); no local file; muse repo study/frans-van-mieris-the-elder.md 404 (and study/frans-van-mieris.md 404); not carried in #522-#545 as a candidate (mentioned once inside a carried entry, as a follower's model -- a mention, not a proposal). FLAG: birth 16 April 1635 "according to Houbraken" (1911 Britannica: "It is a question whether Houbraken has truly recorded this master's birthday" -- an oyster-luncheon picture is dated 1650); "? 1635" in the 1911 Britannica heading. Caspar Netscher (c. 1639 - 15 January 1684; dates search-verified only) - alternate 2: the Hague portraitist who painted silk. Orphaned young, raised in Arnhem, ter Borch's most gifted pupil in Deventer (c. 1655-1659), then The Hague from 1662, where from the 1670s he was the most sought-after portrait painter in the city; master of oriental rugs, silk and brocade, "introduced an international style to the Northern Netherlands"; painted lying in bed with gout until 15 January 1684, when, "according to Houbraken, 'this affliction affected his bowls, and caused his death'". He is alternate 2 because his mechanism (fabric as the picture) is a single brilliant trick rather than a system -- but it is the trick the whole Hague portrait trade ran on. The doorway is the painter who sold silk by the yard and died painting in bed. Arnold Boonen (16 December 1669 - 2 October 1729; dates search-verified only) - spare 1: the Dordrecht portraitist who carried the candle out of the genre piece and into the state portrait. Pupil first of Arnold Verbuis, then six years (from 1683) under Schalcken; small candlelit genre pictures before 1700, then life-size portraits at the German courts (1694-95) and in Amsterdam from 1696 -- Peter the Great, Marlborough, the Prince and Princess of Orange; teacher of Cornelis Troost. The doorway is the Schalcken pupil who took candlelight to the German courts and came home to paint Peter the Great. FLAG: an "Anatomy Lesson" dated 1731 circulates under his name, two years after his death (misattribution or another hand). Antonina Houbraken (30 May 1686 - December 1736, buried 12 December 1736; dates search-verified only) - spare 3: PROMOTED from the #545 extra verified spares, where she was held back only because no dates were established; this run establishes them (born 30 May 1686, baptised 31 May 1686 -- see C569). The daughter who drew the country. Draughtswoman and portraitist; learned in her father's workshop; assisted father and brother on the Schouburgh designs and provided designs for Pieter Tanje; married the draughtsman Jacobus Stellingwerff 1723 and, after his death in 1727, kept supplying topographical drawings to the collectors Andries and Gerrit Schoemaker until at least 1729 -- hundreds of Dutch sites, towns and buildings drawn on the spot, later embellished with animals and people. The doorway is the daughter who drew the whole country for the collectors' albums. FLAG: death given as "December 1736" (EN Wikipedia) with burial 12 December 1736; Prabook's "March 31, 1686" is wrong (see C569). David van Hoogstraten (14 March 1658 - 21 November 1724; dates search-verified only) - spare 4: the poet inside the painter's book. Physician, poet, philologist; Samuel van Hoogstraten's nephew (son of Samuel's brother Frans); prorector of the Amsterdam Latin School; his annotated Latin Phaedrus edition (1701) is his masterpiece, his Dutch verse Phaedrus (1703) a bestseller; his 30-line laudatory poem opens Schouburgh vol. 1; his Groot woordenboek (1725-1733, completed by van Nidek) is the ten-volume dictionary of the Republic of Letters; 61 wedding poems make him one of the most productive occasional poets of the age. The doorway is the poet whose thirty lines open Arnold's book. FLAG: Samuel's nephew, not his son (see the Network section); the RKD page gives his dates as "(1658-1724)" without days. Giorgio Vasari (30 July 1511 - 27 June 1574; dates search-verified only) - spare 5: the root of the whole genre: painter, architect (the Uffizi, begun 1560; the corridor, 1565) and the inventor of the encyclopedia of artistic biographies -- Le Vite (1550; partly rewritten, enlarged and given woodcut portraits, some imaginary, in 1568), dedicated to Cosimo I de' Medici, with its treatise on technique and its Florentine bias; van Mander and Sandrart both built their Italian sections on him, and Houbraken's book is the third generation of his invention. He is a spare, not a main, because his link to Arnold runs through van Mander and Sandrart -- two removes -- and his mechanism (canon-making by biography) is the series' background, not its next step. The doorway is the man who invented the book Arnold spent his life continuing. NOTE: the 13 mentions mean a later leg must not mistake familiarity for study -- he has never been a study subject. Cornelis de Bie (10 February 1627 - c. 1712-1715; dates search-verified only) - spare 6: the Antwerp rhetorician who wrote the South's answer to van Mander. Het Gulden Cabinet (Antwerp, 1662), commissioned by the publisher Jan Meyssens as "the South Netherlandish counterpart of Karel van Mander's Schilder-Boeck": three parts, 95 full-page engraved portraits, biographies and panegyrics of 16th- and 17th-century artists, dedicated to the collector Antoon van Leyen; "the principal source of information for later art historians such as Arnold Houbraken and Jacob Campo Weyerman". A notary and rederijker who deemed it his duty to broadcast the fame of the artists -- the panegyric as a system, against Houbraken's anecdotal biography. The doorway is the Antwerp rhetorician whose Golden Cabinet Houbraken mined. Filippo Baldinucci (3 June 1625 - 10 January 1696; dates search-verified only) - spare 7: the Florentine who corrected Vasari with documents. Cardinal Leopoldo de' Medici's man, put in charge of the Medici drawing collection (the nucleus of the Uffizi's); Notizie de' professori del disegno (1681-1728, six volumes, the last three finished by his son Francesco Saverio), the first universal history of the figurative arts in Europe and "the first to make full use of documents"; the first history of engraving and the first dictionary of artistic terms (both 1686); dozens of Flemish and Dutch painters, including Rembrandt, judged worthy of inclusion. The doorway is the archivist who corrected Vasari with documents -- and wrote the first history of engraving. FLAG: death 1696, NOT 1697 (see the Network section); birth c. 1624 (Britannica, Columbia) vs 1625 (Uffizi, CalendarZ). Willem van Drielenburch (1632 Utrecht - c. 1687 Dordrecht; dates search-verified only) - spare 8: the rampjaar teacher. Bloemaert pupil, landscape painter in the manner of Jan Both; came to Dordrecht 1668; Arnold his pupil in 1672, the year of disaster, when the boy learned less painting than news -- his master "was constantly sending him out to hear the latest news". Also teacher of Wilhelmus Beurs. A small oeuvre and a thinner record than the other spares, but the anecdote is the mechanism: the pupil as messenger, the studio as a newsroom, the year the country nearly ended. The doorway is the teacher who sent his pupil out for the news in the year of disaster. Visscher (1587 - 19 June 1652), Nicolaes Visscher I (25 January 1618 - buried 11 September 1679), Samuel van Hoogstraten (1627-1678), Jacob Levecq (c.1634-1675), Aert Schouman (4 March 1710 - 5 July 1792), Bartholomeus van der Helst (1613 - buried 16 December 1670). George Vertue (1684 - 24 July 1756; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 123 lines) - the London engraver Houbraken superseded on the *Heads*. Apprenticed at 13, seven years under Michael Vandergucht, pupil of Thomas Gibson at Kneller's academy; Rose and Crown Club with Hogarth and Tillemans; official engraver to the Society of Antiquaries 1717, the only engraver made Fellow; ~500 portraits + ~500 antiquarian plates; *Twelve Heads of Poets* 1730; Rapin's *History of England* for the Knaptons 1736; his *Heads* contribution "submerged by the popularity of Houbraken's 'brilliant but less truthful productions'"; his forty volumes of notebooks, bought by Walpole, became *Anecdotes of Painting in England*. He is main 1 rather than top pick because he is the man Houbraken displaced, not the man Houbraken came from: the London half of the portrait trade, where Arnold is the Amsterdam root. The doorway is the London engraver whose plates Houbraken's brighter ones displaced. The parent confirms the promotion (see C561). Isaak Tirion (1705 in Utrecht - 1765 in Amsterdam; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 21 lines) - the publisher who turned Houbraken's faces into the national history. *Hedendaagsche historie* and *Vaderlandse Historie*, "illustrated with engravings by leading artists of Amsterdam"; moved to Amsterdam by May 1727; 1742 bought Wetstein's Kalverstraat house; *Het Verheerlykt Nederland* 1745-57, continued by his widow 1773-74. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the trade, not the hand: the man who commissioned the faces, where Arnold is the man who wrote the book that needed them. The doorway is the publisher who turned Houbraken's faces into the national history. He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the sequel, not the source: the man who continued Arnold's book, where Arnold is the man who wrote it. The doorway is the sequel-writer whose new Schouburgh Houbraken illustrated. Francois Morellon la Cave (15 April 1696 - 9 July 1768; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 39 lines) - the pupil who carried Picart's book-illustration trade into English history publishing. Huguenot engraver, settled The Hague; married Jeanne Bouffet 1722; engraving of Vivaldi 1725; illustrated Voltaire's Henriade and Tragedies, Garcilaso de la Vega's Comentarios Reales (Paris ed. 1731), Rapin de Thoyras's History of England vols I-X (1724-27); engravings after Hogarth and Coypel; portraits of Edward Pococke, Nader Shah, Prince William Duke of Cumberland, Willem de Fesch; active London c.1731-34 and c.1750; self-portrait 1765. He is main 1 rather than top pick because he is the pupil who left the workshop for London, not the collaborator who stayed in it: the trade carried outward, where Houbraken is the line carried forward. The doorway is the pupil who engraved Vivaldi and then illustrated the history of England. Gerard Hoet (22 August 1648 - 2 December 1733; dates from EN Wikipedia read end to end in leg 3, 185 lines) - the painter-engraver who designed the Bible Picart's shop engraved. 119 of the 214 picture-bible designs are his -- more than Picart's own 64; founded a drawing academy at Utrecht 1697 with Hendrick Schoock; published a book on drawing 1712 (103 prints by Pieter Bodart); source for Houbraken's Bentvueghels list; art dealer with Jacques Ignatius de Roore; trained by his father Moses Hoet and Warnard van Rijsen. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the designer behind the engraving, not the engraver: the man who drew the Bible and then taught a city to draw, where Houbraken is the man who engraved the faces. The doorway is the man who drew the Bible and then taught a city to draw. Etienne Picart le Romain (1632-1721; dates from search snippets only, not from a page read end to end) - the father, and the root of the line. Graveur du roi; Rome 1655-61 with Guillaume Vallet; pupil of Gilles Rousselet (not Francois de Poilly, per the British Museum); Academie Royale member 1664; last years from 1710 in the Netherlands, joining his son in Amsterdam; Bernard's first teacher. He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the root before the trunk, not the continuation after it: the king's engraver whose son taught Amsterdam to engrave, where Houbraken is the son who carried the Amsterdam line on. The doorway is the king's engraver whose son taught Amsterdam to engrave. Prosper Marchand (11 March 1678 - 14 June 1756; dates from search snippets only, not from a page read end to end) - the bookseller-bibliographer who fled Paris with Picart and became the book-trade half of his world. Paris shop rue Saint-Jacques under the Phenix (with Gabriel II Martin); The Hague refuge with Picart Jan 1710; abandoned bookselling c.1713; proofreader for Fritsch & Bohm, Rotterdam; editor Journal litteraire 1713-1737; Dictionnaire historique (1758-59, posthumous); London 1726-1735; left his papers to Leiden University Library; with Picart and pupil Jakob van der Schley arranged illustrations for numerous books, supplying descriptions and rough sketches. He is the reserve because he is the trade, not the hand: the man who sketched the pictures Picart engraved, but whose own drawn work is the description, not the plate. The doorway is the refugee bookseller who sketched the pictures Picart engraved. (The 11 names carried in #543 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Jurriaan Andriessen (1742-1819), Hermanus Numan (1744-1820), Jacques-Philippe Le Bas (1707-1783), Jacob Ernst Marcus (1774-1826) (mains); Izaak Jansz de Wit (1744-1809) (reserve); Hendrik Kobell (1751-1779), Jacques Kuyper (1761-1808) (alternates); spares: Pieter Barbiers II (1749-1842), Jacob Haafner (1754-1809), Jacob Otten Husly (16 November 1738 - 11 January 1796), Jan Bulthuis (1750-1801). Jurriaan Andriessen (1742-1819; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Brabant companion who painted the walls of Amsterdam houses and taught the next engraver in the chain. Wall-covering and theatre-decoration painter; travelled to Brabant with Vinkeles and Izaak Schmidt in 1765; subject, with Schmidt and Vinkeles himself, of the 1765 triple portrait; teacher of Hermanus Numan at the Stadstekenacademie in 1771. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the travelling companion, not the trunk: the painter beside the engraver on the road, whose wall-coverings are the large-scale cousin of Vinkeles's theatre sets. The doorway is the Brabant companion who painted the walls of Amsterdam houses -- and taught the next engraver in the chain. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; the 20 "Andriessen" log mentions are all Louis Andriessen (1939-2021), the composer, studied in the Rzewski chain -- never Jurriaan; no local file; not carried in #522-#543 as a subject. Dates search-verified only (Alamy/WGA captions). Hermanus Numan (1744-1820; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Paris meeting who became the copyist, theorist and publisher of the Amsterdam engraving line. Engraver, copyist, theorist and publisher; met Vinkeles in Paris in 1770; travelled to Paris in 1768, studied engraving there, then studied under Andriessen at the Stadstekenacademie in 1771; later Felix Meritis like Vinkeles and Vrijdag. He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the theorist of the line, not its trunk: the man who wrote about engraving as well as engraving, and whose career runs parallel to Vinkeles's from the same Paris meeting. The doorway is the Paris meeting who became the copyist, theorist and publisher of the Amsterdam engraving line. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#543 as a subject. Dates from the EN Wikipedia Hermanus Numan article, search-verified only (not read end to end in this run). FLAG: birth 1744 (EN Wikipedia lead) vs 1743 (KNAW, cited in the same article). Jacques-Philippe Le Bas (1707-1783; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Paris master whose dry needle Vinkeles crossed Europe to learn, and whose workshop was the largest in Paris. French engraver; head of the largest engraving workshop in Paris; teacher of Cochin, Cathelin, Strange and Ryland, as well as of Vinkeles for a year in 1770-71; Vinkeles lived in his house and learned his dry-needle technique there. He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is the French master behind the Dutch pupil: the technique itself, not the Amsterdam line -- and a study of him is a study of Paris, not of the chain that runs through Punt. The doorway is the Paris master whose dry needle Vinkeles crossed Europe to learn -- and whose workshop was the largest in Paris. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#543 as a subject. Dates from the EN Wikipedia Jacques-Philippe Le Bas article, search-verified only (not read end to end in this run). Jacob Ernst Marcus (1774-1826; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the pupil who took Vinkeles's late, loose manner and turned it into a school of his own. Engraver; "one of the few pupils Vinkeles accepted"; also trained in drawing by Steven Goble; gold medal at the Stadstekenacademie 1798; later professor/director of the Akademie van Beeldende Kunsten (1820); founder of the society Kunst Zij Ons Doel with Harmanus Fock and Harmanus Vinkeles; his 106-print Studiebeelden en Fragmenten (1807-16) is the next generation's drawing book; his 1812 etchings portray Jacobus Buys, Izaak Schmidt and Jurriaan Andriessen -- the Vinkeles circle, one generation on. He is main 5 rather than top pick because he is the continuation after Vinkeles, not the trunk before him: the pupil whose handling derives from Vinkeles's late manner, and the proof the school outlived the master. The doorway is the pupil who took Vinkeles's late, loose manner and turned it into a school of his own. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; 1 whole-log mention, in the #542 entry (the extra verified spares list), a carry, not a study; no local file; not carried in #522-#543 as a subject. NOTE: promoted from the #542 extra verified spares paragraph, where he was held back because no dates were established; leg 3 of this run found 1774-1826 (see C533). Izaak Jansz de Wit (1744-1809; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the namesake met in Paris, a different de Wit from the master of the ceilings. Painter; met Vinkeles in Paris in 1770. He is the reserve because he is a near-name collision with Jacob de Wit (study #541) and almost nothing is recorded beyond the Paris meeting -- but the meeting itself is dated and the confusion is worth one study to clear. The doorway is the namesake met in Paris -- a different de Wit from the master of the ceilings. Hendrik Kobell (1751-1779; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the marine etcher who died at twenty-eight, a fellow of the Pax circle. Marine painter and etcher; fellow member of Pax Artium Nutrix with Vinkeles; at the club he "became friendly with prominent collectors such as Jacob de Vos and Cornelis Ploos van Amstel". He is alternate 1 because his life is short and his oeuvre small, but his Pax membership and his marine etchings put him at the centre of the circle Vinkeles drank in. The doorway is the marine etcher who died at twenty-eight -- a fellow of the Pax circle. Jacques Kuyper (1761-1808; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the draughtsman behind the Felix Meritis hall, the drawing the old master engraved. Draughtsman; with Pieter Barbiers II he drew the Felix Meritis drawing hall that Vinkeles and Vrijdag engraved in 1801; also a Barbiers collaborator. He is alternate 2 because he is the designer behind the engraving, not the engraver: the man whose drawing of a room became Vinkeles's largest sheet. The doorway is the draughtsman behind the Felix Meritis hall -- the drawing the old master engraved. Theatre and decorative painter; son of Pieter Barbiers I (carried #542 spare); with Jacques Kuyper he drew the Felix Meritis drawing hall engraved by Vinkeles and Vrijdag in 1801. The doorway is the theatre painter's son who drew the academy hall with Kuyper. Jacob Haafner (1754-1809; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the workshop apprentice who became the travel writer whose Ceylon book Vinkeles illustrated at sixty-five. Writer, not artist; apprenticed in Vinkeles's workshop (introduced by the merchant J. Hilkes), then travelled to India and Ceylon; Vinkeles made the original drawings for the only two illustrations to his Lotgevallen (1806); called the European discovery of India "the biggest disaster this country has been able to face". The doorway is the workshop apprentice who became the travel writer whose Ceylon book Vinkeles illustrated at sixty-five. Jacob Otten Husly (16 November 1738 - 11 January 1796; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the architect who directed the drawing academy beside Vinkeles and built Felix Meritis. Architect; director of the Amsterdam Stadstekenacademie alongside Vinkeles; designed the Felix Meritis building on the Keizersgracht (with its drawing hall, engraved by Vinkeles in 1801), the Weesp City Hall and the Groningen City Hall; wrote a 1768 pamphlet on competitions for the city drawing academy. The doorway is the architect who directed the drawing academy beside Vinkeles -- and built Felix Meritis. Jan Bulthuis (1750-1801; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the draughtsman whose procession Vinkeles engraved, and whose views fill the Rijksmuseum. Draughtsman and engraver; designed the Procession of the Three Estates (W17) and the Vincennes attack (W18) that Vinkeles and Vrijdag engraved for the Tafereelen; topographical views of Dutch towns (Diemen 1789, Zoutelande 1792, Rotterdam 1790). The doorway is the draughtsman whose procession Vinkeles engraved -- and whose views fill the Rijksmuseum. (The 13 names carried in #542 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Cornelis Ploos van Amstel (4 January 1726 - 20 December 1798) (mains); Louis Fabritius Dubourg (bapt. 5 July 1693 - buried 22 September 1775) (reserve); Simon Fokke (1712-1784), Jakob van der Schley (26 July 1715 - 7 February 1779) (alternates); spares: Jan Caspar Philips (1690-1775), Pieter Barbiers I (bapt. 23 April 1717 - 7 September 1780), Jan Baptist Xavery (Antwerp 1697 - 1742, The Hague), Gerard Joseph Xavery (27 July 1700 - after 1747), Jacob Folkema (1692-1767), Jan de Beijer (24 September 1703 - c.1780), Jacob van Huysum (bapt. 25 February 1688 - 1740), Caspar Jacobsz Philips (1732-1789). Cornelis Ploos van Amstel (4 January 1726 - 20 December 1798) - the lumber merchant who married Troost's daughter, collected five thousand drawings, and invented a way to print them a century before photography. Pupil of Norbert van Bloemen and of Jacobus Buys (carried #541 main); married Elisabeth Troost, daughter of Cornelis Troost (carried #541 main); a lumber merchant by trade and a collector of 5,000+ drawings including Rembrandts. He invented the prenttekening -- a transfer/offset method of reproducing drawings in the crayon manner, from 1765; his Hoofdwerk ran to 46 prints at 350 impressions each. His collection sold in 1800 for 109,406 guilders. His pupils included Christina Chalon, William V Prince of Orange, Elisabeth van Woensel and Hermanus Petrus Schouten; he reported Petrus Camper's facial-angle findings; a member of Arte et Amicitia. He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is the collector's answer to the engraver's question: not the man who made the prints, but the man who figured out how to print the drawings themselves, and the proof that the print trade had a second half Punt never touched. The doorway is the lumber merchant who married Troost's daughter, collected five thousand drawings, and invented a way to print them a century before photography. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#542 as a subject. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. He appears in the #541 file only as network/provenance (leg-2 note, "ex Cornelis Ploos van Amstel collection"; patrons entry 27), never as a candidate. FLAG: one source gives "Print made by: Cornelis Ploos van Amstel in 1559-1760" -- an obvious typo for 1759-1760. Louis Fabritius Dubourg (bapt. 5 July 1693 - buried 22 September 1775) - the history painter who swapped the brush for the burin and taught the academy circle to etch. History painter who became an engraver; pupil of Gerard de Lairesse (carried #529 spare), Gerrit Rademaker (1672-1711) and Jacob van Huysum (proposed spare 7); sexton of the Amstelkerk from 1718; cooperated with Picart in 1729; made the title plate for van Gool's Nieuw Schouburg (1750); a central figure of the Stadstekenacademie circle (Folkema, Morellon, Troost, Fokke, Elgersma, Tanje); works at the Westerkerk, Nieuwe Kerk and Town Hall; "exchanged brush for graver"; his collection was auctioned in 1776; Ploos ranked him with Isaac de Moucheron, de Wit and Troost. He is the reserve because he is the hinge between the painter's trade and the engraver's: the man who made the same crossing Punt made in reverse (Punt went from needle to brush late; Dubourg went from brush to graver), but his own engraved oeuvre is thin and his dates rest on one article. The doorway is the history painter who swapped the brush for the burin and taught the academy circle to etch. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#542 as a subject. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Simon Fokke (1712-1784) - the booksellers' engraver who put the whole Dutch history into small plates. Pupil of J. C. Philips (proposed spare 1); the booksellers' engraver of small portraits and vignettes; plates for Wagenaar's Vaderlandsche Historie 1749-59; Danish portraits for Tycho Hofman (1741); after Vernet, Avercamp, Terborch, Ribera and Troost (a burlesque Death of Dido); the title vignette for Rousseau's Discourse on Inequality (1755); his portrait was painted by John Greenwood after Jacobus Buys. The doorway is the booksellers' engraver who put the whole Dutch history into small plates. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#542 as a subject. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Jakob van der Schley (26 July 1715 - 7 February 1779) - the boy who finished his dead master's plates and then mapped the world for the book trade. Pupil of Picart from about age 12; completed unfinished plates after Picart's death; made the memorial portrait of Picart (1734); maps for Prevost's Histoire generale des voyages (publisher Pieter de Hondt, The Hague); illustrations for La Vie de Marianne (1735-47); Brantome frontispieces (1740); L'Imprimerie (1739); taught Philippus van der Schley (1724-1817); cooperated with Prosper Marchand. The doorway is the boy who finished his dead master's plates and then mapped the world for the book trade. German engraver settled in Amsterdam; teacher of Simon Fokke (proposed alternate 1) and of his nephew Caspar Philips (proposed spare 8); plates for Wagenaar's Hedendaagse Historie; 1743 portraits of Mennonite ministers for Kornelis de Wit; Sinterklaasavond. The doorway is the German engraver who taught the booksellers' engraver -- and drew Sinterklaas. Jacobus Buys (19 November 1724 - 7 April 1801; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the wig-maker's son who studied under all three of Amsterdam's masters and ended up running its drawing academy. Painter and engraver; studied under Cornelis Pronk, Jacob de Wit and Cornelis Troost; director of the Amsterdam Drawing Academy; joined the Guild of St Luke in 1750; bas-reliefs, tapestry designs, book illustrations and theatre scenes. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the pupil line after the engraver: the man who learned the witje, the flower piece and the theatre scene from three different masters and turned them into a teaching post. The doorway is the wig-maker's son who studied under all three of Amsterdam's masters and ended up running its drawing academy. Tako Hajo Jelgersma (29 October 1702 - 24 March 1795; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the follower who painted de Wit's grey stone for another forty years, and outlived him by forty-one. Painter; de Wit's follower in grisaille; pupil of Frans Decker and Wigerus Vitringa; moved to Haarlem in 1752; pupils Johan Bernard Brandhoff, Johannes Petrus van Horstok, Cornelis van Noorde, Martinus van der Jagt and Johannes Swertner; lived to 92. He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the influence line itself, narrow and documented: the only named man who kept painting grisailles in de Wit's manner after de Wit was gone, and the proof that the witje outlived its inventor. The doorway is the follower who painted de Wit's grey stone for another forty years -- and outlived him by forty-one. (The 14 names carried in #540 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Johann Heinrich Roos (29 September 1631, Otterberg - 3 October 1685, Frankfurt), Thomas Heeremans (bapt. 29 May 1641, Haarlem - bur. 24 January 1694, Haarlem), Ludolf Bakhuizen (28 December 1630 or 1632, Emden - 7 November 1708, Amsterdam) (mains); Jacob Koninck (c. 1615, Amsterdam - c. 1695) (reserve); Jacob Coning (Jacob Koninck II; c. 1647, The Hague - 16 July 1724, Copenhagen), Klaes Molenaer (Nicolaes Molenaer; 1626-1629, Haarlem - 1676, Haarlem) (alternates); spares: Aernout Smit (1641, Amsterdam - 1710), Michiel Maddersteg (bapt. 5 January 1662, Enkhuizen - d. 1708, Berlin), Jan Claesz Rietschoof (21 May 1652, Hoorn - 3 November 1719, Hoorn), Ferdinand Kobell (7 June 1740, Mannheim - 1 February 1799, Munich), Franz Kobell (23 November 1749, Mannheim - 14 January 1822, Munich), Johann Georg von Dillis (26 December 1759, Gmain - 28 September 1841, Munich), Willem Romeyn (also spelled Romeijn; c. 1624, Haarlem - after 1693), Isaac de Jouderville (1612, Leiden - 1645, Amsterdam). Johann Heinrich Roos (29 September 1631, Otterberg - 3 October 1685, Frankfurt; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the German who painted Italian shepherds he never saw, and died in the fire that took them. German Baroque landscape and animal painter; family fled to Amsterdam in the Thirty Years' War (1640); trained with Guilliam du Gardijn, Cornelis de Bie and Barent Graat, but Berchem and Dujardin shaped him; worked for Ernst, Landgrave of Hesse-Rheinfels 1654-1659; court painter to Charles I Louis, Elector Palatine, 1664; moved to Frankfurt 1667; died in a house fire in 1685 trying to save his belongings. Specialised in pastoral idylls - idealized landscapes with ancient ruins, shepherds, sheep and cattle, the longing for harmony between men and animals and nature; never went to Italy, built his ruins from engravings; also one of the finest German portraitists of his time; many individual animal drawings (Albertina), intensifying the individuality of each species; Goethe called him the "Raphael of all livestock painters"; sons Philipp Peter Roos (Rosa di Tivoli, 1655-1706) and Johann Melchior Roos (1663-1731) were also painters. He is main 2 rather than top pick because he is the German pastoral before Kobell, the model Govert van der Leeuw painted "in the manner of" and one of the masters Kobell engraved after - the animal piece as an ideal, not a copy. The doorway is the German who painted Italian shepherds he never saw, and died in the fire that took them. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions as a study subject; no local file; not carried in #522-#539 as a subject. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. Thomas Heeremans (bapt. 29 May 1641, Haarlem - bur. 24 January 1694, Haarlem; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the winter painter who signed his name so tightly that dealers invented a man called Mans. Dutch painter and art dealer; winter landscapes, cityscapes, harbour scenes, beach views, river views, village scenes; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1664; may have been a pupil of Caesar van Everdingen (no influence of Everdingen visible - Heeremans was purely a landscape painter, not a figure painter); style shaped by Klaes Molenaer, but brighter in palette and livelier in movement, villagers skating, sledging, fishing and talking; frequently returned to Scheveningen beach and Egmond village, beach and ruins; pictures often go in pairs; collaborated with specialist figure painters - Abraham Storck painted the figures in Winter landscape with the Montelbaanstoren, Amsterdam; signed "THMANS" with the letters run together, so dealers long misread him as "Frederik Hendrik Mans". He is main 3 rather than top pick because he is the staffage problem from the other side: a landscapist who hired the figures in, the mirror of van de Velde the hired staffage hand. The doorway is the winter painter who signed his name so tightly that dealers invented a man called Mans. Dates from the EN Wikipedia article read end to end in leg 3. FLAG: the page says earliest dated work 1660 and last dated work 1695, against burial 24 January 1694 - the 1695 date cannot stand as painted in his lifetime; also the 1659 "pupil of an Everdingen" record is assumed to be Caesar van Everdingen, not proven. Ludolf Bakhuizen (28 December 1630 or 1632, Emden - 7 November 1708, Amsterdam; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the marine painter who took the sea when the van de Veldes left. German-born Dutch painter, draughtsman, calligrapher and printmaker, the leading Dutch marine painter after both Willem van de Veldes left for England in 1672. He came to Amsterdam around 1650 as a merchant's clerk, taught himself to draw ships, studied under Allaert van Everdingen and Hendrick Dubbels, joined the painters' guild in 1663, and became famous for violent water - he went out to sea in an open boat to study storms. He painted the great View of Amsterdam and the IJ (1665) as a present for Louis XIV's minister, opened a gallery on the top floor of the Amsterdam town hall in 1699, and was visited by Cosimo III de' Medici and Peter the Great. His pupils and followers include Jan Claesz Rietschoof, Aernout Smit, Michiel Maddersteg and Abraham Storck. He is main 4 rather than top pick because he is the hub of the whole marine spare set - three of this study's spares are his pupils or followers - so studying him unlocks all of them, and his open-boat storm study is a mechanism no other candidate offers. The doorway is the clerk-turned-calligrapher who rowed out into storms in an open boat to learn how water breaks, and then taught half of Amsterdam's marine painters. Burial: Wikipedia gives 12 November 1708 at Westerkerk; BADA gives buried 17 November 1708. Death 7 November 1708 is consistent across sources. Jacob Koninck (c. 1615, Amsterdam - c. 1695; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the teacher whose perspective books made his pupil better than him. Dutch Golden Age landscape painter; lent his perspective books to Johannes Verkolje, who became better at perspective drawing than he was; teacher of his son Jacob II (Coning) and his younger brother Philips Koninck (carried #537); uncle of Salomon Koninck; in Dordrecht 1633-1636, Rotterdam 1637-1645, The Hague 1647-1651, Amsterdam 1658, Copenhagen by c.1676, painter to the court of Christian V of Denmark. He is the reserve because he is the hinge of the whole Koninck family and a second van de Velde disciple whose line runs to Copenhagen, but his own oeuvre is thin and his dates are the shakiest in this block. The doorway is the teacher whose perspective books made his pupil better than him. Jacob Coning (Jacob Koninck II; c. 1647, The Hague - 16 July 1724, Copenhagen; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the fellow pupil who took van de Velde's landscape to a Danish court and died in the poorhouse. Dutch-Danish painter to the Danish Court; son of Jacob Koninck; trained under his father and Adriaen van de Velde - a fellow pupil of van Bergen by the same master, and the #538 pupil list's fourth name; Copenhagen 1676, court work from 1680 (contact possibly through his wife, who worked for Queen Charlotte Amalie); one of the first painters in Denmark-Norway to specialise in topographical painting; Norway 1698-1699 for Frederiksberg Palace; with Thomas Quellinus, Hendrik Krock and others proposed an art academy in Copenhagen on 6 October 1701; also a portraitist to Danish and Norwegian nobility; despite court contact he died in the poorhouse of the Reformed Church in Store Kongensgade after repeated forced auctions. The doorway is the fellow pupil who took van de Velde's landscape to a Danish court and died in the poorhouse. Klaes Molenaer (Nicolaes Molenaer; 1626-1629, Haarlem - 1676, Haarlem; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the Haarlem winter painter whose birth was never written down because his family was Catholic. Dutch Golden Age landscape painter and draughtsman; son of tailor Jan Mienssen Molenaer, brother of Bartholomeus and Jan Miense Molenaer (carried #535 main); Roman Catholic family, so no baptismal register and no exact birth date; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1651, dues paid yearly until 1676; winter landscape painter influenced by Jacob van Ruisdael; frozen rivers teeming with skaters, sleds, horse-drawn sleighs, a church and an inn under low winter sun; taught by the little-known Nicolaes Piemont; earliest dated work 1644; works in the Rijksmuseum, Boijmans Van Beuningen, Schwerin, Braunschweig. The doorway is the Haarlem winter painter whose birth was never written down because his family was Catholic. Dutch Golden Age seascape painter; pupil of Abraham Liedts, then of Ludolf Bakhuizen; active in Hoorn all his career, possibly some time in Amsterdam with Bakhuizen; taught his son Hendrik; one of Bakhuizen's most accomplished pupils (Sotheby's, on a stormy sea after Bakhuizen's Fishing Vessels Offshore in a Heavy Sea, 1684, completed 1685); works in the Rijksmuseum, Het Scheepvaartmuseum, Westfries Museum Hoorn, the Hermitage, the National Maritime Museum Greenwich; Hoorn contemporary of the flower painter Jacob Rootius, son of Jan Albertsz Rotius. Ferdinand Kobell (7 June 1740, Mannheim - 1 February 1799, Munich; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the law student who became the etcher of the Dutch landscape in Germany. German painter and copper engraver; grandson of Johann Heinrich Kobell, grocer of Frankfurt who settled in Mannheim in 1720; studied law at Heidelberg, then painting under Peter Verschatfelt; court painter 1766; Paris 1768-1769 under Johann Georg Wille; followed Elector Charles Theodore to Munich 1794; named Director of the Palatinate Painting Gallery 1798 but died before taking up the post; landscapes in the style of Berchem; about 300 etchings, published as Oeuvres completes (1809), marking a distinct advance in German landscape etching; father of Wilhelm, brother of Franz. The doorway is the law student who became the etcher of the Dutch landscape in Germany. Franz Kobell (23 November 1749, Mannheim - 14 January 1822, Munich; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the uncle who drew twenty thousand landscapes because paint was too slow. German painter, etcher and draftsman; brother of Ferdinand, uncle and teacher of Wilhelm; sent to Italy by Elector Karl Theodor 1776, in Rome until 1785, working from nature and monumental buildings; court painter at Munich; produced only a few oil paintings, highly praised by Goethe (Rocky Landscape with Waterfalls, once in the Bamberg gallery), but left 20,000 landscape and architecture pen drawings and etchings, painting being too slow to keep pace with his ideas. The doorway is the uncle who drew twenty thousand landscapes because paint was too slow. Johann Georg von Dillis (26 December 1759, Gmain - 28 September 1841, Munich; dates search-verified only, not read end to end) - the professor Kobell replaced, who taught Munich to paint what it saw. German landscape painter, pioneer of Naturalism; trained under Johann Jakob Dorner the elder and at the Munich Zeichnungsakademie; inspector of the Hofgarten Galerie 1790; professor of landscape painting at the Munich Academy 1808-1814, replaced by Wilhelm von Kobell in 1813/1814; central gallery director 1822, founding director of what became the Alte Pinakothek; mentor of Carl Rottmann and Ludwig Richter; insisted on Naturstudium, direct study of nature, against the history-painting hierarchy. The doorway is the professor Kobell replaced, who taught Munich to paint what it saw. Willem Romeyn (also spelled Romeijn; c. 1624, Haarlem - after 1693; dates search-verified only, not read end to end) - the Berchem pupil whose pastures Kobell was still copying two centuries later. Dutch Italianate landscape painter and printmaker; pupil of Nicolaes Berchem around 1642; Haarlem Guild of St Luke 1646; sun-drenched southern landscapes with shepherds, cattle, sheep and goats resting by rivers, long shadows, hazy golden light; last dated works 1694; Kobell etched after him (The Shepherd's Rest, 1819, after Romeyn). The doorway is the Berchem pupil whose pastures Kobell was still copying two centuries later. Isaac de Jouderville (1612, Leiden - 1645, Amsterdam; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the orphan pupil whose tronies were filed under Rembrandt for centuries. Dutch Golden Age painter, pupil of Rembrandt; orphan, parents from Metz; apprenticed to Rembrandt from 1 November 1629, six signed receipts for 1630-1631, travelled with him to Amsterdam in 1631; enrolled at Leiden University April 1632; married Maria le Febure 1636; Deventer 1641; back in Amsterdam 1643, died young in 1645; his widow married glassmaker Pieter de Melder in 1648, and his daughter Mariecke later married Frederik de Moucheron - the Jouderville line marries straight into the Moucheron line; painted mainly Rembrandtesque heads or tronies, so faithful that several of his paintings were previously attributed to Rembrandt; Man in Oriental Costume was the Fake or Fortune? case (BBC, 2011), a Nazi-seized Oppenheimer stock picture. The doorway is the orphan pupil whose tronies were filed under Rembrandt for centuries. Extra verified spares (not part of the 8 above, all verified NOT STUDIED): Jan Baptist Boel the Younger (between September and 4 October 1643, Antwerp - 9 January 1689; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S22). Son and pupil of Pieter Boel; family moved to Paris 1668 for the Gobelins; married Maria Catharina Immenraet, daughter of landscape painter Philips Augustijn Immenraet, 4 May 1675; collaborated with other Antwerp painters, adding the still-life and animal elements to their compositions; known oeuvre very limited; assimilated his father's style so completely that attribution problems follow (Nationalmuseum). Held back because his oeuvre is small and mostly indistinguishable from his father's. VERIFIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#539. FLAG: the Wikipedia lead says 9 January 1689, the body says 1 January 1689 - an internal conflict in the same article. Jan Theunisz Blanckerhoff (also Blankerhoff/Blankhof/Jan Maat; 10 January 1628, Alkmaar - bur. 2 October 1669, Amsterdam; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S26). Marine painter; taught by Arent Teerling, Pieter Scheyenburg and Caesar van Everdingen; served on a Dutch warship; Rome, Bentvueghels, called Jan Maat; Italian ports with strange boats his most popular pieces; teacher of Aernout Smit; Battle of the Zuiderzee 1663-1668, 700 guilders, Rijksmuseum SK-A-3235. Held back because his career is short (work period 1649-1669) and his record is thin. Daniel de Koninck (1668, Amsterdam - 1730, England; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S27). Nephew of Philips Koninck; apprenticed to his uncle Jacob Koninck at the Copenhagen court 1687-1690; moved to England 1690 (Oxford, then London by 1720); genre works and portraits/tronies in the manner of Rembrandt; Peter King, 1st Baron King of Ockham (1720, National Portrait Gallery). Held back because he is a portraitist, off the landscape line. VERIFIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#539. Hendrik Kobell (13 September 1751, Rotterdam - 3 August 1779, Rotterdam; dates search-verified only, from EN Wikipedia via search S29). Dutch landscape and marine painter, etcher, draftsman, watercolourist; cousin of Ferdinand and Franz Kobell; father trained him in drawing, he loved drawing ships in Rotterdam port; London 1769-1770, Paris 1772, Amsterdam Stadstekenakademie; founder of the Rotterdam drawing society Hierdoor tot Hooger; father and teacher of Jan Kobell II (1778-1814); pupils Carel Frederik Bendorp and Gerrit van der Pals; The Shipwreck (1775, Rijksmuseum SK-A-1218). Held back because he died at 27 and the record is short. VERIFIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#539. Dominique Michael Serres (also Dominic Michael Serres; c. 1763 - 1816; dates search-verified only, from the Government Art Collection). Younger son of Dominic Serres; landscape painter (the only Serres who left the sea); served in the Royal Navy on HMS Foudroyant 1782; married Lucretia Madden 4 August 1788; prisoner of the King's Bench Prison by December 1813; died 1816; View of the Teign, Devon (GAC). Held back because his surviving work is small and mostly watercolour landscape. VERIFIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #522-#539.
