(The 13 names carried in #531 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Cornelis Bloemaert II (1603 - 28 September 1692; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - second son, painter turned engraver; studied with his father, his brothers Hendrick and Adriaen, and Gerard van Honthorst; learned printmaking from Crispijn van de Passe the Elder. Paris 1630 (engravings for Michel de Marolles's Temple des Muses), Rome from 1633 until his death - 59 years, most of his career. Bentvueghels bent-name "Winter". His Roman workshop attracted young engravers from abroad and spread Italian art through Europe; best-known engravings after Annibale Carracci (The Holy Family), Pietro da Cortona (Adoration of the Shepherds) and Rubens (Meleager); 1664-77 he worked on the engraved record of Cortona's Palazzo Pitti frescoes. Pupils Michel Natalis and Gilles Rousselet. He delayed returning to see his dying father so long that his father died - he stayed in Rome. The doorway is the son who went: the father never went to Italy and learned Caravaggism second-hand - now follow the son who did go, stayed 59 years, and engraved Italy for the rest of Europe. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Death date flagged as disputed: Wikipedia gives 28 September 1692; the NGA gives "in or after 1684" and Te Papa "c. 1684"; an older cyclopedia says 1680. 1692 is the commonly cited date. Leonaert Bramer (24 December 1596 - buried 10 February 1674; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Delft painter of genre, religious and history scenes, listed by the RKD among Bloemaert's pupils. At 18 he left on a long trip reaching Rome in 1616 via Arras, Amiens, Paris, Aix, Marseille, Genoa and Livorno; in Rome he was a founder of the Bentvueghels (bent-name "Nestelg...") and lived with Wouter Crabeth, once got into a fight with Claude Lorrain, and acquired the sobriquet "Leonardo della Notte" - Leonardo of the Night - for his nocturnes. He met Wybrand de Geest in Aix-en-Provence in 1616. Back in Delft by 1628, Guild of St. Luke 1629; painted frescoes, a rarity north of the Alps (not survived), and murals on canvas. A lifelong bachelor, productive until his death at 77; it is possible, though undocumented, that Vermeer trained with him. One of the most intriguing personalities in 17th-century Dutch art. The doorway is the Delft pupil who ran off to Rome at eighteen, helped found the Bentvueghels, fought with Claude Lorrain, and came home to paint the night. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Jan Baptist Weenix (1621-1659; Leiden Collection gives 1660/61 - death date disputed, flag it; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - painter of the Dutch Golden Age, Bloemaert pupil (after Jan Micker, before Claes Moeyaert). Born near Amsterdam harbour, son of an architect; travelled to Italy 1643-47, joined the Bentvueghels as "Ratel" (rattle, for a speech defect), worked for Cardinal Camillo Pamphili (later Pope Innocent X), painted a portrait of Rene Descartes, and in 1649 became master of the Utrecht Guild of St. Luke alongside Jan Both as officer. Mainly responsible for introducing the Italian harbour scene into Dutch art - mid-size paintings with a group of figures in the foreground - alongside Italian landscapes with large figures among ruins, seaside views, and late still lifes of dead game and dogs. With Berchem and Jan Both, a leader of the second generation of Dutch Italianate painters; teacher of his son Jan Weenix and nephew Melchior d'Hondecoeter. Died at 39 at Huis ter Mey, Vleuten. The doorway is the Utrecht pupil who took the master's Italianate landscape and turned it into the Dutch harbour scene - and died at thirty-nine, leaving a son who became the great still-life painter. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. (Mains 2, 3 and 4; main 1 is the top pick above.) Reserve: Wybrand Simonsz. de Geest (16 August 1592 - c. 1661; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Frisian portrait painter, the most important portraitist of Friesland. Learned first from his father Simon Juckesz, a stained-glass worker, then studied with Bloemaert in Utrecht. Grand Tour 1614-18 through France and Italy; met Leonaert Bramer in Aix-en-Provence in 1616; in Rome joined the Bentvueghels as "De Friesche Adelaar" (the Frisian Eagle) and kept an album amicorum (friendship book) from 1611, 22 drawings from which are still missing. Returned to Leeuwarden c. 1620 and became the leading portrait painter of the Frisian nobility and the Orange-Nassau family; married Hendrickje Fransdr Uylenburgh in 1622, niece of Saskia van Uylenburgh - Rembrandt's wife - linking him by marriage to Rembrandt's circle; influenced Jacob Adriaensz Backer; students Jan Jansz. de Stomme and Jacob Potma; sons Julius and Frank also became painters. The doorway is the Frisian pupil who carried Utrecht portraiture north to Leeuwarden, married into Rembrandt's family, and signed his Roman years "the Frisian Eagle". VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Alternates if any of the 5 fails later (all verified NOT STUDIED): Jacob Gerritsz. Cuyp (1594-1651/52; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dordrecht portrait and landscape painter, RKD-listed pupil of Bloemaert; son of stained-glass designer Gerrit Gerritsz. Cuyp, half-brother of Benjamin Gerritsz. Cuyp, father of the far more famous Aelbert Cuyp. Joined the Dordrecht Guild of St. Luke 18 July 1617, treasurer 1629/33/37/41; helped found the Dordrecht painters' breakaway guild in 1642 and is called the nestor of the Dordrecht painters. Best known today for portraits - especially fine portraits of children - and pastoral landscapes; early work shows distinct Caravaggesque influence from his Utrecht training. Teacher of Ferdinand Bol, Aelbert Cuyp, Benjamin Cuyp, Paulus Lesire, Aert van der Neer and Pieter Hermansz Verelst; Jacob's recommendation is said to have sent Nicolaes Maes and Ferdinand Bol to Rembrandt. The doorway is the Utrecht pupil who went home to Dordrecht, founded its painting school, and taught the father of the golden light - Aelbert Cuyp's father, and Rembrandt's Bol. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Birth year flagged: 1594 is the current consensus against 1575 in the 9th edition of the Britannica - the old date is wrong, use 1594. Nicolaus Knupfer (1609 - 15 October 1655; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dutch Golden Age painter, born Leipzig, apprenticed there to Emanuel Nysen, then Magdeburg making brushes for artists until 1630, when he moved to Utrecht to work with Bloemaert - living with him for two years - before setting up his own Utrecht studio (visiting member of the Guild of St. Luke, 1637). Painted small-scale biblical, literary and mythological scenes and popular genre works; worked on the decorations of Kronborg Castle in Denmark; painted the figures in the landscapes of Jan Both and Jan Baptist Weenix - the staffage hand inside two other Bloemaert pupils' pictures. A successful teacher: his students included Jan Steen, Gabriel Metsu, Ary de Vois and Pieter Crijnse Volmarijn. The doorway is the Leipzig brush-maker who lived two years in the master's house, then painted the little figures inside Jan Both's and Weenix's landscapes - and taught Jan Steen. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Spares (verified, NOT part of the 7): Hieronymus Francken I (c. 1540-1610; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Flemish painter, pupil of Frans Floris, worked at Fontainebleau 1566-72, court painter to the French court from 1594; Bloemaert's Paris/Fontainebleau master 1581-83. Elegant Mannerist groups of dancers, musicians and courtiers, uniting Antwerp, Venetian and French elements. Brother of Frans Francken I and Ambrosius Francken I; father of a painting dynasty. The doorway is the Fontainebleau master who gave the boy from Gorinchem the only real training he ever got. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Adriaen Bloemaert (after 1609 - buried 8 January 1666; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - fourth son, first instruction from his father; Italy until 1637, then Vienna, Salzburg (employed there), Freistadt 1640, back in Utrecht by 1651; painted history and portraits "with some success". THIN RECORD WARNING: few works securely attributed; most biographical detail comes from Houbraken/Bryan via the RKD. The doorway is the youngest-but-one son who took the family trade to Vienna and Salzburg and came home to die in Utrecht. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Frederick Bloemaert (c. 1614/17-1690; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - youngest artist son, engraver; published his father's Artes Apellae liber (1650) - the drawing book that taught Utrecht to draw into the 19th century. Dates as commonly cited (c. 1614/17) - flag as approximate. THIN RECORD WARNING: known chiefly as his father's publisher/engraver rather than for independent work. The doorway is the son who turned his father's drawings into the textbook every Utrecht artist learned from. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Andries Both (1612/13 - 23 March 1642; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - elder brother of Jan Both, Bloemaert pupil; Rouen 1633, Rome from 1635, shared a studio with Jan van Causteren, then lived with his brother on the Via Vittoria from 1638; a leading bambocciante - low-life Roman genre scenes after Pieter van Laer, influenced by Brueghel and Adriaen Brouwer - while his brother painted idyllic landscapes. Drowned in a Venetian canal in 1642, aged 29 or 30, on the way home. The doorway is the elder Both brother, who painted Rome's low life while his brother painted its golden light - and drowned on the way home. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Cornelis de Beer (dates unknown) - RKD-listed Bloemaert pupil; son of Joos de Beer (Bloemaert's own second teacher) - the teacher's son becoming the pupil's pupil. THIN RECORD WARNING: no dates or secure works surfaced in this leg's searches; propose only if a deeper dig finds material. The doorway is the son of Bloemaert's own teacher, who came back to learn from the boy his father once taught. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#530. Gerrit Splinter (dates unknown) and Joos de Beer (active 1575-91) - Bloemaert's first two Utrecht teachers - Splinter the drunken dauber who lasted two weeks, de Beer "not the best of painters". THIN RECORD WARNING: both are known almost entirely through van Mander's anecdotes; no independent oeuvre to study. List as a pair only; either alone is too thin for a full study. The doorway is the two bad masters - the drunk and the mediocrity - whose failure sent a fifteen-year-old to Paris. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions each; no local files; not carried in #520-#530.)

(The 23 names carried in #530 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Jusepe de Ribera (Xativa, baptised 17 February 1591 - Naples 1652; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Spanish painter, "Lo Spagnoletto", the chief visual source for Baburen's saints. Cussida commissioned Ribera's Five Senses in 1615-16 while he was commissioning Baburen's Pieta Chapel, and Franits makes Ribera the chief visual source for Baburen's Archimedes, St. Francis and Pan Holding a Syrinx - the hard, sculptural, plebeian body that Baburen brought home to Utrecht. Ribera then did what Baburen never lived to do: he stayed, married into Neapolitan painting, ran the city for thirty years under the viceroys, etched forty plates, and pushed tenebrism to its most brutal extreme in martyrdoms and flayings. The doorway is the Spaniard who shared Baburen's patron and taught him his saints. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529. WARNING: death date disputed (2 September vs 3 November 1652); Ribalta pupillage undocumented, Giovanni Serodine (Ascona 1600 - Rome 21 December 1630; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Ticinese Caravaggist in Rome. Serodine's Saint Michael was painted for San Pietro in Montorio, the same church where Baburen and de Haen decorated the Pieta Chapel, and his Christ among the Doctors (Louvre) and Jesus and the Tribute Money (National Gallery of Scotland) are the most violently brushed tenebrist canvases of the 1620s Roman scene. He was the son of Ticinese stucco workers, trained by his elder brother Giacomo, made few friends and patrons in Rome (Baglione), produced only about fifteen surviving paintings, and died at 30 - the same short fuse as Baburen, the same city, the same decade, the opposite temperament: loose, luminous, provincial, eccentric where Baburen is monumental and composed. Revived by Roberto Longhi; a 2022 dossier exhibition in Modena centred on his Santo scrivente. The doorway is the Caravaggist Baburen never met but shared a church with. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529, Johannes Vermeer (Delft, baptised 31 October 1632 - 15 December 1675; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dutch painter, the long echo of the Procuress. Baburen's Procuress hung in Maria Thins's house, and Vermeer painted it into the background of The Concert (c. 1664) and Lady Seated at a Virginal (c. 1670), and may have taken his own Procuress (1656) from it - the one object, the Essential Vermeer site notes, that materially links us to Vermeer's world besides his own 37 paintings. A Vermeer study after Baburen is the long echo: the brothel scene Baburen staged as a loud, cropped, candlelit transaction becomes, forty years later, a small framed picture on a quiet wall, its moral noise turned into pictorial meaning. The doorway is the painting on the wall. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; one whole-log mention only, inside the Baburen material; no local file; not carried in #520-#529. NOTE FOR THE PARENT: Vermeer is the biggest name in this list; hold or promote him at the parent's discretion. Reserve: Bartolomeo Cavarozzi (Viterbo, born 15 February 1587 - Rome 21 September 1625; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Italian Caravaggist who carried the style to Spain the way Baburen carried it to Utrecht. Taken into the Palazzo Crescenzi before 1617 by his patron Giovanni Battista Crescenzi, he travelled with him to Madrid in 1617, worked on the Escorial, and returned to Rome in 1619 - the exact years Baburen was painting the Pieta Chapel a few streets away. His work is the lyrical, still-life-haunted side of the movement (Virgin and Child, Sacrifice of Isaac, Saint Jerome with Two Angels, 1617), recently revalued after decades of neglect; Gianni Papi's 2015 monograph is the first and only one as of 2019. The doorway is the Italian who carried Caravaggism to Spain the way Baburen carried it to Utrecht. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529. WARNING: birth year sometimes given as 1586; the Guercino-training claim is an error (Cavarozzi was four years older than Guercino). Alternates if any of the 4 fails later (all verified NOT STUDIED): Herman van Swanevelt (Woerden 1603 - Paris 1655; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dutch pastoral landscapist, Bentvueghel (bent-name "heremiet", the hermit) who reached Rome in 1629. Baburen's Granida and Daifilo (1623) was the first painting of Hooft's play and started the Dutch pastoral vogue; Swanevelt made the pastoral landscape itself the subject - sunlit contrejours, shepherds at a ford, no biblical or mythological excuse at all - and with Pieter van Laer and Claude Lorrain brought the Italianate landscape into its classical phase. He was Claude's lodger-companion and model, and the intermediary through whom Claude's art reached the north. The doorway is the pastoral after Baburen's pastoral. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529. WARNING: teacher uncertain (a new hypothesis says Willem Buytewech), Claude Lorrain, born Claude Gellee (Chamagne, Duchy of Lorraine, c. 1600 or 1604/5 - Rome 23 November 1682; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - French landscapist, the sun to Baburen's candle. Claude arrived in Rome around 1617, ground colours for Agostino Tassi, studied under Goffredo Wals in Naples, and by the late 1630s was the leading landscapist in Italy - the one painter of the Roman scene who put the sun itself, low and streaming, into the picture. He learned from Elsheimer's foliage and Breenbergh's example, sketched the Campagna with Poussin, and recorded every finished painting in his Liber Veritatis (British Museum) as an anti-forgery ledger. The doorway is the sun to Baburen's candle. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529. WARNING: birth year disputed: his tombstone says 1600, contemporary sources say c. 1604/05. NOTE: Claude is a very large subject; he works as an alternate precisely because the lane may want the smaller Swanevelt first. Spares (verified, NOT part of the 8): Gerard Douffet (Liege 1594 - 1660/61) - the Liege Caravaggist who knew Baburen in Rome and ended his career in Utrecht. Hold: his ground overlaps Valentin and Manfredi; use if a Liege turn opens. Paulus Bor (Amersfoort c. 1601 - 10 August 1669) - Bentvueghels founder with Baburen ("Orlando"); returned 1626, decorated Honselaarsdijk with Jacob van Campen; his style drifted from Caravaggism to a strange, primitive classicism; friends drank a barrel of wine at his funeral. Hold: better held for a Bentvueghels-family follow-on. Bartholomeus Breenbergh (Deventer, baptised 13 November 1598 - Amsterdam, buried 5 October 1657) - Bentvueghels founder ("het fret"); Rome 1619-1630 with Paul Bril; influenced Claude Lorrain; teachers Pieter Lastman and Jacob Symonsz Pynas. Hold: his fame is landscape, a step sideways from the candlelight line. Orazio Borgianni (Rome 6 April 1574 - 14 January 1616) - Roman Caravaggist, Spanish years 1598-1605, principal patron the Spanish ambassador Francisco Ruiz de Castro; friend of Carlo Saraceni and Antiveduto Grammatica; accused with Saraceni of complicity in an assassination attempt on Giovanni Baglione allegedly planned by Caravaggio; threw a bottle of varnish at Caravaggio in the street. Monographic exhibition Palazzo Barberini 2020. Hold: Italian batch with Saraceni and Gentileschi. Antiveduto Grammatica (Rome 1569 - April 1626) - the head-painter ("gran Capocciante") in whose studio the young Caravaggio worked on arrival in Rome; shared patrons Del Monte, Giustiniani and the Mattei with him; Principe of the Accademia di San Luca 1624, disgraced for trying to sell its Raphael altarpiece. Hold: Italian batch. WARNING: birth given as Siena 1571 in older sources; Petrucci (Paragone, 2004) established Rome 1569. Pieter Lastman (Amsterdam 1583 - buried 4 April 1633) - the man who taught Rembrandt; in Rome 1602-1607, friend of Elsheimer and Rubens; his history paintings (David and Uriah, Coriolanus) laid the foundation of Amsterdam history painting. His pupils included Rembrandt, Jan Lievens and Breenbergh. Hold: big figure, needs a full study slot of its own; doorway runs through Elsheimer. Claude Vignon (Tours 19 May 1593 - Paris 10 May 1670) - French Caravaggist; in Rome c. 1609-1617 with Simon Vouet and Valentin de Boulogne; absorbed Manfredi, Fetti and Elsheimer; later worked for Richelieu and Louis XIII; admitted to the Academie royale 1651. Hold: French batch with Valentin. Giovanni Lanfranco (Parma 26 January 1582 - Rome 30 November 1647) - Carracci pupil (Agostino, then Annibale); dome of Sant'Andrea della Valle (1625-27) and the Gesu Nuovo and San Gennaro chapel in Naples; the illusionistic ceiling painter who carried Correggio's dome language into the High Baroque. Rivalry with Domenichino, Reni and Albani for Roman patronage. Hold: ceiling-painting turn. Cornelis Saftleven (Gorinchem c. 1607 - Rotterdam 1 June 1681) - the versatile younger Utrechter: farmhouse interiors, hell scenes, animal satires, proverb illustrations; van Dyck drew his portrait for the Iconographia as "Hollandus Pictor Noctium Phantasmatum" (Dutch painter of nightly and fantastic scenes); Rubens owned eight of his works. Hold: a generation younger; use for a later-Utrecht turn. Jan Mijtens, also Johannes Mytens (The Hague c. 1614 - 24 December 1670) - the Hague portraitist who carried the Granida line forward: his Meeting of Granida and Daifilo (c. 1655-60, Rijksmuseum) is one of 32 known Dutch paintings of the subject; among the leading Hague portraitists with Honthorst, Adriaen Hanneman and Pieter Nason; co-founder of the Confrerie Pictura (1656). Hold: portrait line, pair with Hanneman. NAME TRAP: Houbraken confused him with the Brussels-born Johannes Meyssens. Jacob Adriaensz. Backer (Harlingen 1608 - Amsterdam 27 August 1651) - Amsterdam's "Rembrandt's opposite": about 140 paintings in twenty years, pioneer of Amsterdam classicism, Granida and Daifilo c. 1635 (Hermitage) and the huge Crowning of Mirtillo (1641); the supposed Rembrandt pupillage is a myth debunked by Blankert and Broos. Hold: Amsterdam classicism turn. NAME TRAP: not Jacob de Backer (c. 1540-c. 1591), the Flemish Mannerist. Giuseppe Vermiglio (Milan c. 1587 - Turin, after 1635) - Milanese Caravaggist; exhibited beside Serodine in the 2022 Modena dossier show; dates and career thinly documented - thin-evidence flag, propose only if the lane wants a deep-cut Roman. Bertholet Flemalle (1614-1675; dates NOT verified in this leg - flag before use) - Douffet's only known pupil; carried the Liege Caravaggist line a generation on. Hold: thin record; pair with Douffet. Daniel Mijtens (Delft c. 1590 - The Hague 1647/48; dates NOT verified in this leg - flag before use) - uncle of Jan Mijtens, portrait painter at the Stuart court in London; the Mijtens family thread. Hold: family follow-on after Jan Mijtens. Jacob van Campen (Haarlem 1596 - Amersfoort 1657; dates NOT verified in this leg - flag before use) - architect-painter, Bor's collaborator at Honselaarsdijk; the classicising turn after Caravaggism. Hold: architecture turn. Cornelis Schut (Antwerp 1597 - 1655; dates NOT verified in this leg - flag before use) - the sixth name in the c. 1620 Bentvueghels drawing; Flemish history painter, Rubens circle. Hold: thin record in this leg. Jan Linsen (dates NOT verified in this leg - flag before use) - Paulus Bor's particular friend among the Bentvueghels (bent-name "Hermaphrodit"); almost nothing else established in the sources read - thin-evidence flag.)

(The 9 names carried in #529 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Cornelis van Poelenburgh (1594/5 - 12 August 1667; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dutch Italianate landscape painter of Utrecht, Bloemaert pupil, co-founder of the Bentvueghels in Rome in 1621 with Jan van Bijlert. He painted the Seven Children of the King and Queen of Bohemia (1628) for Charles I, was himself invited to London by Charles I in 1637 and stayed until 1641, collaborating there with Alexander Keirincx; his small cabinet landscapes with nymphs and ruins were the Utrecht alternative to Honthorst's candlelight. The doorway is the same road to London: the Bloemaert pupil who painted the same Bohemian sitters as Honthorst and then took the same invitation from the same king, nine years later - and the Utrecht painter Rubens actually visited in 1627, while ter Brugghen went unmentioned. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529, Jan van Bijlert (1597/98 - November 1671; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dutch painter, the fourth Utrecht Caravaggist named in the Honthorst article. Bloemaert pupil; co-founder of the Bentvueghels in Rome 1621 with Poelenburgh; some 200 pictures, the most prolific of the group; dean of the Utrecht Guild 1632-1637; painted genre, portrait and religious works, moving from Caravaggism to classicism. The doorway is the fourth man: the most prolific of the Utrecht Caravaggisti, who outlived the other three by forty years and watched the style he helped found become the past - and who painted the same Calling of St. Matthew a decade later. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529. WARNING: a Venus and Adonis in the Centraal Museum is attributed to Honthorst where it is "incorrectly attributed to Jan van Bijlert" per Dayton - the misattribution runs both ways between Honthorst and Bijlert; a study should note it, Joachim Wtewael (1566 - 1 August 1638; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dutch Mannerist painter of Utrecht, the founding generation Honthorst grew up beside. Trained with Joos de Beer, who also trained Bloemaert; stayed Mannerist while the Caravaggisti arrived around him; Honthorst "must also have been extremely familiar with the work of Joachim Wtewael" (Sotheby's). The doorway is the Mannerist who stayed: the Utrecht master Honthorst learned to see against, painting small, jewel-like mythologies while the candlelight painters took the big rooms - and the Utrecht master whose brother rented ter Brugghen his house. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529, Georges de La Tour (13 March 1593 - 30 January 1652; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - French candlelight painter of Lorraine. His Caravaggism "probably reached him through the Dutch Caravaggisti of the Utrecht School" and he "is often compared to the Dutch painter Hendrick Terbrugghen" (Wikipedia); his night scenes - the Magdalen, the Newborn Child, the Cheat with the Ace of Clubs - take the Utrecht single-flame grammar into French religious painting, quieter, stiller, and more severe. The doorway is the daylight-to-night transfer: the Utrecht idiom carried into France, where the candle burns slower and the dark gets deeper - and the Frenchman who learned Caravaggio through the Dutch. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529. NAME TRAP - FLAG IN THE STUDY FILE: this is Georges de La Tour of Vic-sur-Seille, the candlelight painter, NOT Maurice-Quentin de La Tour (1704-1788), the pastellist already studied as #507. Reserve: Matthias Stom (Stomer, c.1600 - after 1652; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dutch or Flemish candlelight painter, the specialist of the covered candle. His half-figures with a covered candle or other hidden light source create dramatic chiaroscuro directly after Honthorst, "Gherardo delle Notti"; worked in Rome, Naples and Sicily, then Malta. The doorway is the covered flame: the painter who hid the light source itself and let only its effect show, the Honthorst grammar taken one step further into darkness - and the northern Caravaggist who never came home, Rome, Naples, Sicily, and 250 pictures. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529. WARNING: his pupillage under Honthorst is contested - the MFAH Houston bulletin calls him Honthorst's most successful student, Wikipedia says no documentary evidence exists and Christie's suggests training under ter Brugghen instead (see C383 in #528 and C417 here); do not state the Honthorst pupillage as fact, Adam de Coster (c.1586 - 4 May 1643; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Flemish painter of Antwerp, the "Pictor Noctium" (Painter of Nights). His half-length candlelight singers - A Man Singing by Candlelight, A Boy Singing by Candlelight - show the Utrecht idiom carried to Antwerp without any recorded Italian trip, "includ[ing] Gerrit van Honthorst" among his models (National Gallery of Ireland). The doorway is the night painter who never went to Rome: the Utrecht candle carried to Antwerp by pictures alone, not by travel - and Antwerp's "Painter of Nights", who took the Utrecht half-length and turned out the lamps. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529. NOTE: birth year varies by source (Wikipedia gives c. 1586, born 1585 or 1586), Bartolomeo Manfredi (1582 - 12 December 1622; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Italian painter, Caravaggio's most important follower in Rome. Honthorst's interest in light and shade was "at one with Bartolomeo Manfredi" (Gerson Digital Italy), and his 'Manfrediana methodus' - half-length figures, actual size - was taken up by Baburen, Honthorst and ter Brugghen. The doorway is the Roman who taught the Utrecht men their method: the teacher none of them sat under, whose pictures were the school - and the Roman who taught the north how to paint low life, the transmitter between Caravaggio and Utrecht. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529. Alternates if any of the 3 fails later (all verified NOT STUDIED): Valentin de Boulogne (1591 - 19 August 1632; christened 3 January 1592, probably born late 1591; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - French Caravaggist in Rome. Baburen in Italy "generally adhered more closely to the strong chiaroscuro of Caravaggio's mature style, as did Valentin de Boulogne" (Gerson Digital Italy); Honthorst's Denial of St Peter (c. 1623) "reveals the influence of the French Caravaggesque artist Valentin de Boullogne" (Dayton). The doorway is the Frenchman in Rome: the third foreigner in the Roman circle, whose chiaroscuro Baburen followed and Honthorst borrowed - and the Frenchman in Rome at the same moment, drinking in the same taverns and painting the same cheats. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529. WARNING: evidence in #528's leg was thinner than for the other five alternates (two citations); the parent may prefer to swap in a spare, Gerard Seghers (1591 - 1651; the Manfredi article gives 1589-1651 - flag the discrepancy in the study file; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Flemish Caravaggist, in Rome c. 1613-1620, pupil of Manfredi, then the most successful history painter in Antwerp after Rubens and van Dyck, sliding from tenebrism into a grander Rubensian manner - the arc from Caravaggio to Rubens in one career. The doorway is Manfredi's pupil, who carried the Roman lesson to Antwerp and then outgrew it. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#529. PROMOTED from a #528 spare (see C417 note). Spares (verified, NOT part of the 10): Simon Vouet (9 January 1590 - 30 June 1649) - French, in Italy 1613-1627, president of the Accademia di San Luca 1624, then Premier peintre du Roi to Louis XIII; the teacher of Valentin and the man who carried Italian Baroque into France. Hold: big figure, needs a full study slot of its own; doorway runs through Valentin. Jan Janssens (1590-1650) - Ghent Caravaggist; his lost Baburen-derived Annunciation survives only through his copy, and his work parallels Stom's - the Ghent branch of the network. Hold: thin English-language literature; pair with a Ghent/Flemish batch later. Theodoor Rombouts (1597-1637) - Antwerp Caravaggist, named with Stom as one of "the most penetrating of Caravaggio's imitators". Hold: his ground overlaps Stom and de Coster; use if an Antwerp turn opens. Hendrick de Somer (1602-1656) - Lokeren-born, Naples-based Caravaggist, pupil of Ribera; the Neapolitan end of the Flemish network. Hold: pair with Stom in a Naples batch. Abraham Janssens I (c. 1571/75-1632) - Antwerp history painter, in Rome 1598-1601, Caravaggesque before Rubens's return; the older Flemish generation. Hold: prequel figure, needs care with the Janssens/Janssens namesakes. Theodoor van Loon (1581-1649) - Mechelen/Brussels Caravaggist, worked for the Archdukes in Brussels; the courtly Flemish branch. Hold: thin literature in English. Carlo Saraceni (1579-1620) - Venetian in Rome, early Caravaggist, teacher of Jean LeClerc; already in ter Brugghen's influence list. Hold: Italian batch with Gentileschi. Orazio Gentileschi (1563-1639) - early Caravaggist, father of Artemisia, later court painter to Charles I; already in ter Brugghen's influence list. Hold: Italian batch; note Artemisia as a possible later study in her own right, not as an appendage. David de Haen (c.1585-1622) - Baburen's companion and housemate in Rome, Giustiniani patron. Held back (carried from #528): thin record; better held for a Baburen follow-on. Jan Both (c.1618-1652) - Italianate landscapist, pupil per Sandrart. Held back (carried from #528): his fame is landscape, a step sideways from the candlelight line. Jan Gerritsz. van Bronckhorst (Bronchorst) - Honthorst pupil. Held back (carried from #528): thin record. Robert van Voerst (1597-c.1636) - Honthorst pupil, Van Dyck's London engraver. Held back (carried from #528): his fame is engraving, not painting. Gerard van Kuijll (1604-73) - Honthorst pupil. Held back (carried from #528): thin record. The Le Nain brothers (Antoine, Louis, Mathieu, 1593/1607-1648/1677) - French Caravaggists named in the La Tour cluster. Held back (carried from #528): three painters, one study would need to choose. Trophime Bigot (1579-1650) - French candlelight painter. Held back (carried from #528): not researched in this leg, hold unverified. Gerard de Lairesse (1640-1711) - later Utrecht classicist. Held back (carried from #528): not researched in this leg, hold unverified. Nicolas Tournier (1590-1639) - Manfredi pupil. Held back (carried from #528): thin record in this leg. Nicolas Regnier (1591-1667) - Manfredi pupil. Held back (carried from #528): thin record in this leg. Angelo Caroselli (1585-1652) - Italian Caravaggesque painter, a common source for the Utrecht St Sebastian group. Held back: Italian batch with Saraceni and Gentileschi. Caravaggio (Michelangelo Merisi da Caravaggio, 1571-1610) - the root of the whole chain. Held back: no study header mentions him in the log, but he was not researched as a candidate in this leg; hold for the parent's decision rather than proposing him as an ordinary hop.)

(The 10 names carried in #527 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Jan Anthonisz. van Ravesteyn (c.1572 - buried 21 June 1657; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dutch court portraitist of The Hague, Mierevelt's follower and his direct rival for the same commissions. Follower of the Delft portrait painter Michiel Jansz van Mierevelt; member of the Guild of St Luke of The Hague 1598; his workshop produced many portraits for the House of Nassau and competed with Mierevelt for several commissions; known for schutterstukken and for the meticulous rendering of costume in muted colour; teacher of a long pupil list including Adriaen Hanneman (disputed - see C335); his daughter Marie married Hanneman in 1640; in 1656 he left the guild with dissatisfied colleagues and co-founded the Confrerie Pictura; his portrait was painted by Van Dyck. The doorway is the rival in the next town: the Hague portraitist who learned Mierevelt's manner, fought him for the Orange commissions for fifty years, and then broke the guild his master had joined. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#526. RECORD WARNING: birth given as c.1572 (Wikipedia) against 1570 (auction houses); his pupillage under Mierevelt is unproven - Wikipedia states "it is unknown who taught him", and the pupil claim rests on stylistic inference (see C336), Paulus Moreelse (1571 - 6 March 1638; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dutch portrait painter of Utrecht, Mierevelt's pupil who built the second centre. Pupil of the Delft portrait painter Michiel Jansz van Mierevelt for about two years, then travelled in Italy; back in Utrecht by 1596, member of the saddlers' guild (which then embraced painters); in 1611, with Abraham Bloemaert, co-founder of Utrecht's new painters' guild (St Luke's), its first deken; a well-known portrait painter receiving commissions from across the Dutch Republic; also architect (Catharijnepoort, Utrecht, 1626), city treasurer and council member; taught at Utrecht's tekenacademie, his pupils including Dirck van Baburen; among the first Dutch painters of Arcadian shepherds and shepherdesses. The doorway is the pupil who left: Mierevelt's Delft portraiture carried to Utrecht, where it founded a guild, a school and a pastoral genre Mierevelt never touched. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#526. Reserve: Pieter Mierevelt (5 October 1596 - 11 January 1623; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - elder son of the subject, the painter who could pass for his father. According to Houbraken, one of two sons of Michiel Jansz van Mierevelt who died early, "whose works were such that they could easily pass for his father's"; Houbraken found him through a 1620 portrait signed with an entwined P and M at the home of the Frisian painter Gerard Wigmana; according to the RKD he painted the Anatomy Lesson of Dr Willem van der Meer (the only person looking at the doctor is Pieter himself). Died in Delft aged 27; his brother Jan died young in 1633. The doorway is the bloodline and the attribution problem in one person: the son whose hand cannot be told from the father's, and whose one firm work is an anatomy lesson - the genre his father's rival's pupil would also paint. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#526. WARNING: thin record - one firm work (the Anatomy Lesson) and Houbraken's anecdote; a study would lean on the workshop/attribution problem rather than an oeuvre. If it fails, fall to the alternates. Alternates if any of the 3 fails later (all verified NOT STUDIED): Anthonie van Montfoort, called Blocklandt (1533 or 1534 - 18 October 1583; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dutch Mannerist painter, Mierevelt's teacher and the founder of Utrecht Mannerism. Born in Montfoort (his father was at one time mayor); pupil of Hendrick Sweersz in Delft and of Frans Floris in Antwerp (Floris is carried as a #526 alternate - the chain continues); settled in Delft, painting for the Oude Kerk and Nieuwe Kerk, works later lost to the beeldenstorm; trip to Italy 1572; settled in Utrecht for good, joining the guild in 1577; with Joos de Beer, responsible for the Mannerist style begun by Utrecht artists around 1590; painted biblical scenes, mythological subjects and portraits; best-known work Joseph interprets Pharaoh's Dream (Centraal Museum, Utrecht); the Assumption triptych of 1579 (Bingen am Rhein); his oil sketches of 1574 were engraved by Philip Galle (carried #525 spare). The doorway is the teacher: the Mannerist master whose school Mierevelt entered at fifteen, and whose Italianate history painting Mierevelt abandoned for the portraiture that made him rich. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#526. WARNING: many works lost to the beeldenstorm; small surviving oeuvre, Anthonie Palamedesz. (1601/02 - 27 November 1673; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Delft portrait and genre painter, Mierevelt's pupil who turned the portrait into the merry company. Born in Leith, Scotland, son of the sculptor/gem-cutter Palamedes Willemsz. Stevens; taught by Mierevelt and the history painter Hans Jordaens; entered the Delft Guild of St Luke in 1621 (or 1636 - sources conflict) and was its hoofdman (deacon) in 1635, 1658, 1663 and 1672; best known for gezelschapje (merry company) scenes of elegant figures at music, play and conversation, and for guardroom scenes; a major figure in the development of genre painting in Delft; his pupils included his younger brother Palamedes Palamedesz. I, the battle painter, and Ludolf de Jongh. The doorway is the pupil who left the single face behind: Mierevelt's portraiture opened out into rooms full of people talking, playing and drinking. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#526. RECORD WARNING: birth given as Delft 1601 (dealers) against baptised 9 November 1602 in South Leith (Wikipedia, citing the baptism record); guild entry 1621 against 1636, Hieronymus Wierix (1553 - 1 November 1619; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Flemish engraver of Antwerp, Mierevelt's first master. Son of the painter/cabinetmaker Anton Wierix I; a child prodigy who began engraving at 11 (1565) and made excellent copies after Durer a year later; worked for the Plantin Press (first paid 1570); admitted to the Antwerp Guild of St Luke 1572/3; known for reproductive engravings after Durer and other masters and for delicate religious prints on a very small scale; brothers Jan (1549-c.1618) and Anton II (c.1552-c.1604) were engravers too; his apprentices included Abraham van Merlen, Jan Baptist van den Sande the elder and Jacob de Weert; "the portraitist Michiel Jansz van Mierevelt began his training with Hieronymus, although he soon moved to another master" (Wierix family article). The doorway is the burin line Mierevelt started with: the boy who could write calligraphy at eight learned the engraver's line before he ever painted a face, and the print is what carried his portraits across Europe afterwards. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#526, Thomas de Keyser (1596/97 - 1667; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Amsterdam portrait painter, the city's leading portraitist before Rembrandt arrived in 1631/32. Second son of Hendrick de Keyser (1565-1621), Amsterdam's famed architect, sculptor and municipal stonemason; trained as a mason under his father from 1616; likely studied painting with Cornelis van der Voort (RKD; British Museum: "Likely studied with the portraitist Cornelis van der Voort"); most important portrait painter in Amsterdam prior to Rembrandt; in the 1620s introduced small-scale full-length sitters in interiors, combining the court portrait format with everyday objects; Constantijn Huygens and his Clerk, 1627 (National Gallery, London); The Company of Captain Allaert Cloeck and Lieutenant Jacobsz. Rotgans, 1632 (Rijksmuseum); from 1640 ran a basalt business, returning to painting in 1654; city architect of Amsterdam 1662-1667. The doorway is the Amsterdam machine against the Delft/Hague one - and Huygens, Mierevelt's sitter and champion, sits to de Keyser too, clerk at his side. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#526. WARNING: the van der Voort pupillage is "likely" (RKD/British Museum), not documented (see C344); birth 1596 against 1596/97, Cornelis van der Voort (c.1576 - buried 2 November 1624; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Amsterdam portrait painter, inventor of the regentenstuk. Born Antwerp, son of a cloth weaver; family moved to Amsterdam c.1585 as Calvinist refugees; painted individual portraits and group portraits including schuttersstukken and regentenstukken depicting regents of charitable institutions, a genre of which he was the inventor; introduced the life-size, full-length format to Dutch portraiture; also an art collector, appraiser and dealer; likely teacher of Thomas de Keyser. The doorway is the civic portrait against the court portrait: where Mierevelt painted princes for the Orange court, van der Voort invented the group portrait of the men who ran Amsterdam's charities - the republican portrait, life-size and in the room. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#526. WARNING: his teachers (Aert Pietersz, Cornelis Ketel) rest on low-quality sources only (see C343); Ketel himself is already carried (#525 reserve), Adriaen Hanneman (c.1603/04 - buried 11 July 1671; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - The Hague portraitist, the Ravesteyn pupil who did go to London and came back a Van Dyckian. According to guild records dated 1619 he studied with Anthony van Ravesteyn II (c.1580-1669), younger brother of Jan (the Leiden Collection, 2024) - though Wikipedia lists him among Jan van Ravesteyn's pupils (see C335); in 1626 he moved to England, where he may have worked in the studios of Daniel Mijtens (carried #524) and of Van Dyck (carried #524), who arrived in London in 1632; married Elizabeth Wilson in London 1630; returned to The Hague c.1638 amid the growing unrest; painted Constantijn Huygens and his five children, 1640 (Mauritshuis); married Maria van Ravesteyn, Jan's daughter, in 1640; painted Charles, Prince of Wales, c.1648 (original lost, about 30 copies); catered to the exiled Stuart court in The Hague; first dean of the Confrerie Pictura. The doorway is the second generation of the refused invitation: the Hague pupil who went to the London Mierevelt refused, learned Van Dyck there, and brought the Van Dyck manner home to paint the exiled Stuarts. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#526. RECORD WARNING: teacher disputed between the Ravesteyn brothers (guild records say Anthony II; Wikipedia and dealers say Jan); birth c.1603 against c.1604; London arrival 1623 (Wikipedia) against 1626 (Leiden Collection). Spares (verified, NOT part of the 10): Jan Mierevelt (1604-1633) - younger son. Held back: the whole record is "died young in 1633" plus workshop membership; no work, no dates beyond the death year; a study now would be invention. Use only if the Pieter reserve fails and the study can be built from the workshop evidence. Willem Jacobsz. Delff (c.1580 - 14 April 1638) - engraver, son-in-law, Mierevelt's exclusive reproductive engraver; married Geertruid 1618; Ekkart: his portrait prints "are among the best of their type ever made in Holland". Held back: his oeuvre is reproductive - the print after the painting, not a painted oeuvre; better held for a print-line study after a painting study. RECORD WARNING: born c.October 1580 / died 14 April 1638 (Wikipedia) against born 19 November 1580 / died 11 April 1638 (genealogieonline). Willem van Honthorst (1594-1666) - younger brother of Gerard; pupil of Abraham Bloemaert and of Gerard; Berlin court painter to Louise Henriette of Nassau 1647-1664; many of his works were previously misattributed to Gerard through the similarity of their signatures. Held back: the brother-shadow; better held for a Honthorst-family follow-on after Gerard is studied. Johan Dircksz. van Nes (died 1650, Delft) - pupil per Houbraken/RKD; teacher of Christiaen van Couwenbergh; one named work. Held back: thin record, no birth date. Hendrick Cornelisz. van Vliet (1611/12 - buried 28 October 1675) - pupil; portraits to c.1650, then Delft church interiors (Oude Kerk, Nieuwe Kerk; Pieterskerk, Leiden 1652). Held back: his fame is architectural painting, a step sideways from the portrait line; hold for a Delft architectural-painting turn. Willem van der Vliet (c.1583/84-1642) - Delft portraitist; uncle/teacher of Hendrick, pupil of Mierevelt. Held back: thin record; overlaps Hendrick. Jacob Willemsz Delff the Elder (c.1550-1601) - Delft portraitist; the fellow townsman whose style Mierevelt first followed; father of Willem Jacobsz. Held back: his record overlaps the Delff family story and the workshop; use if the study series takes a Delft-family turn. Abraham Bloemaert (1566-1651) - Utrecht master; teacher of Honthorst, co-founder of the Utrecht guild with Moreelse. Held back: second-degree link to Mierevelt (teacher of his pupil's contemporary, guild partner of his pupil); a big figure in his own right - hold for a Utrecht-school turn, not as a Mierevelt-network pick. Joachim von Sandrart (1606-1688) - Honthorst's pupil c.1625-1628; author of the Teutsche Academie, a principal source for this whole period. Held back: writer-painter whose fame is the treatise and the source value, not a large painted oeuvre in this line. Palamedes Palamedesz. I (1605-1638) - battle and cavalry painter; younger brother of Anthonie, his pupil; died young. Held back: thin record; better held for a Palamedesz-family follow-on after Anthonie is studied. Jacob Willemsz. Delff the Younger (1619-1661) - grandson; took over Mierevelt's workshop. Held back: thin record; the workshop story belongs inside the #527 study. Anthonie / Anthony van Ravesteyn II (fl. 1614-1669) - younger brother of Jan; primarily a glass painter; Hanneman's teacher per the 1619 guild records. Held back: glass painter, thin record; his value is the Hanneman-teacher correction (C335). Jan Daemen Cool - "possibly" a pupil (Kalden). Held back: one uncertain line, dates not verified in this run. Pieter Gerritsz. Montfoort - "his favourite pupil", an orphan (Kalden). Held back: one line, no dates or works found in this run. Klaudius Kornelisze - pupil named by Kalden. Held back: name only.)

(The 10 names carried in #526 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Jean Bahuet (c.1552-1597; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Flemish court painter to Vincenzo I Gonzaga in Mantua; Pourbus's direct predecessor, post vacant 1597-1600 until Pourbus arrived. He painted almost exclusively idealised female portraits, which the duke presented in his "Gallery of Beauties"; documented in Florence from 1575, settled Mantua 1579, on the ducal payroll at 30 lire a month plus materials. Most of his works are lost; no signed work is known; his oeuvre is being reconstructed from the Gallery of Beauties group, the Vincenzo state portraits and the Mantuan account books. The doorway is the chair Pourbus took: the post stood empty for three years before Pourbus filled it, and the two men's Vincenzo portraits bracket the succession. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#525. WARNING: thin record, most works lost, Frans Pourbus the Elder (1545-1581; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - father of the subject; portrait and religious painter of Antwerp, some genre scenes; trained first with his father Pieter, then in Frans Floris's Antwerp workshop from 1564; master of the Guild of St Luke in Bruges and Antwerp 1569; married Suzanna Floris in 1566, died of typhoid fever 19 September 1581 aged 36, when the Younger was 12. His mature work possibly shows Anthonis Mor's influence (Mor sought his help finding apprentices in 1572). The doorway is the father: the Antwerp training and the Floris workshop the Younger inherited, and the painter whose death at 36 left a 12-year-old to carry the dynasty. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#525. NOTE: listed among #525's exclusions as a family member; proposed here on his own record as a painter (see C330). Reserve: Pieter Pourbus (c.1523-1584; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - grandfather of the subject; Bruges portraitist and painter-cartographer; the leading portrait and history painter in Bruges in the second half of the 16th century; 27 cartographic commissions 1549-1583, 7 maps surviving; the painted Map of the Liberty of Bruges (1571) is his magnum opus; teacher of the Elder and (probably) of the Younger; pupil of Lancelot Blondeel by marriage. The doorway is the dynasty's root: painter and mapmaker in one hand, and the master who most likely trained the Younger. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#525. Alternates if any of the 3 fails later (all verified NOT STUDIED): Gortzius Geldorp (1553-c.1619; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - fellow pupil of Frans the Elder alongside the Younger; left Antwerp c.1579 in the service of Carlo d'Aragona Tagliavia, Duke of Terranova, Philip II's ambassador, and stayed in Cologne, becoming the leading portrait painter of his generation there and the local elite's favorite portraitist; about 70 works attributed, mostly portraits; Cologne painters' guild representative Christmas 1609; Cologne city council 1610 and 1613. The doorway is the fork: same training, civic career instead of court career. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#525. RECORD WARNING: death year 1618 in older sources, c.1619 in recent scholarship, Frans Floris (1519/20-1570; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - the "Flemish Raphael", leading Antwerp Romanist; master of the Elder, great-uncle by marriage of the Younger; his workshop was the Italianate school the Pourbus line came out of; on his death his unfinished grand-prior commission was completed by his assistants Frans Pourbus the Elder and Chrispijn van den Broeck. The doorway is the master behind the father. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#525. The doorway is the Bruges root: the Renaissance polymath the dynasty married into. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#525, Adriaen Thomasz Key (c.1544-after 1589; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Antwerp portraitist; took over Willem Key's workshop 1567; his portraits were long misattributed to Mor and to Frans Pourbus the Elder (one 1574 portrait was successively catalogued as Mor, then Pourbus the Elder, before Key's monogram was revealed on cleaning); Mor's influence visible in his later work; called "the most talented of his generation" by David Freedberg. The doorway is the lookalike: the Antwerp contemporary whose pictures cannot be told from the Elder's. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#525, Jan van Scorel (1495-1562; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Utrecht painter, Mor's teacher (Mor was his apprentice, then assistant by 1540); painter-cartographer like Pieter Pourbus (both named in the "Mapping the wet land" study of painter-cartographers 1480-1550). The doorway is the teacher of the teacher: Mor's master, and the Utrecht link in the cartographer-painter line.
