(The 11 names carried in #525 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Wenceslas Hollar (1607-1677; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Bohemian engraver, etcher, and painter, born Vaclav Hollar in Prague. Ruined by the Sack of Prague, he left law for art, apprenticed to Matthaus Merian in Frankfurt in 1627, and was taken up by Thomas Howard, 21st Earl of Arundel - Daniel Mytens' first English patron - travelling with him to England in 1637. He spent most of his life in England, producing some 2,740 plates and 400 drawings: city views (the Long View of London, 1647), maps, costume books, natural-history plates of shells, muffs, and insects, and portraits after Holbein and van Dyck. He stood the siege of Basing House with Inigo Jones, worked in Antwerp 1644-1652, returned to London, and died in poverty in 1677. The doorway is the face itself: the only surviving image of Gheeraerts is Hollar's 1644 engraving after Gheeraerts' lost 1627 self-portrait, so the study of Hollar is the study of how Gheeraerts is remembered at all. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#524, Michiel Jansz van Mierevelt was carried in #525 and is now studied as #527; he is NOT carried here - see Do NOT re-pick. Reserve: Cornelis Ketel (1548-1616; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dutch Mannerist painter, born out of wedlock in Gouda, apprenticed to his uncle at 11. Driven out by the Dutch Revolt and the 1573 plague, he arrived in London in 1573 and worked there until 1581, painting Sir Christopher Hatton, the Earl of Oxford, the German Hansa merchants of the Steelyard, and - in 1578 - Elizabeth I herself, who sat to him at Hanworth. He then returned to Amsterdam, where in 1588 he painted The Company of Captain Rosecrans, the earliest Dutch group portrait with figures standing full-length, and from 1595 he sculpted as well as painted and wrote poetry. The doorway is the earlier exile: Ketel is the previous generation of the exact emigre line Gheeraerts belongs to - a Netherlandish Protestant portraitist working the Tudor court a decade before Gheeraerts' earliest signed works - and his full-length group portrait is the Dutch cousin of Gheeraerts' full-length court portrait. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#524. Alternates if any of the 4 fails later (all verified NOT STUDIED): Marcus Gheeraerts III (c.1602-c.1654; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - elder surviving son of Marcus Gheeraerts the Younger and Magdalena de Critz, baptised at the Dutch Church, Austin Friars, and "also a painter" - the third Marcus in a three-generation painting family. That is nearly the whole record: no signed work, no patron, no documented commission was found in this run. His value as a study is the dynasty question - what a court painter's son inherits, a workshop, a name, and a clientele, and whether the name alone sustains a career after the father's fashion fades. The doorway is the bloodline: he is the literal continuation of Gheeraerts' workshop into the Caroline era, and the study would test how much of a famous father's practice survives in a son with no surviving work. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#524. WARNING: thin record - a study may need to be built from the family and workshop evidence rather than from his own works, Hendrick Goltzius (1558-1617; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - German-born Dutch printmaker, draftsman, and painter, the leading Dutch engraver of Northern Mannerism. Burned as a child, his right hand permanently damaged, he nevertheless perfected the swelling burin line; he moved to Haarlem in 1577, set up his own print publishing business around 1578, and produced some 388 plates plus about 500 drawings and 50 paintings. His workshop engraved Spranger's designs for Rudolf II's Prague court, and his 1593-94 Meisterstiche imitated Durer, Lucas van Leyden, Raphael, and Parmigianino in their own manners. In 1600 he gave up printmaking and painted. The doorway is the print standard: Goltzius was Galle's pupil and the master engraver of the Northern world the Elder Gheeraerts' prints circulated in, and the English engravers who carried Gheeraerts' sitters into print - the de Passes, then Hollar - all worked in his shadow. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#524, Simon de Passe (c.1595-1647; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dutch engraver, eldest son of Crispijn van de Passe the Elder. He settled in London in 1616 and built a successful portrait-engraving practice, contributing to Henry Holland's Baziliologia (1618), engraving the royal family and noblemen, making James I's silver portrait medallions (1616-1619), and producing the famous 1616 print of Pocahontas. In 1624 he moved to Copenhagen as royal engraver to Christian IV of Denmark - Anne of Denmark's brother - and stayed until his death. The NPG associates him with 199 portraits. The doorway is the printed court: de Passe engraved the same royal family Gheeraerts painted in oils, in the cheaper medium that actually spread their faces, and his Copenhagen move follows Anne of Denmark's own Danish family line. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#524, William Segar (c.1554-1633; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - English portrait painter and officer of arms, trained as a scrivener, admitted to the College of Arms in 1585, Somerset Herald 1589, Norroy King of Arms 1593, Garter Principal King of Arms 1607-1633. He painted Elizabeth's favourite Essex in black armour for the 1590 Accession Day tilt, is sometimes credited with the Ermine Portrait of Elizabeth, and painted Leicester, Drake, and other courtiers; his last recorded payment as a painter is a portrait of the queen in 1597. He also wrote The Booke of Honour and Armes (1590) and Honor Military and Civill (1602), the literature of the tilt yard. The doorway is the tilt: Segar painted the same Essex and the same Elizabeth as Gheeraerts, and wrote the books that explained the Accession Day pageantry Lee devised and Gheeraerts illustrated - painter, herald, and author of one court spectacle. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#524, Hieronymus Custodis (died c.1593; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Flemish portrait painter, native of Antwerp, one of the Protestant exiles who fled to England after the fall of Antwerp in 1585. Three signed and dated portraits of 1589 - Giles Brydges, 3rd Baron Chandos, and his 14-year-old daughter Elizabeth Brydges, maid of honour to Elizabeth I - firmly establish him in London by 1589; Strong attributes a Sir Henry Bromley of 1587 and an Edward Talbot of 1586 to him. He was a member of the Dutch Church, Austin Friars, and his widow remarried in 1593, the presumed year of his death. His surviving work is small: half-length figures on dark grounds, linear precision in ruffs and jewels, cool colours, meticulous flesh. The doorway is the fellow exile: Custodis arrived in London in the same Antwerp exodus that shaped Gheeraerts' world, painted the same Protestant nobility in the same years Gheeraerts was finding his feet, and died just as Gheeraerts became fashionable - the road not taken, cut short. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#524, Crispijn van de Passe the Elder (c.1564-1637; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dutch publisher and engraver, founder of the van de Passe dynasty of engravers, comparable to the Wierix and Sadeler families. His Cologne publishing house produced portraits of European nobility; his works include the famous 1605 engraving of the Gunpowder Plotters and a 1598 engraved portrait of James VI of Scotland. Four of his children - Simon, Crispijn II, Willem, and Magdalena - were also engravers, and the family's Heroologia Anglica (1620) holds 65 portraits of English notables. The doorway is the family press: Crispijn is the father of Simon de Passe, who engraved Gheeraerts' royal sitters in London, and his own James VI print of 1598 carried the future James I's face before Gheeraerts ever painted him. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#524, John Bettes the Younger (died 1616; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - English portrait painter, son of John Bettes the Elder (active c.1531-1570, painter to Henry VIII and Catherine Parr). Like Isaac Oliver and Rowland Lockey, he is believed to have studied under Nicholas Hilliard. He lived on Grub Street, St Giles Cripplegate, later St Gregory-by-St Paul's; a miniature of Francis Walsingham is attributed to him, and his studio is thought to have made a number of portraits of Elizabeth I. Signed works are very rare - a Portrait of a Lady signed and dated 1589, a girl of 1587. He died in January 1616, bequeathing his son two easels and a porphyry slab and muller for grinding pigments. The doorway is the Hilliard line: Bettes is a fellow pupil of Hilliard alongside Gheeraerts' brother-in-law Oliver, painting the same queen from the same training, with almost nothing signed to prove it - the anonymous end of the Elizabethan portrait trade. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#524. Spares (verified, NOT part of the 11): Ferdinando Clifton (active 1627) - Gheeraerts' apprentice, freeman of the Painter-Stainers' Company in 1627. Held back because the entire record is one line: no work, no dates, no further trace found; a study now would be invention. Aert Mytens (c.1541-1601) - uncle of Daniel Mytens, first known Mytens painter; worked in Naples 1574-1592 for the Viceroy's court. Held back because his whole career is Italian, one step removed from the English court line; better held for a Mytens-family study after Daniel Mytens (carried #524) is done. Philip Galle (1537-1612) - Antwerp publisher who printed after Marcus Gheeraerts the Elder's designs; Goltzius was his pupil. Held back because his link is to the Elder, not the Younger, and his ground overlaps Goltzius heavily; hold until the Elder himself is studied (carried as reserve in #522), when Galle becomes the natural print-publisher follow-on.)

(The 10 names carried in #524 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: William Larkin (early 1580s - 1619; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - English portrait painter, active 1609-1619, known until the 1950s only as the "Curtain Master" for the voluminous silk curtains in his pictures. About 40 portraits identified, of Jacobean courtiers and gentry, including Richard Sackville, 3rd Earl of Dorset (1613), the twin Cecil sisters (1615), and the nine enormous Suffolk Collection full-lengths at Kenwood; he never painted members of the royal family. His earliest portraits (Edward Herbert, 1609-10, and Sir Thomas Lucy III) had formerly been assumed to be the work of Isaac Oliver until Lees-Milne identified Larkin in 1952, and the ODNB judges his style points to training in one of the London studios - of Isaac Oliver or Marcus Gheeraerts. His work marks the last stage of the Holbein-Hilliard flat portrait tradition, with sitters in meticulously rendered wardrobe and props; he died in 1619, the same year as Hilliard and Robert Peake the Elder, and Apollo calls those three deaths the end of the insular Tudor-Jacobean portrait tradition. The doorway is the Curtain Master whose first pictures were taken for Oliver's, who may have learned his trade in Oliver's own studio, and who closed the flat-portrait tradition Oliver stood in the middle of. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#523. CAVEAT: the studio training is a stylistic inference, not a document, and it could have been Gheeraerts' studio, Daniel Mytens (c.1590-1647; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Dutch portrait painter (Daniel Mijtens). Anne of Denmark commissioned him alongside Paul van Somer and Isaac Oliver, the three names in one sentence in her biography, as the artists who "led English taste in visual arts for a generation". He arrived in England by 1613, lured by van Somer's success, to whom he was related and whose neighbour he was in St Martin's Lane; he became van Somer's official successor after his death, was granted a pension in 1624, and became Charles I's picture-drawer in ordinary by patent of 30 May 1625, painting 15 portraits of Charles. He is the Dutchman who took the Jacobean court portrait from van Somer and held it until Van Dyck arrived. The doorway is the Dutch successor who shared Anne's patronage with Oliver and van Somer, then held Charles I's portrait until Van Dyck took it from him. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#523. Reserve: Alexander Cooper (baptised 1609 - 1660; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source; RECORD WARNING: death 1660 is the commonly cited year, not verified in this run) - Miniaturist, younger brother of Samuel Cooper, adopted with him by their uncle John Hoskins. Sandrart states he "was taught by Peter Oliver (c.1594-1647) and was 'by far [his] most celebrated pupil'" - the direct teaching link from Isaac Oliver's son to the next generation. He became limner to Christina, Queen of Sweden, and painted a series of portraits of the children of the King and Queen of Bohemia; his Portrait of Elizabeth Stuart, Queen of Bohemia, is in the 2013 Cooper catalogue. He is the pupil who carried the Oliver miniature out of England into a northern European court. The doorway is the pupil Peter Oliver called his most celebrated, who took the Oliver miniature to the court of Christina of Sweden. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#523. Alternates if any of the 4 fails later (all verified NOT STUDIED): Lucas de Heere (1534 - 29 August 1584; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Flemish painter, watercolourist, poet and biographer, pupil of his father Jan de Heere in Ghent; Protestant refugee, in England c.1567-1577, elder of the Dutch stranger church at Austin Friars. He trained John de Critz, probably Marcus Gheeraerts the Younger, and possibly Robert Peake the Elder - the teacher behind the whole Jacobean workshop web that Oliver worked inside. His pupil Karel van Mander is the source for Lucas Horenbout teaching Holbein. He painted the earliest known realistic depiction of Stonehenge (1573-1575, British Library watercolour) and a gallery of national costumes for the Earl of Lincoln in 1570. The doorway is the Ghent master who taught de Critz, Gheeraerts and perhaps Peake, the teacher behind the four Jacobean workshops. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#523, David des Granges (baptised 1611 - died in or before c.1672; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source; RECORD WARNING: birth baptised 1611 here against 1613 in Foster's table; death "in or before c.1672" against 1675 in the 1911 Britannica) - Anglo-French miniature painter, initially influenced as a miniaturist by John Hoskins and Peter Oliver. He worked also as an engraver and in oils; was with Charles II in Scotland in the early 1650s copying a likeness of Charles by Adriaen Hanneman; after the Restoration he was influenced by Jacob Huysmans and possibly Samuel Cooper. His Saltonstall Family is in the Tate; work at Ham House and Windsor Castle. He is the next generation of the miniature after Peter Oliver, carrying the Hoskins-Oliver influence through the Civil War into the Restoration. The doorway is the Anglo-French miniaturist Hoskins and Peter Oliver shaped, who carried their miniature through the Civil War to the Restoration court. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#523, Cornelius Johnson (1593-1661; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Portrait painter, known in the Netherlands as Cornelis Jonson van Ceulen, born in Britain to Flemish parents. "Charles I's other portrait painter" (The Art Newspaper, on Karen Hearn's book): appointed "his Majesty's servant in ye quality of Picture Drawer" in 1632, the first British painter regularly to sign his work, and "almost a worthy rival to his contemporary Van Dyck". He painted three small full-length portraits of Charles I's eldest children in 1639, and his popular portrait miniatures were painted in oil on metal - the miniature carried into oil, the direction Oliver's chiaroscuro had pointed. He left for the northern Netherlands in 1643 after the outbreak of civil war and died in Utrecht. The doorway is Charles I's other portrait painter, who took the miniature into oil on metal and signed his work when no English painter did. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#523, Richard Gibson (1615-1690; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source; RECORD WARNING: birth 1615 in Cumberland is the anecdotal date; it has recently been proposed he was born in London in 1605 to the picturemaker John Gibson) - Portrait miniaturist and courtier, "Dwarf Gibson" for his 3 foot 10 inch stature. Apprenticed to Francis Cleyn at the Mortlake Tapestry Works; entered the service of Philip Herbert, 4th Earl of Pembroke, in 1639. In May 1672 he was re-elected to the court as the king's limner under Charles II, two weeks after the death of Samuel Cooper - "To succeed Samuel Cooper was no light feat". He was succeeded the following year by Nicholas Dixon, and later became drawing-master to the two young daughters of James, Duke of York. His daughter Susannah-Penelope Rosse imitated Cooper's work. The doorway is the dwarf limner who took the king's limner post from Cooper's line two weeks after Cooper died. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#523, Anthony van Dyck (1599-1641; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Flemish Baroque painter, leading court painter in England, dominant influence on English portraiture for 150 years. His first task on arriving in England in 1620 was copying Paul van Somer's royal portraits - copying the man who had displaced Oliver's brother-in-law as Anne's favourite. Peter Oliver made miniature copies after Van Dyck, including the Countess of Southampton, "one of the most remarkable works in miniature existing". Samuel Cooper was "commonly stil'd the Van-Dyck in little", learned his art from Van Dyck's works, and copied Van Dyck's oil portraits, some under his supervision. The doorway is the Fleming whose portraits the Olivers copied in little and whose manner Cooper made the miniature's own, the man who ended the world Oliver worked in. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; no local file; not carried in #520-#523. STATUS CAVEAT: one whole-log mention exists, a #513/#514 Veronese-influence note listing Van Dyck as "second-order influence, held for a later Veronese-influence leg" - a held note, not a carried candidacy in any New-from #520-#523 block, Francis Cleyn (c.1582-1658; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - German-born painter and designer at the Mortlake Tapestry Works, and a miniaturist in his own right (Foster groups him among the 17th-century miniaturists). He is the master of Richard Gibson: Gibson was apprenticed to him at Mortlake after his talent for drawing was noted while he was a page in a noble household there. The link to Oliver is second-degree, through Gibson and the Mortlake circle, and is flagged as such; Cleyn's own miniature work is the direct art-historical link. The doorway is the Mortlake designer-miniaturist who taught Dwarf Gibson his trade. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions; no local file; not carried in #520-#523, Inigo Jones (1573-1652; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Architect and stage designer, the designer of the Jacobean court spectacle Oliver's sitters performed in: the Barriers devised by Ben Jonson and designed by Jones, performed on Twelfth Night 1610 in the Banqueting House at Whitehall, is cited in the Royal Collection catalogue entry for Oliver's Henry, Prince of Wales miniature, and Jones designed Anne of Denmark's masques, including The Masque of Blackness (1605). His built work - the Banqueting House, the Queen's House at Greenwich - is the architecture of the court Oliver painted for. The doorway is the architect of the masques and palaces Oliver's sitters performed in, the designer of the Jacobean court itself. VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero "## Study #" headers; zero whole-log mentions (the local-file grep hit "tony-ray-jones" is a different person, a photographer); no local file; not carried in #520-#523. Spares (verified, NOT part of the 11): Rowland Lockey (c.1565-1616) - painter, goldsmith and miniaturist; apprenticed to Hilliard for eight years from 1581, alongside Oliver; copyist of Holbein's Family of Sir Thomas More; joint beneficiary with Oliver of Peter Matheeusen's 1588 will. Held back because his known work is mostly copies, so a study would be a study of copying as a practice rather than of an original oeuvre. Laurence Hilliard (baptised 5 March 1582 - 1648) - miniaturist, son and pupil of Nicholas Hilliard; royal limner after his father's death; took over the Gutter Lane workshop Oliver had left as a rival. Held back because his surviving oeuvre is small and was judged a "feeble" version of his father's style. Edward Norgate (1581-1650) - writer, herald and limner; author of Miniatura or The Art of Limning, the treatise that carries Hilliard's teaching; wrote of Oliver's Madonna that "cost him two years [of his life]". Held back because he is a writer-painter whose fame is the treatise, not a large surviving painted oeuvre.)

(The 10 names carried in #523 are carried forward here, NOT re-proposed as new: Peter Oliver (1589 - before 27 December 1647; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - English miniaturist, eldest son of Isaac Oliver, trained by his father; member of Charles I's household from 1625; remarkable for his series of miniature copies in watercolour after Old Master pictures (Raphael, Titian, Correggio, Holbein), most made at the king's desire, seven still at Windsor Castle; his most important work is the group of the three grandsons of the 1st Viscount Montague with their servant. The doorway is the son who learned limning at his father's bench, took the Oliver miniature into Charles I's household, and turned the portrait miniature into small history painting by copying the Old Masters in watercolour; VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero log header hits, zero whole-log mentions, no local file, not carried in #522; RECORD WARNING: birth year given as 1589 (Wikipedia, Royal Collection Trust) against c.1594 (1911 Britannica, DNB), death 1647 against 1648 (DNB/Britannica), Samuel Cooper (1609 - 5 May 1672; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - English miniature painter, nephew of John Hoskins, by whom he and his brother Alexander were instructed; Pepys called him "the great limner in little" and Aubrey "The Prince of Limners"; Graham Reynolds judged him "the greatest English-born portrait painter of the seventeenth century"; gave the miniature "the strength and freedom of oil" (Walpole); painted Oliver Cromwell several times and Charles II, who appointed him official limner in 1663; his 1665 Charles II in ceremonial attire is among his best-known works. The doorway is the nephew Hoskins taught who out-painted his master, gave the miniature the strength of oil, and became the greatest limner of the seventeenth century, painting Cromwell and Charles II alike; VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero log header hits, zero whole-log mentions, no local file, not carried in #522; RECORD WARNING: birth given as 1609 (DNB, most sources) against autumn/winter 1608 (Philip Mould) and 1607/8 (2013 exhibition catalogue). Reserve: John Hoskins (1589 or 1590 - February 1664; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - English miniature painter, uncle of Samuel Cooper and Alexander Cooper, who received their artistic education in his house; born in Wells; trained first as an oil painter, then devoted himself to miniature; his early work (c.1615) shows Hilliard's influence, including the flat blue ground and the jewelling technique for a pearl earring, and twenty years later he painted pearls and gold in a more painterly fashion under Van Dyck's influence; painted Charles I, Henrietta Maria and many of the nobility; Charles I granted him an annuity of 200 pounds a year in 1640. The doorway is the Wells painter who carried Hilliard's blue ground and jewelled pearls into the Caroline court, then let Van Dyck teach him to paint them instead, and who taught the greatest miniaturist of the century; VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero log header hits, zero whole-log mentions, no local file, not carried in #522; CAVEAT: his early training is undocumented and he is NOT documented as Hilliard's pupil -- the Hilliard link is stylistic influence, not apprenticeship. Alternates if any of the 4 fails later (all verified NOT STUDIED): Simon Bening (c.1483-1561; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Flemish miniaturist, generally regarded as the last major artist of the Netherlandish tradition; member of the Bruges guild of St John and St Luke as an illuminator in 1508; father of Levina Teerlinc, who became a miniature painter and emigrated to England; his works include the Golf Book, the Flowers Book of Hours (c.1520-1525), the Hennessy Hours and the Da Costa Hours; his self-portrait of 1558, aged 75, is in the Met. The doorway is the Bruges illuminator whose daughter Levina carried the Ghent-Bruges miniature into the Tudor court and may have taught Hilliard to limn -- the root of the whole English miniature tradition, two generations back from Hilliard; VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero log header hits, zero whole-log mentions, no local file, not carried in #522, Lucas Horenbout (c.1490/1495-1544; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Flemish artist, the founding painter of the long and distinct English tradition of portrait miniature painting; master of the Guild of St Luke in Ghent in 1512; came to England in the mid-1520s with his father and his sister Susanna; King's Painter to Henry VIII from 1525 until his death; twenty-three surviving portrait miniatures usually attributed to him, almost all of royalty, including the earliest known miniature portrait in England, Princess Mary (NPG 6453); he taught miniature painting on vellum to Hans Holbein the Younger, at least according to Karel van Mander. The doorway is the Ghent illuminator who founded the English portrait miniature a generation before Hilliard was born, and taught Holbein to limn on vellum; VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero log header hits, zero whole-log mentions, no local file, not carried in #522, Gerard Horenbout (c.1465 - c.1541; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - Flemish manuscript illuminator, father of Lucas and Susanna, and the teacher of both; master in Ghent in 1487; court painter to Margaret of Austria, regent of the Netherlands, 1515 to about 1522; painted the entire calendar decoration of the Grimani Breviary; first recorded in England in 1528 and had died in Ghent by 1540. It is a great marvel that a woman should do so much."; came to England with her family in the mid-1520s, was gentlewoman of the Privy Chamber to three of Henry VIII's queens, and had a successful career as a miniature painter and illuminator. The doorway is the illuminator's daughter Durer marvelled at in Bruges, who crossed to Henry VIII's court with her brother and worked as a miniaturist while serving three queens -- the woman artist at the very start of the English miniature; VERIFIED NOT STUDIED: zero log header hits, zero whole-log mentions, no local file, not carried in #522; RECORD WARNING: dates vary, one source gives 1506-1554, and her birth year is uncertain; CAVEAT: her miniatures today cannot be told apart from her brother's, so attributions are shaky at best, Robert Peake the Elder (c.1551-1619; as commonly cited, not verified from a primary source) - English painter, the only English-born painter of the group of four artists whose workshops were closely connected: Peake, John de Critz, Marcus Gheeraerts the Younger and Isaac Oliver; first heard of professionally in 1576 in the pay of the Office of the Revels; appointed picture maker to Prince Henry in 1604 and Serjeant-Painter to James I in 1607, a post he shared with de Critz; his best-known works are the full-length portraits of Henry, Prince of Wales (1603, Met; NPG 4515) and Princess Elizabeth (NPG 6113). ## Corrections

Leg 1 corrections (C792-C807), as reported:

- C792. D1 letter -- "c. 1649/1652" is not a sourced date form. Sources give "1649/50" (SMK paper title; Sotheby's "Antwerp 1649/50"; NL Wiki body "1649 of 1650"; Christie's "1649" and "1650") or "c. 1652" (EN Wiki). The study date line must give both poles, e.g. "1649/50 or c. 1652", not the merged "c. 1649/1652".
- C793. D3 letter -- "carried the pronk still life back to Amsterdam" overstates. Willem Kalf was already the Amsterdam pronk master before 1685; no source read calls Broeck a "pronk" painter. Accurate: he brought de Heem's banquet manner back to Amsterdam in 1685.
- C794. D5 substance -- Kick, Kouwenbergh and Walscapelle were not "in tow" in 1685. Kick died 18 June 1681 in Amsterdam (EN Wiki Kick) and was Amsterdam-based; Walscapelle was Kick's Amsterdam pupil; Kouwenbergh was Broeck's pupil in Amsterdam after the return. The chain's Amsterdam opening is Broeck alone, returning to a city where his teacher's line was already established.
- C795. Houbraken makes Broeck a pupil of Ernst Stuven ("ELIAS van den BROECK, born in Antwerp, was one of his first and best students", vol. 3 p. 378, the "his" being Stuven). Do not repeat this as fact: it conflicts with the documented Kick (1665) and de Heem (1669) pupillages and with chronology (Stuven b. 1657, in Amsterdam from 1675). Treat it as a Houbraken muddle; the same passage's "born in Antwerp" is usable, the pupillage is not.
- C796. Birthplace is disputed, not settled for Amsterdam. EN Wiki says "probably in Amsterdam" (c. 1652); NL Wiki body, Houbraken ("born in Antwerp") and Sotheby's ("Antwerp 1649/50") say Antwerp. The study must present both, not state an Amsterdam birth as fact.
- C797. Do not cite "Still Life with Flowers in a Glass Vase (c. 1685, Rijksmuseum)" as a Broeck work. MyOpenMuseum attributes it to him; the picture is Rachel Ruysch's, Rijksmuseum SK-A-354 (Wikimedia Commons file page). The aggregator is wrong on this attribution.
- C798. NL Wikipedia's infobox lists "Werkgever Athenaeum Illustre" and work locations including London. No biographical source read supports a London period or an Athenaeum Illustre employment; these read as Wikidata artefacts. Do not build a London chapter or an academic post into the study.
- C799. The SMK paper dates Broeck "(1649/50-1708)" -- a third birth form alongside "c. 1652" and the doorway's "c. 1649/1652". The study's date line should carry the dispute openly: born 1649/50 (Antwerp) or c. 1652 (Amsterdam); buried 6 February 1708.
- C800. Chronology anchor -- the only dated Broeck work known is the 1676 picture sold by Valls Limited, London, 1998 (Christie's oyster-still-life essay). The oyster still life itself is dated c. 1669-1670, from the de Heem workshop years. The study's early/late split rests on this single dated picture plus the 1673 and 1685 moves.
- C801. Keep Weyerman's and Christie's butterfly tellings separate. Weyerman (via Sphinx): pasting real butterflies caused such indignation he was "forced to leave Antwerp". Christie's (lot essay, citing Meijer 1980): pasting real wings on a white ground was standard contemporary practice, shared with Marseus van Schrieck, Falch and Kouwenbergh. No source read documents an expulsion. Present Weyerman as gossip, Christie's as technique.
- C802. D6 count reconciliation -- 8 whole-log mentions in 5 lines was correct at #561 check time (4 in 3 before the #560 entry; the #560 entry added 4 in 2 lines). The #561 entry itself added 3 occurrences in 2 lines, so the live log now reads 11 in 7. The letter holds as a snapshot; the drift is the entry's own writing, not a miscount.
- C803. "Vivat Rex" (1689), "Roses, flowers, insects and snakes" (c. 1700), "Still Life with Herring, Crab, Bread and Grapes" (1686), "Flowers in a Vase" (1686), "Cactuses and lizards" (1688) and "Stilleben mit zerlegtem Fisch und Krabben" (1686) rest on wahooart/artsdot/meisterdrucke-class aggregators only. Use them as provisional titles, not anchored works; do not build a mechanism on any of them alone.
- C804. Sotheby's lot heading "Antwerp 1649/50 - 1708 Amsterdam" supports the Antwerp birth pole and is the strongest auction-house dating; it agrees with the SMK paper's "1649/50" against EN Wikipedia's "c. 1652".
- C805. Unused: reserved, no correction in leg 1's range.
- C806. D2 note -- Antwerp 1673 is the year Broeck accompanied de Heem to Antwerp and became a Guild of St. Luke master there (EN Wiki). It is not a pupillage date. The doorway parenthetical "(Utrecht 1669, Antwerp 1673)" is acceptable shorthand, but the study must not say he became de Heem's pupil in Antwerp in 1673.
- C807. The Fitzwilliam forest-floor piece (PD.39-1975, thistle/boletus/snail/lizard/butterflies/bee) is only "Attributed to" Broeck, was once given to Ruysch, and the museum note proposes Gustavus Hamilton as an alternative. Cite it as attributed/contested, never as a secure Broeck.

Leg 2 corrections, as reported:

- Leg 2 correction 1. W02 (KMSst61) is not a flower piece. The Commons file is named "Flowers -- KMSst61", and the SMK poster lists it among the five flower pictures, but the image itself is a stone-plinth forest piece: oak branch with acorns, a pink rose and poppies on the plinth, a lizard, a stag beetle, mushrooms, a tortoiseshell butterfly. Any study text or prompt that calls KMSst61 a vase/bowl flower piece is wrong; the "flowers" in it are a minority element.
- Leg 2 correction 2. W20 is black-and-white only. The only Commons image of the Wallraf-Richartz fish-and-crab still life (WRM 1918) is a greyscale archival photograph. Its colour, palette and handling are unknown from this file. Do not describe them, and do not use W20 as evidence for any colour claim.
- Leg 2 correction 3. W14 is "attributed to", not secure. The Fitzwilliam/Commons filename for PD.39-1975 says "(attributed to)". It is the only work in the set with a landscape background (blue dusk sky, hills at left), which is itself a reason for caution. Present it as attributed, never as certainly his.
- Leg 2 correction 4. B08 duplicates W10. The generic Commons file "a vase of flowers" (B08) is a near-duplicate composition of the Ashmolean Vase of Flowers WA1940.2.16 (W10): same orange poppies, same red-orange tulip, same white rose centre, same pink rose bottom left, same fallen poppy on the ledge. It is a second version or a copy, not an independent invention. Count the motif once.
- Leg 2 correction 5. "Vivat Rex" (W06) is not a flower piece, and JHNA's Antwerp dating of the forest pieces conflicts with the biography. W06 contains almost no cut flowers: it is a nature tableau (nest, beetles, grasshoppers, orange, lizard, mushrooms) with "VIVAT REX" carved on the plinth. Separately, JHNA says Broeck "was working in Antwerp on a series of forest pieces" for the Snake dated ca. 1695 -- but he left Antwerp in 1685 (Wikipedia, Sphinx). Either the c. 1695 date or the Antwerp location is wrong. Do not repeat "Antwerp, c. 1695" for the Snake.
- Leg 2 correction 6. Birth year/place: 1657 is wrong; Antwerp vs Amsterdam unresolved. The Ashmolean/Fitzwilliam Commons filenames give "(1657-1708)"; 1657 would make him 8 years old when he became Cornelis Kick's pupil in 1665 -- impossible. Do not use 1657. The remaining sources disagree: Wikipedia says c. 1652, probably Amsterdam; Sphinx/askART say born Antwerp, 1649/1650; SMK file data says 1650-1708; the task brief says c. 1649/1652. Birthplace (Antwerp vs Amsterdam) is unresolved in the sources read.
- Leg 2 correction 7. W15 has no visible ants. The title is "Still life of flowers with ants in a glass vase", but no ants are visible in the image at 2048 px. Do not assert ants as a motif in this work. (An ant IS visible in W18, bottom left -- cite that one instead.)
- Leg 2 correction 8. W17 is a photograph of a framed painting. The Boijmans file shows the painting in its gilt frame on a white wall. Colour, crop and proportions taken from it are approximate only; do not use it for palette claims.
- Leg 2 correction 9. There is no Commons category "Elias van den Broeck" under that exact name. The categorymembers API query returned 0 files; the Ashmolean subcategory exists, but the flat category does not (or is named differently). Files were found by Commons file search instead (50 results). Do not cite a Commons category page for this artist.
- Leg 2 correction 10. W20 and B10 may be the same painting. Stilleben mit zerlegtem Fisch und Krabben (WRM 1918, W20) and Still Life with Herring, Crab, Bread and Grapes (Wallraf-Richartz, c. 1686, B10) are both Wallraf-Richartz fish-and-crab still lifes dated 1686 in listings; the English title may be a translation/re-titling of the same work. Do not count them as two works and do not give them two different dates.
- Leg 2 correction 11. The Weyerman anecdote is single-source; the "pale spots" are not verifiable as former real wings from images alone. The expulsion-from-Antwerp story, the market crash and the idle end in the flower garden all come from Weyerman 1729 as quoted by Grootenboer (JHNA nn. 27-29); Sphinx repeats the butterfly part. Treat it as anecdote, not established fact. Grootenboer's claim that the glued wings "turned to dust" leaving "pale spots" is consistent with the pale moths in W09/W14, but a pale painted moth and a former glued wing cannot be told apart from a reproduction -- do not state as fact that any specific pale spot in W09 or W14 IS a former real wing.
- Leg 2 correction 12. Rijksmuseum "1670-1708" is a life-span range, not a date. W07 and W08 are dated "1670-1708" on their Rijksmuseum pages. Do not cite either as "c. 1670", "1670s" or any narrower date. The Rijksmuseum pages as rendered also list NO dimensions -- the absence is on the page, not a failure to look.

Leg 3 corrections (C808-C823), as reported:

- C808. D5 "in tow" fails. Kick died 18 June 1681, four years before Broeck's 1685 return (EN Wikipedia Kick, L27); Walscapelle quit painting in 1667 per Houbraken and took up another profession (EN Wikipedia Walscapelle, L13-14), and is a fellow Kick pupil, not a follower. Only Kouwenbergh is a genuine Broeck follower in Amsterdam. The Amsterdam line is Kick to Broeck to Kouwenbergh, with Walscapelle as Kick's other pupil.
- C809. D1 letter: "c. 1649/1652" is a composite no source states. Sources give 1649/50 (SMK title), 1649 (Meisterdrucke/Sphinx text; #557 F7), c. 1650 (NGA, Christie's, Boijmans, askART), 1650/1 (Valls), c. 1652 (EN Wikipedia). Birthplace also splits: Antwerp (Houbraken, Sphinx, askART, Valls, Meisterdrucke) vs Amsterdam (Wikipedia "probably", Boijmans "about 1650"). The study should state the range and the split, not the composite.
- C810. D2 Antwerp year: 1673 (Broeck article: accompanied de Heem to Antwerp, Guild master 1673) vs 1672 (Sphinx/askART: "followed him when he returned to Antwerp in 1672"). De Heem article: he left Utrecht in 1671 when French troops approached; return date unknown. The 1673 mastership is firm; the accompaniment year is 1672 or 1673. Flag, do not pick one silently.
- C811. D3 letter: "carried the pronk back" overstates. Willem Kalf was painting pronk still lifes in Amsterdam from the 1650s, and Willem van Aelst's diagonal composition -- already in Amsterdam -- influenced Broeck. Broeck brought his own de Heem-derived banquet manner to Amsterdam in 1685; he did not introduce the pronk form to the city.
- C812. D7 confirmed. Houbraken Translated, vol. 3, pp. 370-379 (RKD Studies) gives verbatim in the Broeck biography: "He lived outside the Utrecht gate of Amsterdam on the Molenpad, where he had a flower court next to his house for his use and died in the year 1711", and "ELIAS van den BROECK, born in Antwerp, was one of his first and best students". The 1708 burial stands (EN Wikipedia, RKD). The same pages give the Molenpad flower court -- the garden is Houbraken-sourced, not dealer-copy.
- C813. Kick article internal chronology fault. The father-in-law's garden land was "claimed by the expansion of the city in 1657-1663", yet the article says Kick painted flowers from nature there "with his student Jacob van Walscapelle", who was his pupil only 1664-1667. The garden anecdote's dates do not cohere; use Houbraken's account with the caveat, or the Diemermeer move.
- C814. NGA hedges Kouwenbergh's pupillage: "may have been Van Kouwenbergh's teacher" (NGA, read end to end) against RKD/EN Wikipedia's flat "was a pupil of Elias van den Broeck". The study should cite RKD and note the NGA hedge. NGA also gives Broeck "c. 1650-1708" -- a fourth birth variant (see C809).
- C815. Valls catalogue outlier: "From 1700 he was in London" for Broeck conflicts with the Amsterdam residence, the Kuiperspad baptisms from 1689 and the 1708 Amsterdam burial; dealer-catalogue claim, not used.
- C816. Walscapelle death date: the #561 block gives "16 August 1727"; the EN Wikipedia article read end to end gives years only (1644-1727). The day-month rests on the RKD, not on the article read; keep the RKD attribution in the block.
- C817. SMK poster text garble: the extracted poster text reads "yellow fox roses" for the orpiment signature flower; the poster's own table says "Yellow flowers: Realgar/orpiment". Verify the flower name against the poster PDF before quoting it in the study; quote the table, not the garbled sentence.
- C818. Mention-count increments explained: the #561 log entry (lines 5206-5212) re-carries the whole block in its Future candidates line (5212); every carried name gained mentions there (Joris van Son +2, Jan Mortel +3, Jan de Heem +2, all in line 5212 alone). Future legs must not mistake carry mentions for new candidacies.
- C819. Micheux HELD. "Michel Nicolas Micheux" occurs only as one RKD-derived line in the Walscapelle article ("He influenced the painter Michel Nicolas Micheux"); no dates, no works, no standalone article found in this run (S6). Do not propose until a leg finds dates.
- C820. Floquet HELD. Bartholomeus Floquet is a dealer, not a maker (10 October 1674 deed binding Broeck to work a year for him); no dates established; S9 returned nothing on him personally. Do not propose.
- C821. Broeck's only documented pupil is Kouwenbergh (Broeck article, RKD). No other pupil is named in any source read this run; do not present Walscapelle as Broeck's pupil -- he is Kick's pupil and Broeck's fellow-pupil (Kick's shop, 1665-1667 overlap).
- C822. Stuven-Broeck link is a Houbraken trap. The #557 extra-spare entry calls Stuven "van den Broeck's teacher", tracing to the EN Wikipedia Stuven article's reading of Houbraken ("Houbraken mentions in his biography of Elias van den Broeck that he had been Stuven's first and best pupil (c. 1665)"). That reading is chronologically impossible: Stuven was born c. 1657, Broeck c. 1652, and Broeck was documented as Kick's pupil from 27 August 1665 -- an 8-year-old Stuven cannot have taught a 13-year-old Broeck in 1665. RKD lists Stuven's pupils as Herman van der Mijn, J.W. Windtraken and Willem Grasdorp -- not Broeck -- and Stuven's own teachers as Hainz, Voorhout, van Aelst and Mignon. Do not repeat "Stuven taught Broeck" in any study; the Houbraken "his" is ambiguous (the Broeck entry follows Stuven's on pp. 370-379, but the same pages carry a third biography ending in a 1705 death, so layout alone does not settle the antecedent). If Stuven is ever studied, this line needs its own leg.
- C823. Kick-de Heem pupillage unverified. The #561 Kick entry says "also trained by / in the manner of Jan Davidsz. de Heem"; the Kick article read end to end says he was trained from c. 1650 under his father Simon Kick, and gives de Heem influence only stylistically. No source read this run documents Kick as de Heem's pupil. Keep the hedge ("in the manner of") or verify before building on a pupillage.

Parent corrections this build (C824-C833; leg 1 used C792-C807, leg 3 used C808-C823; C805 unused):

- C824. Doorway reconciliation: legs 1 and 3 agree on all seven D verdicts -- substance holds on D1, D2, D3, D4, D6, D7 and fails on D5; letter holds on D2, D4, D6, D7 and fails on D1, D3, D5. The parent follows both legs on every claim. The check is therefore NON-HOLD in substance and NON-HOLD in the letter -- the fifty-seventh non-hold; the substance-hold count stays at 133.
- C825. Doorway tally arithmetic (leg 1 C757, leg 3 C773, continuation of C729/C788): 133 substance-holds + 57 letter non-holds = 190 against 183 checks run, a gap of 7, unchanged from #561. The gap persists because a substance-hold and a letter non-hold are counted on separate tallies, which is the series' own method since #556. Flagged, not fixed.
- C826. Carried-block adjustments: the #561 new block in this study's Future-candidates section was adjusted from 27 to 27 names (Broeck removed, now the subject; Kick promoted from Alternate to Main/top pick, his Alternate entry removed; Simon Kick added as new Alternate). No deeper block changes. The two "Broeck" strings in the carried region are false positives (Pieter Schoubroeck in the #554 block; Chrispijn van den Broeck in the #526 block), neither is the subject. The 6+7 still-carried names and all deeper blocks are carried verbatim from the #561 file.
- C827. Stuven: the #557 extra-spare entry's "van den Broeck's teacher" is a Houbraken trap (leg 3 C822); do not repeat. The carried pool in this file lists Stuven only as a bare name in the extra spares (Still carried paragraph); the teacher claim lives in the #558 file's #557 block, and this correction strikes it there for any future leg that carries that block.
- C828. Leg 2's twelve assembler corrections, all applied in the build: (1) W02 not a flower piece; (2) W20 black-and-white only; (3) W14 attributed; (4) B08 duplicates W10; (5) W06 not a flower piece, JHNA Antwerp/c. 1695 conflict; (6) 1657 birth year impossible; (7) W15 no visible ants; (8) W17 framed-wall photograph; (9) no Commons category; (10) W20/B10 may be the same painting; (11) Weyerman single-source, pale spots not verifiable as former wings; (12) Rijksmuseum 1670-1708 is a life-span range, no dimensions on page.
- C829. Houbraken pp. 370-379: read end to end by leg 1 (226 lines); leg 3 used search snippets for the same pages. The verbatim "died in the year 1711" and the Molenpad flower court rest on leg 1's read; there is no conflict between the legs.
- C830. SMK poster: read end to end by legs 1 and 3 (250 lines each); leg 2 knew it only via snippets. The study's dead-colouring/orpiment claims rest on legs 1 and 3's reads. C817 caveat applied: the study quotes the palette table ("Yellow flowers: Realgar/orpiment"), not the garbled "yellow fox roses" sentence.
- C831. Only securely dated work is the 1676 picture (Valls, via Christie's essay, leg 1 C800); W20/B10 c. 1686, W06 c. 1689, W09 ca. 1695, B01/B14 c. 1700 are dealer/repro-listing dates, reported not verified. The study presents four coexisting modes, not a dated progression.
- C832. D2 Antwerp year: 1672 (Sphinx/askART) vs 1673 (Broeck article/RKD); the 1673 Guild mastership is firm; the accompaniment year is 1672 or 1673 (leg 1 C806; leg 3 C810).
- C833. SMK five dimensions stay REPORTED, not VERIFIED: the poster is a conference paper, not a museum/collection page under the #533 definition, even though legs 1 and 3 read it end to end. The Rijksmuseum pages for W07/W08 list no dimensions as rendered; the absence is on the page.

**Depth (this study).** Three research legs, no parent searches and no parent page read end to end. Leg 1 (biography/technique): 10 searches, 2,507 lines; 6 pages read end to end, 648 lines (EN Wikipedia Elias van den Broeck 50; Monaghan and Vila SMK technical poster 250; Sphinx Fine Art artist page 14; Christie's forest-floor lot essay 35; Houbraken Translated vol. 3 pp. 370-379 226; NL Wikipedia Elias van den Broeck 73). Leg 2 (works/evolution): 9 searches, 2,185 lines; 7 pages read end to end, 1,049 lines (EN Wikipedia Elias van den Broeck 50; JHNA Grootenboer article 693; Sphinx Fine Art artist page 14; Fitzwilliam Museum Vase of Flowers PD.19-1966 74; Rijksmuseum Still Life with Roses SK-A-1463 20; Rijksmuseum Still Life with Passionflowers SK-A-788 92; Sphinx Fine Art forest-floor artwork page 106); 29 Commons files downloaded sequentially, byte-verified JPEG, with .url.txt sidecars and MANIFEST.txt in ~/workspace/fly-watch/562-broeck/; 29 distinct works seen as images (W1-W20 and B01-B09), 6 text-only (B10-B15). Leg 3 (network/candidates): 11 searches, 2,786 lines; 7 pages read end to end, 566 lines (EN Wikipedia Elias van den Broeck 50; Monaghan and Vila SMK technical poster 250; EN Wikipedia Cornelis Kick 47; EN Wikipedia Jacob van Walscapelle 27; EN Wikipedia Jan Davidsz. de Heem 148; EN Wikipedia Philip van Kouwenbergh 18; NGA Flowers in a Vase c. 1700 26); 27 muse-slug 404 checks on public raw URLs; whole-log greps for 27 names plus the subject. Total article lines counted: 2,263 (leg 1: 648; leg 2: 1,049; leg 3: 566), with the overlap stated plainly (EN Wikipedia Elias van den Broeck read end to end by all three legs, 50 lines each; the SMK poster read end to end by legs 1 and 3, 250 lines each; the Sphinx artist page read end to end by legs 1 and 2, 14 lines each). Search lines counted: 7,478 (leg 1: 2,507; leg 2: 2,185; leg 3: 2,786). Works touched: 20 main (W1-W20) plus Beyond the 20 (B01-B15) = 35. Dimensions: 1 of 20 VERIFIED under the #533 definition (W12), 6 reported (W01-W05, W09), 13 not verified. Not accessed: Grove Art Online (paywalled); RKD never opened (leg 1: cookie wall only; legs 2 and 3: not opened); Segal and Alen 2020 pp. 447-450 (cited by EN Wikipedia, not read); Meijer (cited by Sotheby's and Christie's, not read); Weyerman 1729 (via Sphinx, Christie's and JHNA, not read in the original); Houbraken's De Groote Schouburgh vol. 3 in Dutch (read in the RKD Studies English translation by leg 1; leg 3 used snippets only); SMK collection pages for the five works (not opened; dimensions via the poster); Ashmolean and Boijmans collection pages and Fitzwilliam PD.45-1966 and PD.39-1975 pages (not read end to end); no Dutch-language sources read end to end beyond NL Wikipedia; no work seen in person by any leg.
